Sygl

Søren Stromberg


CHAPTER ONE

The Roof and the Flicker

The roof of the Saint Andre’s orphanage was the one place Balthazaar could go when he needed to be away from everyone. None of the other children ever came up. The roof was off limits, and had been since a boy fell from it a few years back and died.

It had never stopped Balthazaar. The staff brought the story out whenever someone was caught loitering near the attic hatch, and most of the children took it to heart. He had heard it as many times as anyone. He kept well back from the edges, he watched where he put his feet, and he figured an afternoon of quiet was worth a scolding if he ever got caught.

It was fairly flat up there, laid with gray slate tiles in rows so even that some long-dead roofer must have measured every one. By early afternoon the tiles were warm through the seat of his pants, and the rug from beside his bed sat rolled up next to him. From up here the noise of the building fell away. There was only the wind, and a pair of pigeons squabbling on the north chimney.

The orphanage itself was old. It had been built sometime in the 1700s, but it was in decent shape for a building that age. It was three stories of weathered brick with a roof that only leaked over the laundry. A long gravel lane ran out toward the road, and no one ever seemed to come down it.

Behind the orphanage, past the vegetable beds and a stretch of rough lawn gone to clover, stood the mausoleum.

That was what everyone called it, though nobody could say for certain what it was. It was a large stone building, bigger than the chapel in the village, with a peaked roof and a band of carvings running all around beneath the eaves. Its front doors were tall and golden and, by every account, impossible to open. Every child who had ever lived at Saint Andre’s had tried at least once. One summer a gang of the older boys had spent an entire Saturday at them with a crowbar and come away with nothing but blisters and a lecture. The building’s secrets stayed exactly where they were.

Balthazaar idly rubbed the stump of his left wrist as he looked across at the mausoleum roof.

It itched. For some reason the stump always itched when he thought about getting inside that building, which was odd enough that he’d stopped mentioning it to anyone. The skin at the end of his arm was pale and long healed, and most days he forgot about it entirely. Then he’d catch sight of those carvings and start wondering, and the itch would come back.

Balthazaar Norheart, or Bal to his friends, was eighteen and could not remember ever having a left hand. He had come to Saint Andre’s as a baby, and the stump had come with him. Growing up, he’d watched the other children get their sygls. A sygl appeared in the palm sometime in childhood, often overnight, and whoever woke up with one spent the next week holding it out for everyone to admire. Green spirals, blue ripples. Kids compared them at breakfast and argued over whose glowed brightest after dark. Bal had watched all of it, and even when he was small he’d understood that he would never have one of his own. A sygl came up in the left palm, and he didn’t have a left palm.

That didn’t mean he couldn’t do magic. He could. It was not as strong as it would have been with a sygl, but it was there, and it was his. It only meant he had to work harder than everyone else. So at least he had that. There were others in the building who could do no magic at all, not so much as a spark, and they got on with their lives the same as anybody.

From what he’d read, the sygl was a sort of guide. It gave a person an affinity, pointing them toward the magic they were best suited to and making that magic easier to reach. Someone with a waves sygl would find water spells came to them without a fight. Someone with a terra sygl would feel the ground answer when they called. There were even factions out in the world that would not let you through the door unless you held their sygl.

Bal didn’t worry about that. Factions sounded like a great many rules and a great many people telling you what to do, and he had always wanted to find his own way of doing things.

Which was why, on this particular afternoon, he was on the roof of the orphanage about to attempt something that could kill him.

* * *

It had started a few days earlier.

He’d come up after lunch with half an apple in his pocket, meaning to take a closer look at the carvings around the top of the mausoleum. From up there you could see them far better than from the lawn, and he’d been working his way around them a section at a time for weeks. They were mostly vines and leaves, with shapes tucked among them that might have been birds or might have been bats, and here and there a symbol he couldn’t match to anything in any book he owned. He’d been squinting at one of those, chewing, trying to decide whether it was a cup or a crescent moon, when the mausoleum roof flickered.

There was no better word for it. One second the slate was there, solid and gray and mossy at the edges. The next it was gone, the way a picture drops out on a bad television signal, and for the space of a heartbeat Bal could see straight down inside. A floor of pale stone. A row of tall pillars. The orange glow of a fire. At the far end, a large statue standing in the center of the room.

A blink later the roof was back, plain and solid, and nothing had happened at all.

Bal had sat there with the apple halfway to his mouth until it went brown.

He’d come back up every day since. The roof hadn’t flickered again, though he’d watched it until his eyes watered. But he knew what he’d seen. Pillars, fire, a statue. That was a temple. There was a temple inside that building, with fires burning in it, and not one person in the building behind him had the faintest idea.

Getting into it was the problem. The doors were out; generations of children with crowbars had proven that. Which left the roof. He’d thought about a ladder first, but the orphanage owned exactly one, it lived in the garden shed, and it barely reached the kitchen gutters. A ladder tall enough to reach the top of the mausoleum would have to be borrowed from somewhere, and carried down the lane, and explained. Jumping across was out too. He had measured the gap with his eyes about a hundred times, and every time the answer came out the same, which was that he would land among the cabbages in several pieces and become the second story the staff told about the roof.

So that left magic.

He’d gone through a fair number of ideas before settling on one. A rope charmed to hold itself straight across the gap had sounded clever until he pictured inching along it. Making himself lighter had sounded clever until he read the part about how hard it was to steer. In the end the safest idea he could come up with was to enchant a small carpet to levitate and carry him over. A carpet had edges you could hold. A carpet could be sat on. If something went wrong partway across, at least he would have something to grab.

A month ago he wouldn’t have had the spell. Then Fredrick left.

Fredrick had been at Saint Andre’s nearly as long as Bal had. He was a year older, tall and all elbows, with a terra sygl on his left palm. The two of them had shared a bench at lessons and more than a few nights of whispering across the dark of the dormitory about what they’d do when they were grown. Earlier in the year, the letter had come from one of the Sygldry schools of magykal arts, and the whole orphanage had turned out on the front steps to see Fredrick off.

The night before he went, he’d come up to the dormitory with a stack of beginner spell books tied together with garden string and dropped them on the end of Bal’s bed.

“I won’t need these where I’m going,” he’d said. “They’ll give me new ones. You take them.”

Bal had looked at the stack, then up at him. “Fred, you could sell these.”

“I could. I’m not going to.” Fredrick had sat down on the end of the bed, making the springs complain. “You’re better at this than half the kids who’ll be in my year, and you know it. They should go to somebody who’ll use them properly.”

Since Bal did not have a sygl, he would not be able to attend the school. No school would take him without one, no matter how much magic he could push through a stump. Self-study was the only option he had, and Fredrick knew it as well as he did. Neither of them had said so. Fredrick had ruffled his hair, told him not to blow up the laundry, and left in the morning with his trunk and a wave through the rear window of the car. It was the kindest thing anyone had done for Bal in years. The garden string was still on the end of his bed.

The books were old but a good lot. There was a primer on charms with half its pages dog-eared and a battered guide to simple wards. At the bottom of the stack, its spine cracked white from use, was a book called *Sygldry Year 2*.

That was where he’d found the spell he needed.

It sat on page eighty-one, in a chapter on moving inanimate objects, between a charm for sliding a heavy trunk upstairs and one for sweeping a floor without touching the broom. *Levimotorcorpus*. Lifting and carrying, the book said, for objects of moderate size, to be practiced first on small items and only by students who had mastered the first-year levitation charms. Bal had mastered the first-year levitation charms in the spring, alone, in the boiler room, with a stack of tin cups. He’d decided that counted.

He had practiced for three nights straight. First on a sock, which shot up and smacked against the dormitory ceiling and had to be fetched down with a broom handle. Then on a pillow, which behaved. Then on a book, which wobbled, and on a chair, which wobbled worse, and finally on the rug from beside his own bed, a small square of patterned wool that had been there longer than he had. On the third night it had lifted a hand’s width off the floorboards and hung there, steady as a table, until he let it down. He’d lain awake afterward staring at the ceiling, the end of his stump still tingling.

* * *

Now Bal was standing on the roof, the rug spread flat in front of him, about to attempt something crazy. The rug had never carried anything heavier than itself.

He checked the gap one more time. Then he checked the sky, which was clear, and the lawn below, which was empty, and the attic hatch behind him, which was shut. There was nobody to stop him. That also meant there was nobody to see him fall, which was less comforting than he’d hoped.

With a deep breath he knelt down and placed his left wrist on the carpet.

The wool was sun-hot and rough against the skin of the stump. He closed his eyes, found the spell in his memory, and reached for his magic. It was always there when he went looking for it, a low current somewhere under his ribs. The trouble was always getting it out. When he tried to push it down that arm, it ran easily enough to the elbow, then thickened and slowed until, somewhere around the end of his wrist, it hit what he had come to think of as a blockage. He had to lean his whole will against it before anything came through.

He leaned.

“Levimotorcorpus,” he said, and pushed.

He could have used his right hand. It would have been easier; magic left his good hand with far less fuss, even without a sygl to guide it. But he had always hoped that if he worked his magic through the stump often enough and hard enough, a sygl might one day appear there, stump or no stump. It was silly, and he knew it was silly. It was still something to hope for.

The blockage gave. Power flowed out of the end of his arm and down into the wool, and the gold and sky blue carpet rose gently into the air, bearing him upward a few inches before it stopped and hung there, as steady as it had been on the dormitory floor.

Bal breathed out. Then he laughed, a short surprised sound that the wind carried off over the gutters.

With a grin he grabbed his backpack from the roof and slipped his arms through the straps. He’d packed it the night before: a flashlight, a bottle of water, two cheese sandwiches wrapped in a napkin, and *Sygldry Year 2*, in case he needed to look anything up on the trip home. He settled himself cross-legged on the carpet, gripped the front edge with his fingers, and tucked the stump firmly against his knee.

He looked across at the mausoleum and silently willed the carpet to move.

It moved.

The trip was brief, because the floating carpet went much faster than Bal had expected. One second he was hovering over the orphanage roof, and the next the edge of the slates was sliding away beneath him and there was nothing under the carpet at all except a long drop to the vegetable beds. The wind snatched at his hair and flattened his shirt against his chest. His stomach stayed somewhere behind him on the slates. He clamped his hand on the carpet’s edge so tightly his knuckles ached, and he did not look down, and then he did look down, and saw the rows of cabbages and beans laid out far below him as neat as a map, and decided that had been a mistake.

The carved band of the mausoleum came up to meet him. Vines and leaves and bats, close enough now to see the chisel marks. He leaned back without thinking and the carpet slowed obligingly, and he coasted in over the peak of the roof, aiming for the slates just below it where he meant to land.

What Bal didn’t expect was that when he tried to land on the roof, he would find there was no roof.

The carpet sank toward the tiles and passed through them as though they were mist. There was a cool tingle over his skin, gone in an instant, and the daylight turned amber around him, and he was no longer above the mausoleum at all. He was inside it, high in the air above a stone floor, held up by a bedside rug and a second-year spell.

For a few wild seconds he couldn’t think of anything to do except hold on.

Then his wits came back. Lower, he told the carpet. Slowly. It obeyed. He kept giving the same command, lower and lower, and the rug drifted down past the carved tops of tall pillars, past their fluted shafts, down and down until, with a soft bump that rattled his teeth, it landed on the solid stone floor of the temple.

The inside of what he had always thought was a mausoleum was lit by two huge braziers, one on either side of the hall, standing taller than a man on legs of black iron. Their fires burned low and gave off a soft, warm light that pooled gold in the hollows of the stone. There was no smoke anywhere, and no smell of burning wood, only a dry smell of cloves and old resin. The air was still, with the hush of a place where nobody had raised their voice in ages.

At the far end of the hall stood the statue he had glimpsed days before. It was larger than he’d realized, a tall robed figure carved from dark stone, set back where the firelight did not quite reach. He couldn’t make out its face from here.

Bal got to his feet.

“Locos cantis,” he murmured as he stepped off the carpet.

The spell let go. The carpet dropped the last inch to the floor and lay there, an ordinary bedside rug again, looking small and shabby against all that bare floor. He’d have to remember to take it back with him. The matrons would notice it was gone eventually.

He turned slowly on the spot, taking it all in.

The temple reminded him of the old Greek ones he’d seen in his history books, the ones that were mostly ruins now. High stone pillars marched down both sides of the hall in two long rows, plain and grand, their tops lost in shadow. The floor was swept clean, without so much as a dead leaf on it. Above him, where there should have been a ceiling, there was nothing. The roof was open to the sky, to let the sun in. A broad shaft of afternoon light came slanting down through the gap and lay across the floor between the braziers, bright enough that dust motes turned in it.

Bal glanced up into it, squinting, and frowned.

He could see the sun. He could see blue sky, and the edge of a cloud drifting past. But from outside there was a roof there; he’d been looking at it every day for weeks. He’d flown straight through it two minutes ago. He was still wondering how he could possibly be seeing the sun through a roof that was solid on the other side when someone answered the question for him.

“Because it’s magic,” a mischievous voice whispered in his ear.

Bal whirled around at the sound.

There was no one there. The hall stretched away empty behind him, the braziers burning, the rug lying where he’d left it. He turned again, and again, peering between the pillars and into the shadows along the walls, trying to find the source of the voice. Nothing moved. But the words had been close enough that he’d felt them stir the hair beside his ear, and whoever had said them was plainly enjoying themselves.

Bal swallowed, and wished he’d brought a better plan than two cheese sandwiches.

“Hello?” he asked.


CHAPTER TWO

The Queen in the Temple

For a few heartbeats nothing answered him.

His own voice bounced off the pillars and returned thinner than he’d meant it. One of the braziers popped and threw up a spark. High in the shadows above the pillars, something rustled once and was quiet again.

Out of the darkness at the back of the temple, behind the last of the pillars where the firelight gave up, a pale girl stepped forward into the light.

She came unhurried, her footsteps making no sound at all on the stone, and stopped a few paces short of the nearer brazier where the glow could find her. Bal guessed she was about his own age, though she carried herself like someone far older. She cocked her head and studied him with frank, open interest, much as you might study a cat that had turned up on your doorstep in the rain.

“Hello yourself,” she said. “Mind telling me how you got in?”

It was a low voice with a laugh tucked away somewhere inside it. Whether it was the same one that had spoken right beside his ear a minute ago, Bal couldn’t tell, and he wasn’t about to ask.

The girl was wearing a long black dress that fell to the floor and moved around her ankles as she walked. Teardrop rubies hung from her ears, and every time she moved her head they caught the firelight and threw it back in small red sparks. Her hair was midnight black, straight and glossy, and reached to the middle of her back. Her skin was pale as candle wax against all that black. She wasn’t skinny, but she wasn’t fat either; she had what Bal considered exactly the right amount of curves, and the dress made no secret of them.

He had a whole speech somewhere in his head. He was certain of it. He’d planned what he’d say if someone caught him, back on the roof that morning, and it had been a good speech, reasonable and apologetic and only a little bit untrue. None of it would come out.

At a loss for words, which was a first for Bal, he numbly pointed at the ceiling.

The girl followed his finger up to the open roof and the patch of blue sky beyond it. Then her gaze came back down to him, and to the rug lying in a heap on the floor behind him, and she arched a perfectly trimmed eyebrow.

“You flew in?”

He managed a jerky nod.

“Huh.” She sounded delighted. “No one’s tried that one. Oh, they’ve tried the doors. Kicking, prying, chanting. One boy stood out there and asked them nicely to open. I liked him. But nobody’s ever come in through the top.” She clasped her hands behind her back and rocked on her heels. “What gave you the idea?”

That, at least, he could answer, once his tongue came unstuck.

“I live at Saint Andre’s,” he said. “The orphanage, over the wall. I was up on the roof the other day looking at the carvings, and this whole building flickered. Only for a second. I saw the pillars and the fires and the statue, and then it was plain stone again.”

“Really?” She glanced upward, eyebrows raised. “How careless of it.”

“Our ladder’s too short to get up here. And the gap’s too wide to jump.” He was talking faster now and couldn’t seem to stop. “So I found a spell in *Sygldry Year 2* for carrying things, and I practiced on a sock first.”

“And how did the sock do?”

“It hit the dormitory ceiling. Somebody had to fetch it down.”

“Poor sock,” the girl said gravely. She considered the rug in its heap on the floor. “I see you’ve moved up in the world.”

“I honestly thought I was seeing things,” Bal finished. Now that he had started talking, the questions crowded up behind his teeth. “Who are you? How did *you* get in?”

The girl laughed and waved a hand.

The air between them shivered. Stone grated softly on stone, and in a matter of seconds two stone benches stood on the floor between them, facing one another, as solid as if they had been carved there a hundred years ago. They were plain gray, smooth-topped, with lion’s feet at the corners. Bal crouched, curious in spite of himself, and touched one of the stone feet. Every claw had been carved separately, down to the tiny curved tips.

“Have a seat,” she said, “and I’ll explain.”

Bal stared at the benches. He had spent a whole spring getting tin cups to float, and three nights coaxing one rug off the floorboards. She had pulled two pieces of furniture out of the air with a flick of her wrist, without a word. He’d never seen a display of magyk like it, not from the teachers who came to the orphanage, not from anyone.

He walked slowly to the nearer bench and sat down on the edge of it. The stone was cool and entirely real. The pale girl swept her skirt aside and took the other bench, settling in with the ease of someone in her own favorite chair.

“My name is Amelia,” she said, “and this is my temple.”

It was only polite to answer that. “I’m Balthazaar,” he said. “Bal, to my friends.”

“Balthazaar.” She tried the name out slowly, tasting it, and looked pleased with the result. “Now that’s a name. Somebody chose well.”

Bal took in the two rows of pillars, the two braziers, the shaft of sunlight falling through the roof that wasn’t there, and the dark statue with its face in shadow. Now that he was sitting still, he noticed things he’d missed on the way down. The braziers burned low and steady and gave off no smoke at all, and he couldn’t see any wood in them. The floor didn’t have a speck of dirt on it anywhere, though nobody in living memory had been able to get through those doors. Bal wondered, briefly, who did the sweeping.

Then he looked back at Amelia, sitting across from him with her hands folded in her lap.

“So,” Bal asked, “a temple for what?”

Amelia frowned. Every look she’d given him so far had been amused. This one put a little crease between her brows.

“You’ve never heard of me?” she said. “The Queen of the vampires?”

Bal’s mouth dropped open.

He knew the words. Everybody knew the words. Vampires were a story you told younger children in the dormitory after lights out, a flashlight held under your chin. The Queen of the vampires was the one you saved for last, because she was the worst, and because she was supposed to have been real once, ages ago.

“Wait, wait,” Bal stuttered. “You mean the originator of the blood sygl?” His voice came out embarrassingly high. “I thought you were a myth!”

Now it was Amelia’s mouth that dropped open, and this time it was in outrage.

“A *myth*?” She came up off the bench. “They have made me a *myth*?”

Her voice rang off the pillars and came back doubled. The fires in both braziers leapt up, tall and crackling, and threw her shadow huge over the paving and halfway up the far wall. Something in the air around her went tight and cold, and every hair on Bal’s arms stood on end.

Bal scrambled to his feet and began backing away slowly, one careful step at a time, keeping his eyes on her and his hand out in front of him for all the good it would do.

Amelia saw this. He watched her see it. She shut her eyes, pressed her lips together, and took a long breath through her nose, then another, and the tight cold feeling eased out of the air. The braziers sank back to their low, steady glow. When she opened her eyes again, she was a pale girl in a black dress and nothing more.

“I’m sorry,” she said. “I wasn’t expecting that. Although I guess I should have known.” She sat back down on her bench, smoothing her skirt, and gave him a small, rueful smile. “Please. Sit. I won’t bite. Not without asking.”

Bal wasn’t sure that was the comfort she seemed to think it was.

He stayed where he was, halfway between his bench and the rug, unable to decide whether sitting or running was the more sensible choice. The rug was nearer. It had got him in, but he wasn’t at all sure it could get him back out through a roof that let the sun come down.

Amelia didn’t seem to mind. Her attention was on his arm.

“What happened to your sygl?” she asked, pointing at the stump of his arm. “Did the thought of having a myth’s sygl disgust you so much you had to cut off your hand?”

At the mention of his hand, Bal stopped.

He glanced at his stump, then back at her. The itch was there again, under the skin, sharper than he’d ever felt it.

“What do you mean?”

Amelia laughed. It was a high, clear laugh, and it echoed off the stone walls of the temple and came back to them from every side.

“My, how the fates work,” she said, shaking her head in wonder. “You lose your hand for showing my sygl, and you still end up stumbling into my temple. Flying into it, no less.”

Bal made it back to his seat. He didn’t decide to; his legs simply took him there, and he sat down hard on the cool stone.

“You mean,” he said quietly, “I had a blood sygl?”

Amelia inclined her head.

“A good, strong connection, too,” she said. “I can feel it from here. I felt it the moment you dropped through my roof. It’s still in there, you know. Cutting off the hand doesn’t cut out the blood.”

At her words, Balthazaar heard the truth.

He couldn’t have explained how. It was a settling more than a sound, a click deep down in his chest, as something that had been crooked all his life slid into its proper place. The blockage in his wrist. The itch. Someone had bricked up a door in him while he was too small to know there was a door there at all.

He knew what blood sygls were. Everybody did. Blood sygls, while not strictly outlawed, were frowned upon. Badly.

Bal met Amelia’s eyes. She was watching him, waiting, her pale hands still in her lap.

“You don’t know what they say about it?” he asked. “Out there?”

“I’ve been in here,” Amelia said. “Tell me.”

He rubbed the stump against his knee. “Well. If a baby’s born showing a blood sygl, the doctors usually take the hand. Before the mark can settle in.”

“Usually?” Amelia asked.

“Mostly. I don’t think anybody asks the baby. People think it makes you crazy. That’s what the stories say, anyway. Blood magic gets into your head, and you lose it, and you start going after people.”

“Crazy,” Amelia said. She spread her hands, inviting him to look. “Do I look crazy to you?”

Bal looked. Rubies, black dress, one eyebrow up.

“Not right now,” he said honestly.

That got the corner of her mouth to twitch. “Go on.”

“I always figured a lot of it was there to get people riled up against anyone with the mark. But that’s just me. Nobody else I know ever wondered about it.” He hesitated. “The other part is that nobody’s seen you. Not for years. Longer than years. For a long time, people thought the blood originator was Dracula.”

“Dracula,” Amelia repeated. Her voice had gone flat.

There was a picture of him in one of Bal’s history books, a long-faced man in a high collar glaring out of an old engraving. Next to Amelia he looked like somebody’s bad-tempered uncle.

“But then somebody killed him. Which kind of proved he wasn’t.” Bal shifted on the bench. “Everyone felt pretty stupid about that afterward.”

Amelia said nothing. Behind her, the nearer brazier sank low and then flared up again, though there wasn’t a breath of wind. Her hands had stopped moving in her lap.

There was one more piece, and Bal wished there weren’t. He made himself say it anyway.

“Once a year,” he said, “all the originators come to the Heir’s School. It’s the first school, the big one. They come to claim their blood, the new students born under their mark. There’s a place set aside for you there, the same as for the others. You’ve never come. Not once, as long as anyone can remember. After enough years of an empty chair, people stopped believing anyone would ever fill it.”

Once he was done, Amelia exploded.

He meant it literally. One moment she was sitting on the bench across from him, very still and very pale, and the next there was no girl on the bench at all. There was a whirling, shrieking cloud of bats, hundreds of them, their wings beating the air into a gale. They boiled up toward the open roof and wheeled around the pillars and streamed between the braziers so that the fires guttered and flared. Bal threw his arm over his head and ducked. Leathery wings brushed his hair. Tiny claws snagged his sleeve and let go. The noise filled the whole temple, a high furious chittering that set his teeth on edge.

It went on for about a minute. Then the bats poured back together over the bench, and folded in on themselves, and Amelia was sitting there again, not a hair out of place.

Bal lowered his arm cautiously. The hall smelled warm and musty, like the orphanage attic in August, and both fires were still swaying. A single tiny black wisp drifted down past his nose and vanished before it reached the floor. He looked at the place where it had been, and then at her.

“Does that happen often?” he asked.

“Only when I’m angry,” Amelia said through her teeth. “I haven’t been this angry in a long while.”

Her jaw was still set hard, and her fingers had knotted so tightly in the black fabric of her skirt that the knuckles were white. When she spoke, she spoke mostly to herself, in a low, furious mutter.

“They lock me up in here and then act like I don’t exist.” She drew a slow breath and let it out again. Then she turned back to him, and some of the anger left her. “Why haven’t you gone to school? You’ve got the magic for it. I can sense it on you. Even without my magic, you’re still powerful in your own right.”

Bal held up his stump.

“No sygl,” he said simply. “No school will let you in unless you have a sygl.”

He’d said it to himself so many times that the words had worn flat, like a grace said before supper. No use sounding sorry about a door that was shut.

Amelia narrowed her eyes. The firelight caught them as she did, and now Bal saw clearly what color they were. Red, the same red as the rubies at her ears.

Then she grinned.

“If you had a sygl,” she asked, “would you go?”

Bal answered immediately.

“Of course. I’ve always wanted to.”

It needed no thought at all. He’d been answering that question in his head for years, lying awake in the dormitory with *Sygldry Year 2* under his pillow.

“Would you do anything to go?” Amelia asked slowly.

She rose from her bench as she said it and walked toward him, one unhurried step after another, her dress whispering on the flagstones. She never took her eyes from his. Bal found that he couldn’t look away either. The rubies swung gently at her ears. She was close enough now that he could smell her, a faint scent of cold stone and something sweeter underneath it, roses maybe, or wine.

Enough stories got told after lights out for Bal to know that this was the question that came right before the bad part. Nobody in those stories ever asked what *anything* meant until it was far too late.

Bal swallowed hard.

“Uhm,” he said. “Within reason.”

Amelia laughed and sat down beside him, and the moment broke. Her shoulder brushed his. She gave off no warmth at all, though she was right up against him and he should have felt some. She swung her feet beneath the hem of her dress.

“Within reason,” she repeated. “Good. I like a boy with sense.” She turned toward him on the bench, her knee against his, and her voice went serious. “Then hear me, Balthazaar. You shall go to this school. I will bond myself to you as your familiar. Doing so will free me from this place.”

Bal blinked. “Your familiar?”

The familiars in his books were cats and owls, mostly, and one very patient toad. Not one had been a goddess, and not one had done the asking.

“Your familiar,” she said firmly. “That way you and I can show the rest of the world that the Blood Bond is alive and well.” Her eyes glinted. “Let them call me a myth with me standing in the middle of their courtyard. I’d like to see them try.”

Bal pictured it: Amelia walking into the yearly claiming and sitting down in the place they’d kept empty for her all this time, while the other originators watched. He wanted a seat for that.

Then her voice softened, and she laid one cool hand on his knee.

“I will not ask anything of you that you are unwilling to give,” she said. “You have my word on that.”

Bal looked at her hand, and then at her face, and then down at the stump.

It all sounded so wonderful that he didn’t quite trust it. Going to a Sygldry school, as Fredrick had, climbing into a car with a trunk and waving goodbye to everyone on the steps. Learning magic from people who knew what they were doing instead of from a cracked secondhand book by flashlight. All of it was sitting there in front of him, close enough to touch, and there was still one big problem standing in the way.

Bal held up the stump between them.

“How am I going to get your sygl back?” he asked. “I’d need a hand to put it on.”

Amelia smiled. It was a slow smile, and a warm one, and it showed the very tips of two sharp white teeth.

“Are you thirsty?” she asked.


CHAPTER THREE

A New Hand

As it turned out, regrowing a hand was accomplished by drinking the blood of a vampire goddess who was thousands of years old.

Nobody had ever put that in a book. Bal was fairly sure of it. He’d read every book Fredrick had left him, some of them twice, and not one of them had a chapter on it.

Amelia did not make a ceremony of it. She sat beside him on the stone bench, turned her left wrist up between them, and drew one pointed fingernail across the pale skin. The cut welled at once. Her blood was darker than he’d expected, nearly black in the firelight.

“Not too much,” she said. “A few swallows. It’s strong.”

Bal looked at the cut, and at her, and thought about those ghost stories in the dormitory. He thought about the school too, and Fredrick waving goodbye through a car window, and the itch in his stump that had been trying to tell him something for eighteen years. He bent his head and drank.

It did not taste like blood. It tasted like heat. It went down his throat hot and fierce, far too strong for him, and spread out from his stomach in a slow warm wave, through his chest and down both arms. His fingers tingled. His left arm burned.

Amelia drew her arm away. The cut had already closed over, and the pale skin was as smooth as if nothing had ever touched it.

The burning started at the elbow and ran down to the end of the stump, and there it gathered and grew. Bal sat back hard against the bench and gritted his teeth. It hurt. There was no point pretending otherwise; it hurt badly, a deep, pulling ache in the bones. But Bal had always had a high tolerance for pain, and this was something he could sit through. He breathed slowly in through his nose and out through his mouth, and kept his eyes on the stump, because he wanted to see.

The end of the stump tingled, then swelled, and something pale pushed outward: the heel of a palm first, then the knuckles, then five fingers unfurling one after another. Bone and muscle and skin, all of it rising together, filling out, finishing itself. The ache peaked, held for a long, grinding moment, and then drained away all at once, leaving behind a deep, tired warmth.

Amelia had stayed quiet through all of it. Now she leaned in to look, and gave a satisfied little hum.

“There,” she said. “Good as new. Better, in fact.”

She was right about that. On top of the hand itself, his blood magic, once he learned to use it, should be way stronger than it would ever have been if he’d simply grown up with the sygl from the start. Amelia’s blood had gone into the making of this hand. Some of her strength had come with it.

Bal sat there flexing his new left hand.

He made a fist. He opened it. He touched his thumb to each fingertip in turn, the way the others did with their own hands a thousand times a day, never thinking about it. Everything worked. Everything was there. The skin was a shade paler than the rest of him, fresh and unmarked by sun or work, and the fingers were long and a little clumsy, not yet sure what was expected of them. He pressed his palm flat against the cool stone of the bench and felt the grit of it. He laughed, a shaky, unbelieving sound, and pressed it there again.

He turned the hand over, and there in the center of his palm was the sygl.

It was blood red. It looked a little like a drop of water and a little like a flame, curled round on itself, with fine lines branching away from it toward his fingers. It glowed with a soft inner light, faint but unmistakable, brightening a little under his gaze and dimming when he looked away. He tilted his palm toward the braziers. It glowed. He shaded it with his other hand. It glowed brighter.

He had seen other children hold their palms out at breakfast for years. He understood now why they couldn’t stop.

He wished Fredrick were here to see it. He wanted to write to him that very minute, and then realized he couldn’t think of a single way to begin the letter.

“I don’t know how I’ll ever explain this,” Bal confessed, still staring at it. “Something like this doesn’t happen every day.”

Amelia stood up and stretched, rising onto her toes with her arms high over her head and her back arched, the black silk pulling up as she did until it was riding high on her legs. It was very distracting. Bal tried hard not to look, and failed, and was caught at it.

Amelia laughed.

“Time for that later,” she said, and dropped her arms, and the dress fell back to her ankles. She didn’t so much as blush. “Now. As for your hand, I’ll take care of that, and your school too. You leave the explaining to me.” She tilted her head toward the golden doors at the front of the temple. “For now, we’ve got one last thing to do, and then you can take us out of here.”

She came and stood in front of him. Then, without any fuss at all, she knelt on the stone floor between his knees, gathering her skirt beneath her, and looked up at him.

“Do you know the words for the familiar binding?” she asked.

Bal’s heart beat faster as he looked down into her eyes.

He did know the words. They were in the back of the household enchantments book, in a short chapter on familiars that he’d read so often he could have recited it in his sleep. He’d never expected to use them. Nobody made a familiar bond in a boiler room. He nodded.

Slowly, a little unsteadily, he raised the new hand and laid his palm against her forehead, sygl to skin. Her brow was cool beneath it. The sygl warmed as it touched her.

“Caro familiare,” he recited. “I offer you this soul bond.” And then, before he could stop himself, the last part, which was in the book and which had always struck him as a little over the top: “May I be worthy to hold a place in your heart.”

He felt his face go hot as he said it. It had seemed perfectly fine when he was reading it in the dormitory. Said aloud, to a girl at his feet in the firelight, it sounded a lot more like something else.

But Amelia didn’t laugh. At his words she smiled softly instead, and closed her eyes, and answered him.

“Caro maestro,” she said. “I accept your bond. May our blood sing together for a thousand years and more.”

As she finished speaking, Bal noticed that Amelia now had the blood sygl on her forehead, a smaller twin of the one on his palm, glowing faintly red beneath her dark hair. It was just above her eyebrows, no bigger than his thumbnail, as neat and sharp as if someone had painted it there with a fine brush.

The sygl in his palm flared.

He felt it go, a pulse of warmth out of his hand and into her, and then an answering pulse back again, and then something in between the two of them that hadn’t been there a breath before. A thread. A line of warmth running from the center of his chest to somewhere outside it. He knew without looking where she was. The book had said only that a familiar bond would settle, which was a very small word for it.

All at once he felt drained, wrung out from his scalp to his heels. His arm was too heavy to hold up. He let his hand drop, and leaned forward, and rested his forehead against Amelia’s.

She let him. They stayed like that for a few minutes, his forehead on hers, her cool hands resting on his knees, neither of them saying anything. The braziers had sunk to embers. Sunlight lay on the paving beside them. Bal could hear his own breathing, slowing gradually, and the quiet tick of cooling metal, and nothing else at all.

“How do we get out of here?” Bal asked eventually, opening his eyes. Amelia’s were already open, and very close, and looking straight at him.

“Through the front door, of course,” she said, and giggled.

She stood up and brushed off her knees where she had knelt on the stone. Behind her the sunlight had crept onto the statue, and the face it lit was hers. Bal looked from the stone face to the living one, then turned to the tall golden doors across the hall. From the inside they were even grander than from outside, gleaming softly in the sunlight, their surfaces worked all over with the same carved foliage and wings as the band under the temple roof.

“But it’s locked,” Bal said, frowning at them. “Everyone’s tried to open it. Everyone. Some of the big boys even tried a crowbar.”

Amelia waited while he stood, then set off across the hall toward the doors, explaining as she went.

“The door is only locked one way,” she said. “From the outside, it won’t open for anyone. From the inside, it opens for anyone at all. Even if we hadn’t bonded, you could have walked right on out whenever you liked.” She glanced back at him over her shoulder. “Me, on the other hand? I’d still have been stuck in here. The only reason I get to go out now is because I’m your familiar. Where you go, I go. That’s what it means.”

She said the word lightly, with a sidelong look to see how he would take it. Bal didn’t know how to take it. He settled for turning pink, which seemed to satisfy her.

Bal stopped to grab his rug from the floor, and rolled it up, and tucked it under his arm. It was his own bedside rug, and it had just flown him over a wall. Somebody would miss it sooner or later.

They reached the doors. Up close they were huge, three times his height, and the carving was finer than it had looked from across the wall: every leaf had its veins and every feather its barbs. He couldn’t see a handle or a latch anywhere. Amelia didn’t need either. She laid both palms flat against the gold and pushed, and the doors swung open, smooth and silent on hinges that should have rusted solid long ago, and daylight came pouring in over the stone.

She stopped in the doorway.

Bal came up beside her. Before them was the courtyard in front of the temple that he’d never properly noticed from his rooftop, paved in old flagstones with grass growing up between them, and a low wall at the far side gone green with moss, and beyond it the rough lawn and the garden and the back of Saint Andre’s. Nothing special. Somewhere around the side of the building a door banged, and a dog barked twice and went quiet. A warm afternoon, and a bumblebee working its way through the clover at the foot of the steps.

Amelia stood there admiring all of it, her face tipped up to the sun. She followed the bumblebee from one clover head to the next. For all Bal knew, she hadn’t seen one in ages.

“It’s been a long time,” she said softly.

For once, she seemed no older than Bal. She looked like someone who had been cooped up for so long that she could hardly believe she was finally allowed to go outside. Bal didn’t know what to say to that. He wasn’t sure anything needed saying.

He took her hand instead, and smiled at her.

“Come on,” he said. “Let’s go outside.”

Hand in hand, they crossed the threshold together and went down the worn steps into the sun.

Bal had half expected something to stop her in the doorway, a flash or a wall of force or a bell going off somewhere. Nothing did. She simply walked out.

The grass between the flagstones was warm and springy underfoot, and the air smelled of clover and hot brick and, faintly, of whatever the kitchen was burning for supper. Amelia stopped on the bottom step and breathed it in, a long slow breath, then another. Bal waited. Through the new thread in his chest he could feel something coming off her, bright and fizzing.

“It smells of people,” she said. “I’d forgotten how much I liked that.”

“Mostly it smells of cabbage,” Bal said. “The beds are right there.”

“Then I’d forgotten how much I liked cabbage.” She was already walking. “Show me everything.”

Amelia was in no hurry. She ran her free hand along the rough brick of the orphanage as they passed it. She stopped dead to watch a car go by out on the main road, far off at the end of the lane, and followed it with her eyes until it was gone. “Loud,” she said, sounding pleased about it. “And it smells.” She stayed facing the road anyway.

“I live here,” Bal said, nodding up at the building. “My mom died giving birth to me, so they put me here. It’s been my home for as long as I can remember.” He considered for a second. “It’s not a bad place. The food’s terrible, but it’s not a bad place.”

He’d never had to describe Saint Andre’s to anyone before. Everyone he knew already lived in it.

It occurred to him, belatedly, to worry about the car.

“Do you know anything about the modern world?” Bal asked. “Things have changed a lot. There are cars, and phones, and TV, and…” He tried to think how to explain the last few hundred years in one breath, and gave up.

“Yeah,” Amelia said, amused. “I can talk to certain creatures. Bats, ravens and such.” She lifted her chin toward the roof of Saint Andre’s, where a big black raven sat on the gutter watching them with one bright eye. It ruffled its feathers at her and croaked. Amelia made a soft clicking sound back at it, and the raven bobbed its head and hopped farther along the gutter. “They come and talk to me, so I do know things. I know about cars. I know about telephones. I know that the cook here burns the porridge every Tuesday.”

“She does,” Bal said, staring at her. “Every single Tuesday.”

Bal breathed a little easier. He hadn’t been sure whether she’d go into shock on meeting her first car. It was nice to know he wouldn’t have to explain a television to a goddess.

The schools themselves were on a different plane altogether, one without any of the modern technology, and only people who had attended a magykal school were allowed to live there, them or their families. No cars, no phones, no television. Bal couldn’t picture Fredrick getting through a whole term without a radio. Amelia would probably feel more at home there than he would. With luck, they could enroll soon. Amelia had said she would take care of that.

As they rounded the corner of the building, Bal heard kids playing in the front yard.

It was a familiar sound: the crack of a bat and a dozen voices all shouting at once. Apparently, while Bal had been inside the temple with Amelia, the other kids had started a softball game. They’d marked out the bases with an old bucket and a couple of jackets, as they always did, and someone’s sweater, already grass-stained, was serving as home plate. The ball was an old one, gone gray and soft from years of being hit, and it never flew far. A little girl perched on the fence by first base, keeping score with a stick in the dirt, getting most of it wrong and arguing with anyone who said so. One of the older girls was pitching. A small boy with no magic and a very large bat was swinging with all his might and missing every time, and nobody minded, and the whole field was cheering him on.

Bal stopped at the corner, Amelia’s hand still in his, and watched the game.

Amelia leaned against his shoulder. “What are they playing?”

“Softball,” Bal said. “It’s a game. You hit the ball and run.”

“And then what?”

“And then you run some more.”

She considered this with great seriousness. On the field, the small boy finally caught the ball with the end of his bat and sent it trickling about four feet, and took off for first base with his elbows going, while the entire field screamed at him to keep going. Amelia clapped.

Two of the boys in the outfield heard and turned, saw a strange pale girl in a dress down to her ankles, and went right on arguing about whether the runner was out.

Bal flexed his new fingers around hers as the boy reached first base. Any minute now, somebody was going to look over and notice he had two hands, and he was going to have to say something about it.

They watched the game for a few minutes before Bal took Amelia inside.


CHAPTER FOUR

Mrs. Brown

The front door thumped shut behind them, and the shouting from the softball game went thin and far away.

After the bright yard, the front hall of the orphanage was dim and cool, and it took Bal’s eyes a second to catch up. The walls were a dull gray that somebody, years back, must have thought looked respectable, and the floor was laid with white tiles that always seemed to echo underfoot. Bal’s shoes clacked on them now. Amelia stopped in the middle of the floor, stamped once on purpose, and listened to the sound come back to her off the walls with her head cocked.

“Good acoustics,” she said.

“Good for the staff,” Bal said. “You can’t sneak anywhere.”

Most of the other kids were outside, which suited Bal fine. He had to go and see Mrs. Brown, and he would rather not have half the orphanage trailing behind him asking about his hand.

Mrs. Brown was one of the women who helped run the orphanage. She was in her forties, and ever since Bal was small she had worn a light gray skirt and a white blouse, with her graying hair tucked up into a bun. Some kids didn’t like her. If she caught you doing something you weren’t supposed to, you got a severe punishment, and she was very good at catching people. Bal had never held that against her. As he saw it, if what you were doing was worth getting caught for, why get upset when you were? He had weeded his share of cabbages on the strength of that idea, and he still thought it was sound.

But Balthazaar had always known something else about Mrs. Brown, too. If he ever truly needed someone to talk to, or if he had really messed up, he could go to her.

This was one of those times he needed to talk to her. He hadn’t decided yet whether it was also the other kind.

Her office was at the end of the downstairs hallway, past the bulletin board with its curling chore lists. Five wooden chairs stood in a row beside her door. He knocked twice, propped the rolled rug against the end chair, took the one nearest the door, and motioned for Amelia to do the same.

“What are we doing?” Amelia asked, plopping into the next chair.

She looked up and down the hallway with open interest. Even the chore lists got a look.

“I need to talk to someone about my hand,” he said. He held it up for her to see. “Well. That, and me going to school. There’s a new orientation class any day now. We had three boys and two girls leave a few days ago.”

“And you think there’s room for one more?”

“I think,” Bal said, “that’s what we’re here to ask.”

Amelia did not seem worried in the slightest. Bal was worried enough for both of them.

“Come in,” a voice yelled from behind the door.

Bal stood, wiped his palm on his pants, and led Amelia inside.

* * *

The inside of Mrs. Brown’s office showed little in the way of personality. It never had. There were two gray filing cabinets against one wall and a heavy wooden desk in front of the window, and that was nearly all. The only thing in the room that belonged to Mrs. Brown herself was a single picture frame on the corner of her desk, turned toward her chair, showing her husband. He was dead. Bal had known that for years without knowing anything else about it.

Mrs. Brown was writing something. She did not look up when they came in, and Bal knew better than to interrupt, so they waited just inside the closed door. Her pen scratched steadily across the page. Through the window behind her came a faint crack of the bat, then a cheer.

At last Mrs. Brown finished her line, put down her pen, and raised her head.

She froze.

Her eyes went to his left arm first. Everyone’s did. All his life they had found the same thing there. Today they found a hand. Then they went to Amelia, to the red eyes and the small blood-red sygl glowing on her forehead, and back to the hand.

Hoping to forestall a lot of questions he wouldn’t have any answers to, Bal quickly summarized what had happened.

“I saw the roof of the mausoleum flicker the other day,” he said, “and I could see inside, and it isn’t a mausoleum, it’s a temple. So I enchanted my rug and flew over. And the roof isn’t really there, so I went right through it, and she was inside.” He tipped his head at Amelia. “This is Amelia. She’s where blood sygls come from. Their originator. The rest of them locked her in there. It turns out I was born with her sygl. That’s why they took my hand off. And she gave me some of her blood and it grew back. And then we bonded, so she could get out. She’s my familiar now.”

He shut his mouth.

Bal couldn’t tell what shocked Mrs. Brown more, the fact that he had a new hand or the fact that he now had the Vampire Goddess for a familiar. For a good while she kept opening and closing her mouth without any sound coming out, which made Bal think of a goldfish. He very carefully did not say so.

Finally she said, “Well then.”

Then she sat back in her chair.

They stared at each other. Outside, someone shouted that it was a foul, and several people disagreed.

Then Mrs. Brown laughed.

It was a real laugh, sudden and helpless. Bal had heard her laugh before, at Christmas, at the younger ones’ plays. He had not heard her laugh in this office before.

“You just don’t do anything by half measures, do you?” she said.

Bal grinned.

“I can honestly say this isn’t how I pictured my day going.” Then the grin slipped a little. He held out his new hand and showed her the palm, and the sygl glowing red in the center of it. “Is this going to be a problem, though? You know how the schools are about blood sygls.”

Mrs. Brown studied the sygl. Then she picked up her pen again and tapped it on the desk, thinking.

“I know what you mean,” she said slowly, “but they really don’t have a choice, do they? I mean, not if she shows up.” She pointed the pen at Amelia. “Once they call for the blood sygls, you’ll stand. Then they’ll ask for your originator to acknowledge you. Which is Amelia here.” The pen tapped the desk once more. “If she didn’t, you couldn’t enroll. But she will. So you can.”

She dropped the pen on the desk and leaned toward them, her wooden chair creaking with the motion.

“They’re not going to be happy you’re there,” she warned them. “Either of you. The only reason they haven’t barred blood sygls from attending is that they want to be seen as fair.” Her mouth went thin. “That’s probably why the originators locked her away, now that I think about it. It made it seem as though she’d simply abandoned her followers.”

“Mostly,” Amelia said. “That, and the fact they knew they couldn’t win in a straight-up fight with me.”

She said it without any heat at all, the same as she might have mentioned the weather. Then she looked over at Balthazaar, and her face changed.

“But I think it’s time they see what a true blood mage can do,” she said, smiling.

Bal liked the sound of that.

“Can you get me enrolled, then?” he asked Mrs. Brown. “I know I’ll have to leave soon to get there in time. Plus I’ll need the school supplies.”

Mrs. Brown waved that off.

“The school provides supplies for first years,” she said. “You’ll only need to bring whatever personal belongings you want. They have their own uniforms and everything.”

One problem fewer, then.

“Can I ask a question?” Amelia said.

Mrs. Brown sat back and gestured for her to go on.

“Why are you willing to help a blood mage,” Amelia asked, “when most people want them dead, or at the least want nothing to do with them?”

Mrs. Brown sighed. Her eyes drifted to the picture on her desk.

They stayed there. Bal had stood across this desk more times than he could remember, for broken windows and missed chores, and he had never seen her look at that frame before.

“Weak affinities sometimes don’t show up until later in life,” she said. “If at all. My husband’s didn’t show up until he was in his thirties. It was blood.” She did not soften the word. “Once it did, the factory he was working for fired him. They said they didn’t want to be seen as a blood mage supporter. After that he did what work he could find, but it was never enough. He ended up getting shot coming home from work one day.”

She stopped there. Outside the window, the game went on.

“I’ve never been able to find out why,” Mrs. Brown said, “but I’ve got my suspicions.”

That was the whole of it. It had taken her less than a minute to tell. Bal stood there and looked at the man in the frame, a stranger with a narrow face and his collar done up to the top button, smiling at whoever had held the camera, and thought that it must have taken her a great deal longer than a minute to learn to tell it that short.

Then she turned from the picture back to them, and she was Mrs. Brown again, gray skirt and white blouse and the bun, the woman whose office this was.

“So I want to help Bal overcome this hatred, if I can,” she said to Amelia. “His sygl showed the day he was born, so I know his affinity must be strong. Otherwise they wouldn’t have removed his hand that soon.”

Bal’s new hand closed into a fist at his side. He didn’t tell it to. The fingers simply curled in on their own, hard enough that the nails bit into the sygl, and stayed there.

Amelia saw. She reached over and covered his fist with her own hand. Her palm was cool, and she held on.

“Don’t worry,” she told him softly. “They can’t do it again.”

Bal looked into her eyes. They were steady, and none of the teasing was in them now. He believed her. Under her hand his fingers came open one at a time, and she didn’t let go.

“So what do I need to do?” he asked Mrs. Brown. The words came out rougher than he meant them to, and he cleared his throat.

“Nothing, except pack the few things you want to take with you,” Mrs. Brown said. “I need to reach out to the school, so you’ll probably leave here in the morning.” She looked at them both, and at their joined hands, and her eyebrows went up a fraction. “The fact that you have a familiar, not to mention that it’s your own originator, will cause a stir as well. Most students don’t get a familiar until they cover them in class. I’ll let the school know you have one, but I’ll leave the details out.” She glanced down at her letter, which in that office meant the talk was over. “For now, it’s best you run along and pack. I’ll send Pierre for you in the morning.”

* * *

With that dismissal, Bal collected the rug off the chair in the hall and headed up to his room, and Amelia followed him.

The dormitory was upstairs, a long room with the beds in two rows and a narrow locker at the foot of each. With everyone outside it was empty, and it smelled of clean sheets and somebody’s forgotten oranges. Bal’s bed was the third from the window on the left.

The first thing he did was unroll his rug and lay it back on the floorboards beside the bed, where it had always been. He straightened the corner with his foot. It looked like it had never gone anywhere.

Amelia came and stood beside it.

“You’re leaving it?”

“It belongs to the orphanage,” Bal said. “Somebody else can have it next.”

Most of his belongings meant nothing much to him, so it did not take Bal long to pack. He slid his backpack off and emptied it onto the blanket. Out came the flashlight, the water bottle, *Sygldry Year 2*, and both cheese sandwiches squashed flat in their napkin, which he had forgotten all about. Then he knelt in front of his locker. Most of it he left where it was: shirts with the cuffs gone, comics everyone had read, a jar of marbles. A school would not need his marbles.

What he really wanted to take didn’t amount to much. A few books. The compass he’d been given for his birthday, brass and dented, with a needle that still swung true. A pocketknife. It was a good pocketknife and he liked it. And, wrapped in a dish towel at the very back of the locker, his rune engraving kit.

It was a flat wooden case with a brass catch and his first scratchy attempts still inside. He’d bought it with his earnings from working in the kitchen. It had cost him most of a year of peeling potatoes and scrubbing pans after supper, and he did not regret a single pan of it. He wrapped it back up and fitted it into the bottom of the pack where nothing could knock against it.

Behind him the bedsprings creaked. When he turned around, Amelia had swiped one of his spell books off the top of the pile and was lying back on his bed with her ankles crossed and her hair spread across his pillow, reading.

The books themselves were old. Their covers were soft at the corners, and one had a ring on the front from a mug of cocoa. The school was going to provide him with new ones. Bal packed these anyway. Fredrick, the boy who had given them to him, was a friend, and he didn’t want to leave them behind.

“You know,” Amelia commented, turning a page, “most of the stuff they teach first years doesn’t seem that hard.”

Bal rolled his eyes.

“That’s because it’s for first years.” He took *Sygldry Year 2* from the pile and held it up so she could see the cover. “They don’t teach the spell I used on the carpet until second year.”

Amelia peered at him over the top of the book. Only her eyes showed above it, red and laughing.

“Oh, so you’re an overachiever, huh?”

It felt a little odd, being teased like this by the originator of an entire sygl. But she was lying on his bed with her feet on his blanket, making fun of his reading, and Bal had to admit she seemed more like a regular girl than anything out of the stories. A regular girl who could make benches out of thin air. But still.

“I like to read,” Bal said, and shrugged. He packed *Sygldry Year 2* with the others. “Just think, if I hadn’t seen that flicker, or come across that spell, I’d never have found you.”

Amelia lowered the book. For a little while she didn’t say anything. Then she closed it, serious now, set it aside on the blanket, and sat up.

“I should warn you,” she said. “The others aren’t going to like you being at the school. It’s not just the students and the teachers. I’m talking about the other originators.” She drew her knees up. “They can’t interfere with you directly. But other people can.”

Bal buckled the pack shut and came to sit beside her on the edge of the bed. The springs dipped and tipped them a little closer together.

“Why did they lock you away in the first place?” he asked.

Amelia didn’t answer straight away.

“Do you know why blood mages are so dangerous?” she countered.

Bal thought about all the stories told in the dark in this very room, and how little was in any of them once you took out the screaming.

“Not really,” he admitted. “I know that some of their magic has to do with their own blood. But other than that, no.”

“I’m not surprised that’s all you know.” Amelia studied her hands in her lap. “Blood mages are dangerous because we can alter people’s sygls.”

Bal opened his mouth.

“Not without them wanting it,” she went on quickly. “Never that. But it can be done. The other originators were shocked when it came out. It used to be a closely guarded secret, honestly. Then Raizen, the storm originator, had his own high priest kidnap a high priest of mine.” Her voice stayed even, but her fingers had tightened on each other. “The others had started to worry because some of their followers were disappearing from their sight. So Raizen’s high priest captured one of mine and tortured him to find out what was going on.”

“Truthfully, I had other things on my mind at the time,” Amelia said. “I didn’t realize that one of my high priests had gone on a converting crusade. The others wouldn’t believe I had nothing to do with it.” She lifted one shoulder. “To be fair, if I’d known, I’d have been fine with it. The only reason converting bothers the others is that when someone converts, they keep the inherited gifts from their old sygl.”

A dozen questions jostled up at once, and he went with the one that had snagged.

“Inherited gifts?” he repeated.

Amelia grinned at him. The seriousness went out of her face as fast as it had come.

“Don’t worry,” she said. “You’ll find out at the ceremony.”

“That’s not fair. You can’t just…”

“Now,” Amelia said over him, “I’m hungry. Let’s go get something to eat.”

Bal’s eyes widened. He was all too aware of the side of his neck.

Amelia rolled hers.

“*Normal* food. I can survive off it, but I do need blood to really thrive.” She swung her legs off the bed and stood, and was at the door before he had moved. “Come on. I’ve heard all about this cook of yours. It’s time I tasted the evidence.”

Bal got up off the bed. “You’ll be sorry.”

“I’ve been locked in a temple longer than you’d believe,” Amelia said. “I doubt it.”

She went out ahead of him in search of sustenance. Bal looked back once from the doorway. His rug lay straight on the floorboards beside the bed, and at the foot of it sat his backpack, buckled and full and leaning against the locker, waiting for the morning.


CHAPTER FIVE

Verin’s Cottage

Pierre knocked on Bal’s bed frame at six o’clock.

The dormitory windows had gone from black to a thin, watery gray, and all down both rows the other boys were lumps under their blankets. Pierre stood at the foot of the bed in pajama bottoms and a sweater, his hair flattened on one side. He was another of the orphans, and some mornings he helped Mrs. Brown before the rest of the building woke up.

“Get up,” Pierre whispered. “Mrs. Brown wants you.” He yawned hugely halfway through the message and had to start over. “She says grab your things, then stop by the kitchen for a bite to eat, then go to her office. In that order.”

“In that order?”

“She said that part too.” Pierre squinted at Bal’s left arm, lying on top of the blanket. Word of the hand had gone through the whole building by supper. He looked at it as though it might have vanished overnight, then shuffled off down the aisle. “Kitchen, then office,” he said over his shoulder. “Don’t make me come back up.”

Bal stayed put a little longer with his left hand on the blanket. It was still there. He got up.

He dressed without a sound and shouldered the pack he had buckled yesterday. Nobody else was awake yet to say goodbye to.

* * *

Somebody had left a plate of blueberry muffins on the counter under a dish towel. Amelia was already sitting on the counter next to them, eating one in small, thoughtful bites.

“These are much better than supper was,” she said.

“Don’t tell the cook,” Bal said.

“I wouldn’t dream of it.”

The muffin was a little stale and he ate all of it anyway. He was stuffing the last of it into his mouth when he and Amelia pushed open Mrs. Brown’s office door.

Mrs. Brown sat behind her desk. She was not alone.

Sitting in one of the chairs in front of it was an older man in well-worn mage’s robes. They were red, or they had been once. The color had faded to something nearer brick, but the cloth was clean and pressed and the buttons all matched. Seeing them come in, the man rose and offered his hand.

“Hello there,” he said. “You must be Balthazaar and Amelia. My name is Verin, and I’ll be escorting you to the Heir’s School.”

Bal swallowed the muffin in a hurry and shook. Verin had an easy smile and kind blue eyes set in a nest of wrinkles. He looked less like a mage than somebody’s grandfather. He didn’t stare at Bal’s left hand, or at Amelia, though the brand on her forehead glowed plainly through her bangs.

Verin looked across the desk. “Sarah, I’ll go ahead and set the portal up outside. The rest of the children are in their rooms?”

Bal blinked. In eighteen years it had not occurred to him that Mrs. Brown had a first name.

Mrs. Brown thanked him.

“Good.” Verin faced Bal again. “I’ll be waiting out front. Please don’t take too long saying your goodbyes.” He strode out past them, closing the door softly behind him.

Bal swung back to Mrs. Brown.

“I didn’t think to ask yesterday,” he said, “but how am I supposed to explain Amelia until everyone knows who she is? A familiar brand isn’t something most people would expect to see on a person, is it?”

Amelia tapped him on the shoulder.

He turned. She was standing right behind him with her face perfectly innocent, and her forehead was bare. No sygl, no glow, no mark at all.

“Where did it go?” Bal asked.

Amelia laughed at him.

“Familiars can choose whether or not to show the brand,” she said, “unless they’re directly commanded to keep it visible or hidden. I’ll just say I’m enrolling as well, until it comes out who I am.”

“Besides,” said Mrs. Brown, standing up and coming around the desk, “Verin knows not to ask too many questions. He’s been at this for years.”

She stopped in front of Bal. For a second he thought she was going to straighten his collar. Instead she put her arms around him and gave him a quick, hard hug. She smelled of soap and starch and, very faintly, of ink. Bal stood there with his arms halfway up, too surprised to finish the job, and then she had stepped back.

“You,” she told Amelia, “keep him out of trouble.”

“I promise,” Amelia said solemnly.

She had both hands tucked out of sight at the small of her back. As they turned to go, she tipped them toward Bal where nobody else had a view. Two of her fingers were crossed, and she looked very pleased with herself.

* * *

The portal took up the whole main walkway in the front courtyard.

It was twelve feet wide, if Bal had to guess, and eight feet tall, a flat upright oval of air with a faint shimmer around its rim. The gravel lane showed around its edges, ordinary in the early light. Through the middle of it was somewhere else entirely.

On the far side a horse and buggy stood waiting on a dirt track in a dark forest. The trees over there were huge and close together, their trunks black, and the light between them was dim and blue, late-evening light, though on this side the sun was barely up. A lantern hung from a curved metal arm on the front of the buggy, already lit, its soft yellow light falling across the horse’s back.

“We’ll have to go the rest of the way by cart, I’m afraid,” Verin said, ushering them through. Stepping across took a single stride, and then Bal’s shoes were on packed dirt and the air was all pine needles and damp earth. “Portaling doesn’t work in or near the school. Well.” He handed Amelia onto the buggy, then Bal. “Not properly, anyway.”

“What does that mean?” Bal asked, settling onto the bench beside Amelia and wedging his pack between his feet.

Verin climbed up into the driver’s seat with a small grunt and gathered the reins. Behind them the portal folded in on itself with a soft pop, and the orphanage was gone.

“When the school was built,” Verin said, “the originators helped. Each of them laid an enchantment over the place to keep it safe. One of them is a diverting enchantment. If anyone tries to portal straight in, they get shifted off to some other spot on this plane instead.” He clucked to the horse, and the buggy lurched forward. “There’s really no telling where you might end up.”

“Doesn’t that work on anyone?”

“The only exceptions are the originators themselves,” Verin said. “The enchantment doesn’t touch them.”

* * *

The forest was deathly quiet.

Bal listened for birds for the first mile or so and heard none. There was the dull thud of hooves on packed dirt and the creak of the buggy wheels, and that was all. Verin hummed for a while and then stopped. The lantern swung on its arm, pushing its small circle of light along the track, and everyone sank into their own thoughts.

Going to this school had been all Bal had ever dreamed about. Granted, he had never pictured going with Amelia’s sygl on his palm. Even that didn’t seem like such a bad thing now. He’d always been a curious kid, the kind who hunted out secrets, and blood mages were the most secretive mages of all. They had never been allowed into any of the magykal schools, since Amelia had never been there to claim them, so nobody really knew what they could do.

He turned his new hand over in his lap and looked at the faint red glow at its center. He was going to be the one who found out.

Bal glanced over at Amelia. She was gazing out at the dark trees, but he didn’t think she was seeing them. There was a small line between her eyebrows. He figured she was thinking about tomorrow, and about the other four, and what they would do when they saw her.

He reached for her hand.

Amelia started. Bal squeezed her fingers lightly and gave her a small smile.

She smiled back. Then she turned to look out at the forest again, and she didn’t take her hand back.

They rode like that for ten miles or so, her cool hand resting in his warm one on the bench between them, until the trees opened up on the left and there was a cottage.

* * *

It was an old cottage, low and stone-walled, with one lit window. Out front a fire was burning in a pit ringed with stones, spitting sparks at the dark, though nobody was in sight to tend it.

Verin drew the buggy up beside it and climbed down, stiffly, and motioned for them to follow.

“This is my home,” he said.

He began unhooking the horse from the buggy. Freed, it shook itself from nose to tail, walked over to a wooden trough by the corner of the cottage and started to drink.

“Normally I’d try to push on to the school,” Verin said, “but with how late it’s gotten, we’d all better rest and head out in the morning. I’ve got both your school uniforms inside. You’ll need to change into them before we leave tomorrow.” He gestured at his own back, where a student’s sygl would sit. “Once you’re officially claimed, your sygl will appear on your clothes. Don’t worry that it isn’t there yet. There’s stew in the pot inside. Make yourselves comfortable while I see to old Thistle here.”

He went off toward the trough. Thistle lifted a dripping muzzle and blew at him affectionately.

Bal and Amelia went inside.

The cottage was one room, and well kept: a table with a single chair, a narrow bed under a patchwork quilt, a shelf of dishes. In the corner a fire burned in a stone hearth under a large black pot, and the smell hit Bal the moment he stepped inside, savory and peppery. His stomach reminded him that breakfast had been one blueberry muffin, hours ago.

On the floor near the fire lay two bedrolls, side by side. On top of each was a parcel wrapped in brown paper and tied with string. Their uniforms, Bal guessed.

He sat down by the nearer one and started working at the knot. Then he glanced up.

Amelia hadn’t moved from the doorway. She hadn’t taken a single step into the room. She was biting her bottom lip.

Bal frowned. He had seen her laugh and tease and turn into a cloud of bats. Nervous was new.

“Are you okay?” he asked, setting the parcel aside.

Amelia crossed the room and knelt on the bedroll in front of him.

“I am,” she said. “But there’s something I need to ask you.”

Bal waited. In the hearth a log shifted and settled.

“Can I feed from you?” she asked softly. “I haven’t had human blood in a very long time, and I need it to reach my full strength. Without it, I’m worried I won’t be able to stop the others if they try something tomorrow.”

Bal sat back on his heels.

“If it’s something you need, then of course you can,” he said. “You’re my familiar. And hopefully my friend. Why wouldn’t I help you?”

Amelia peeked up at him through her black bangs.

“It doesn’t freak you out?” she asked. “I mean, I am a vampire.”

Bal shrugged.

“Nah,” he said, and grinned at her. “Just makes you more interesting.”

Amelia grinned back at him, shyly, a little crookedly, and then she leaned in and gave him a quick hug. It was over almost before his arms caught up. He hugged her back anyway, a beat late. By then she had let him go and was sitting back on her own bedroll, pulling at the string on her parcel and saying nothing about the hug.

Soon she was talking. She talked while she unwrapped a dark sweater with a blank place between the shoulders and measured it against herself to check the length. She told him how the originators had come up with the idea for the schools, and why they had wanted more than one: so that magic would have room to grow.

It was information Bal would never have gotten anywhere else, so he filed it away. Most of his attention, though, was on her.

Ten minutes ago she had been biting her lip by the door. Now her eyes were shining and the words were tumbling out of her, all because she was going to show him a school she had helped build. Bal had never had a girlfriend at the orphanage. He’d kissed a few girls, but nothing more serious than that. Watching Amelia now, he thought this was what he wanted in a girl. Well, maybe not the vampire part; he hadn’t expected that. But definitely all the rest.

Amelia broke off halfway through a sentence about the library.

“What?” she asked. “What are you looking at?”

“You,” Bal said.

Amelia blushed. Bal hadn’t known she could.

* * *

Verin came in shortly after, smelling of horse and woodsmoke, and found them both sitting on their bedrolls with bowls of stew in their laps. He ladled himself a bowl, sat down in the one chair at his table, and between spoonfuls began to explain what tomorrow would be like.

“I’ll drop you off at the school gates,” he said, “so you can walk in with the other students. In the inner courtyard there’ll be a raised stage. All the first years assemble in front of it. Once everyone’s gathered, the originators will start to arrive on the stage.”

He blew on a spoonful of stew.

“As each student is claimed,” Verin went on, “you’ll walk around the stage and form a line behind your originator. Once everyone’s been claimed, each originator leads their students to their dorms.” He pointed his spoon at them in turn. “This may well be the only time you ever see them in person. So if you’ve any questions for them, that’s when you ask.”

Bal glanced sideways at Amelia. She had her lips pressed tightly together and was staring very hard into her stew, and her shoulders were shaking.

Verin didn’t notice. He was chasing a piece of carrot around his bowl.

“You’ll get your schedule from the Lead Mage in your dorm,” he said, “but all first years take the same classes. It’s only the times that are different. They mix all the sygls together in the classes so you can get to know other mages and make friends. They know that once you leave school, a lot of people tend to stick with their own.” He scraped up the last of the stew and set his empty bowl on the table. “That’s about it. Anything else, you’ll have to figure out on your own.”

He yawned, and stretched until his shoulders cracked.

“Better get some sleep,” he said. “Morning comes early.”
