He threw a couple more logs onto the fire, pulled off his boots, and crawled into the narrow bed under the patchwork quilt. Before long he was snoring softly.

* * *

Bal collected Amelia’s bowl along with his own, picked Verin’s off the table and went outside to wash them in the horse trough. Amelia came after him.

The fire in the yard had burned down to a red glow. Thistle was grazing at the edge of the clearing. He spared them a glance as they came out of the house, then returned to his supper. Bal dipped the first bowl into the trough. The water was cold enough to make his fingers ache.

While he rinsed the bowls, Amelia stood in the middle of the yard with her head tipped all the way back, staring straight up.

Bal looked too. With the fire low, the sky over the clearing was thick with stars, more than he had ever seen from the roof at Saint Andre’s, running together in places into pale smudges like spilled flour across a dark table.

“It’s been so long since I was out here,” Amelia said softly. “I didn’t remember it being this beautiful.”

Bal shook the water off the last bowl.

“How long were you in that place?” he asked.

“Almost three thousand.” She was still looking up. “A long three thousand years.”

There was a wooden bench against the side of the cottage, under the lit window. Bal set the bowls on the edge of the trough to dry and motioned to it, and they both sat down.

“They won’t try to take you away again tomorrow,” Bal said, “will they?”

Amelia shook her head.

“No,” she said. “Honestly, them trapping me in the first place was mostly my own stupidity. That, and the fact that I trusted them.”

“What happened?”

Amelia leaned back against the stone wall of the cottage and let her eyes fall shut.

“They found a really talented stone crafter,” she said. “A high priest of Naisura’s, actually. They had him build that temple for me. A peace offering, they said, because they were sorry for ever thinking I had anything to do with their missing followers.” Her voice was quiet and very even. “Immortals like us can only be trapped inside one of our own places of power. A temple, for instance. I hardly ever go into one, and they knew that. So when Naisura’s high priest built that temple for me, they knew I’d want to go and thank him. And I went.”

An owl called once, somewhere far off in the trees. Neither of them looked toward it.

“What I didn’t know,” Amelia said, “was that they had their other high priests hidden outside. Every one of them gave their lives to shut me in. It took the lives of eight high priests to imprison me for all that time.”

She didn’t open her eyes. A single tear slid out from under her lashes and ran slowly down her cheek, and in the window’s light Bal saw that it was blood.

He wiped it away with his thumb.

It left a faint smear on her cheek, and a smaller one on his thumb. He didn’t wipe his thumb on anything.

“They chose their path,” Bal said. “Now you’re free. I can release you from the bond, if you want. I know being bonded to a mage who hasn’t had a single day of training probably isn’t what you expected.”

Amelia turned her head against the wall and looked at him. She was smiling.

“Thank you for offering,” she said. “That you would offer at all means a lot. More than you know.” She reached up and touched the smear on her cheek with one fingertip. “But I’d like to stay. Who else is going to teach you blood magic?”

And she stuck her tongue out at him.

Bal laughed so loudly that Thistle lifted his head again to see what the fuss was about. Then he remembered what she had asked him inside, by the fire, and the laugh settled into something steadier.

“Okay,” he said. “Well, if you’re my familiar, then it’s my job to look after you. How do you want to feed?”

At the word, Amelia’s eyes brightened in the dark. She took his left hand, the one her blood had grown, in both of hers and drew it up toward her mouth. Then she stopped, his wrist an inch from her lips, and met his eyes over it.

“Are you sure?” she asked.

“I’m sure.”

Slowly, Amelia bit into his wrist.

Bal barely felt it. Her fangs going in were a quick, sharp pressure, there and then gone. After that he felt only the cool of her hands holding his arm steady, and her hair brushing his knuckles, and the small, quiet sound of her drinking.

He listened to it for a bit. Then he started talking.

He told her how excited he was to be headed for school at all. Without a left hand his whole life, he had more or less given up on school. He’d figured he would end up a regular laborer somewhere, lugging things and fixing things, until he could teach himself enough magic to be useful some other way. He kept talking, and Amelia kept drinking, her eyes half closed, until at last she lifted her head.

She licked the two small puncture marks on his wrist, once.

Bal watched, astonished, as the holes drew shut on their own. Within a breath the skin was smooth and whole again, and below it, in his palm, the sygl’s faint red glow went on as before.

Seeing his face, Amelia laughed.

“It wouldn’t have looked good,” she said, “to show up for school with two holes in your wrist.”

“No,” Bal agreed. “I guess it wouldn’t.”

He stood up from the bench and offered her his hand. Amelia took it. Her fingers were warmer than they had been in the buggy, almost as warm as his own.

Behind them the bowls dripped on the edge of the trough, and out in the dark Thistle tore up another mouthful of grass. Tomorrow there would be gates, and a courtyard, and a stage, and four gods standing on it who expected her place to be empty again.


CHAPTER SIX

The Claiming

It was an hour or so after they left the cottage before Bal saw anyone else on the road.

The forest track came out onto a wider road, packed hard and rutted by years of wheels, and there ahead of them was a black carriage with a coachman in a tall hat perched up front. Behind it rattled a buggy much like Verin’s. Soon more were coming in from side tracks, all heading in one direction. A boy in a school sweater hung out of one carriage window with his elbows on the sill until somebody inside hauled him back in by the belt.

Bal sat up straighter. His own sweater still smelled of brown paper.

Verin slowed Thistle to a walk and fell in behind a buggy with three passengers crowded onto its back bench. Two of them were boys around Bal’s age, swapping elbows over some joke. The third was a girl a bit older than either of them. She sat with her back straight and her hands still, and she was the only one of the three not talking.

She had the Tempus sygl on the back of her sweater.

Bal twisted to check his own back. What little showed of it was still blank, exactly as Verin had promised it would be. The girl’s sygl was sharp and angular, and whenever the sun broke through the branches and landed on it, the thing crackled. Tiny threads of light jumped across the wool and vanished. Bal caught it three or four times and couldn’t work out whether the sweater was doing it or the girl was.

Since they were all in the same buggy, he figured the three of them were siblings.

Amelia had her chin propped on her fist and was watching the sparks too.

It wasn’t long before he caught his first view of the school.

At the top of a long rise the trees fell away, and there it was across a wide green valley, the school where every Heir began. It was the oldest of all the schools the originators had made, though no one knew exactly when it was built. From a distance it could have been a castle out of a picture book, an enormous ornate pile of pale stone. Five stone towers stood around it, one for each of the originators, but the main building in the middle was so massive it made the towers look slender.

Five. Bal worked out what that meant and stole a look at Amelia.

Her eyes were on the towers too. Which one was hers, she didn’t say.

As they came down into the valley, the school kept growing. A great castle wall ran right around it, high and thick, with the tops of the towers standing up behind it. The road ran straight to a gate of black iron, and the gate was up. Bal craned his neck as Verin drove them underneath. The bottom of the gate was a row of iron points, each one longer than his arm, hanging over the road.

* * *

The noise hit him the moment they were through.

The courtyard inside was huge, paved in pale flagstones and walled in on every side by the school itself, and it was full of students. A large crowd of them had gathered in front of a raised stone stage at the opposite end, all in the same dark sweaters, all talking at once.

On the stage stood five thrones in a row.

They were carved of marble, white and veined all through with lines of blue, so that from here they looked cool to the touch even in the sun. Each throne had a sygl engraved in gold across the front of it. The gold gleamed. Bal’s eyes went along the row, one sygl after another, until they reached the last one.

The sygl on that one was the shape he had been staring at in his own hand for two days.

Verin pulled the buggy to the side, clear of the carriages still unloading, and they climbed down.

“Head right to the back and don’t wander off,” Verin warned them, leaning down from his seat. “It won’t be long before it begins.”

“Thank you,” Bal said. “For the stew, and everything.”

Verin gave him that easy smile again. “Good luck to the both of you.”

They waved to him as he turned the buggy around. Thistle took the turn without any hurry, and the buggy went back out under the iron points and was lost among the other carriages. Verin was going home, to his cottage and his fire pit and his one chair. He had no idea who had slept on his floor.

The two of them hovered at the back of the crowd.

Up close the noise broke apart into conversations. Everyone already knew which sygl they would be claimed under, and they had started grouping up by it without being told. Near Bal a boy was complaining to nobody in particular that his trunk had come off in the wrong carriage. A girl beside him wasn’t listening. She was standing on her toes to see the stage.

No one came to stand by them. There was no group for them to join.

Bal leaned toward Amelia.

“Why have this ceremony at all,” he asked quietly, “if everyone already knows what sygl they are?”

“Because it keeps the thought that the originators care about you alive,” Amelia said. “You know. That they took the time to choose you, personally.” She looked out over the sea of dark sweaters, and spoke more gently. “Plus, for a lot of these people, this is their first step into the magykal world. Something like this stays with you.”

As she finished, a gong sounded.

It was a deep, round note, and it rose out of the stones under their feet as much as from anywhere. The talking died away in stages, from the front of the crowd to the back, until even the boy with the trunk went quiet.

Bal had a thought he should have had a day ago.

“Can they sense you here?” he asked her in a rush, under his breath.

Amelia grinned and shook her head.

There was a bright flash.

* * *

It was white and total, and for a second Bal was blind. He blinked hard. Colors swam back into the courtyard, the stone, the sweaters, the blue-veined marble.

Every throne but the far right one was now filled.

On the far left sat Aren, the originator of the Duality sygl. Since it was daytime, he appeared as an elderly man with a long white beard, in brown wizard’s robes and sandalwood sandals. Bal knew from his books that after dark Aren would be someone else entirely: Nera, a young blond woman in a shimmering white dress. He tried to picture the change happening at sunset, beard and all, and couldn’t.

Next to Aren sat Kenmei, the Waves originator, a tall, stately woman in flowing blue robes with her brown hair falling over her shoulders.

Naisura was next, the Terra originator. He had the look of a man in his thirties, and he was bare-chested, with a pair of white drawstring pants and no shirt at all. He looked like he had stopped by on his way back from the gym.

Last in the row was Raizen, the originator of the Tempus sygl. He looked middle-aged, in plain orange monk’s clothing, his hands resting together on his knees.

The fifth throne was empty. Nobody up there looked at it. They’d had centuries of practice.

The courtyard was entirely silent. Aren rose from his seat and walked to the edge of the stage, and when he spoke his voice carried easily to the back of the crowd without his having to raise it.

“Welcome to our school,” he said. “As most of you know, this was the school we built first, together, and so we have always been particular about who is chosen to come here. Never forget that out of all the students, it was you who got the chance to come here, rather than to one of the lesser schools. As of now you are officially an Heir, with all the prestige that comes with the title.”

Bal thought of Fredrick, waving from the back window of a car on his way to a lesser school, and decided Fredrick would have laughed at that.

“Now it is time to begin,” Aren went on. “We will start with me. I will call out each of my chosen, and you will line up behind my throne. Then Kenmei, and so on, until all of you are gathered behind us. Once everyone has been chosen, each of us will lead you to your sleeping quarters, where your Lead Mage will give you your class schedule. Any questions you have for us must be asked at that time. Is that understood?”

All across the courtyard, heads bobbed. Aren went back to his throne and sat.

There were about fifty students in the crowd, so it would take a while.

Aren read his names first. Students broke from the crowd as their names were called and climbed the steps at the stage’s edge, and a line began to grow behind his throne. After the first few names Bal stopped listening to them. He leaned in close to Amelia instead.

“You okay?” he whispered.

“Mm-hm.” Amelia kept her eyes on the stage.

“You don’t know how long I’ve waited for this,” she said. Her eyes shone bright in the sunlight.

“About three thousand years, right?” Bal said.

Amelia’s head turned, startled. Then she grinned at him, wide and crooked.

“Well, yeah,” she said. “I guess you do know.” Her gaze returned to the four on their thrones. “I promise you, this will be an initiation no one forgets.”

* * *

The claiming went on and on.

Aren finished, and Kenmei rose and read her list in a low, clear voice, and the Waves students filed past in a steady stream. Naisura read his without standing up at all. The crowd in front of the stage shrank. Gaps opened in it, then whole stretches of bare flagstone where groups had been standing. The sun moved off one throne and onto the next. Bal’s feet began to ache, and then stopped bothering to.

Raizen was the last. He stood to read, and his voice was drier than the others’. When he had been reading for a while, Bal saw a flicker of light at the edge of the thinning crowd. It was the girl from the buggy, heading for Raizen’s line, and the sygl on her back gave off one small crackle as she passed out of the shade. Bal had missed her name. He hadn’t been listening.

Raizen read on. The last few students went up.

Raizen stopped reading and took his seat.

Bal and Amelia were the only two people left standing in front of the stage.

A pigeon was walking on the flagstones, and Bal could hear its feet. Back where all the lines stood, a whisper started up, and then another, as the students there noticed that Raizen had finished and the two of them were still standing there.

Aren rose and came to the edge of the stage again.

“Young man,” he said, and his voice carried across the courtyard. “Come closer. Bring your companion.”

The two of them walked up to the stage. It was a long way with everyone watching. When they stopped at the foot of it, Bal could finally see the originators properly. Aren’s beard was combed. Kenmei’s eyes were a very pale sea green.

Aren turned to the others.

“Are they yours?” he asked them.

One by one, each of them said no.

Aren faced them again with a sad smile.

“I’m sorry, young ones,” he said. “If yours is the blood sygl, then I’m afraid you’ve come so far for nothing. As you can see.” He gestured toward the empty throne, and the gold sygl on its front shone back at him. “Lady Amelia has not come to claim you. And unless you have an originator as your patron, you may not attend this school.”

Bal stepped forward.

“But what if she did claim us?” he asked. He pitched it to carry, loud enough that he was sure the lines at the back would hear every word. “Could we attend then?”

Aren nodded, but his smile was still a sad one. “Of course you could. But alas, Lady Amelia has not been seen in a very long time.”

Bal felt Amelia step up beside him.

She looked at Aren. Then she looked past him at the others, Kenmei, Naisura, Raizen, taking her time about each of them.

She clapped her hands, once.

It wasn’t loud. Half the students at the back probably never heard it. But Aren stumbled backward as if the stage had tipped under him, and caught himself on the arm of his own throne. Kenmei’s hands had locked onto the arms of hers. Naisura sat bolt upright. Raizen’s hands came apart.

Bal turned his head.

She looked exactly the same to him. Black hair, pale skin, the ruby earrings, the red eyes. Nothing about her had changed at all.

“I disguised myself,” Amelia muttered to him out of the side of her mouth. “You could see me as I was. No one else could.”

That made sense, Bal thought. He faced the stage again.

Amelia sprang lightly up onto the edge of it. The originators followed her with their eyes the whole way, warily, none of them moving. She paid them no attention. She walked along behind Aren and Kenmei and Naisura and Raizen, past each of their thrones in turn, to the end of the row, where she turned and took her seat.

She settled into it as if she had last sat there yesterday.

“Balthazaar Norheart,” she said, “I formally claim you. I also name you my High Priest.”

Amelia turned in her seat to face the others. Her eyes narrowed.

“Let the first one harm him at their peril.”

Nobody on the stage said anything.

“There are things that need to be said,” Amelia told them. “But they will wait. You will not return to your domains until they are said.” She paused. “Will you?”

The other originators shook their heads, all four of them, and quickly. Bal saw them do it. Up until then, he hadn’t understood how anybody could be afraid of her. Now he was starting to.

The coldness left her face. She turned to Bal and motioned with two fingers for him to go on around to the back.

* * *

He went up the side steps and around the back of the thrones, and there they all were.

Every first-year in the school was standing back there in four lines, with the space behind the fifth throne left bare. All of them were staring at him. Some looked confused. Some looked awed. A few, here and there, looked downright excited. A boy near the front of the Terra line had his mouth open, and nobody had told him.

Bal went and stood behind Amelia’s throne by himself. It was the shortest line in the courtyard by a wide margin.

Aren clapped his hands. This one was an ordinary clap. Nobody stumbled. Each of the originators stood and descended the side steps, and their lines filed after them into the school in long dark ribbons.

Amelia stood last. She came around her throne and beckoned for Bal to follow her.

She led him in through a small door and down a narrow stone corridor, leaving the noise of the others behind, until it ended at a set of stairs going down. Their footsteps echoed in the narrow space. Bal waited until the courtyard was well behind them.

“Gotta admit,” he said, “the angry vampire Goddess was a good look on you.”

Amelia’s laugh went bouncing down the stairwell ahead of them.

“They just know I could have wreaked havoc if I’d wanted to,” she said. “And they’re glad I didn’t.”

At the bottom of the stairs they came out into another corridor, wider than the first, and this one was lined with paintings. Old ones, mostly, in heavy frames, landscapes and portraits of strangers in clothes nobody had worn for centuries. A woman in one of them was holding a fish. He made a note to come back and find out why.

“Why didn’t you?” Bal asked. “Wreak havoc, I mean.”

Amelia shrugged.

“As mad and hurt as I was when it first happened,” she said, “the anger has died down since then.” She glanced at a portrait as they went by and then away again. “There are other ways to get revenge.”

Bal waited, but she left it there. So he tried something else.

“And the High Priest thing?”

Amelia winced.

“Yeah. I know I probably should have warned you first,” she said. “But honestly, I did it to keep you safe.”

“Keep me safe?” Bal asked.

“Any of the originators can mess with any follower,” Amelia said, “even a regular priest, up to a point. But they are not allowed to interfere with a High Priest. If they do, the offended party can challenge the originator who did it.” She cut her eyes toward him. “They do not want that. I would be only too happy to go toe to toe with any one of them, and they know it. This way, if they try anything, I can back you up all the way. Not only as far as a familiar is allowed to.”

Bal turned that over as they walked. He pictured Aren catching himself on the arm of his throne. Then he nodded.

“Anything special I need to know?” he asked. “About the whole High Priest thing?”

Amelia hesitated. For a few steps she didn’t answer at all, and her eyes traveled on along the corridor, past the paintings to wherever it was they were going.

“We’ll talk more about it tonight,” she said.


CHAPTER SEVEN

The Blood Dorm

The pedestal bit him.

It stood at the end of the corridor of paintings, a squat column of gray stone about as high as his waist, with the blood sygl carved deep into its flat top. Amelia stopped in front of it.

“Put your left hand on it,” she said.

Bal put his palm flat over the carving. There was a sharp little prick in the middle of his hand, like a pin left in a new shirt, and before he could pull back the stone was already drinking. His blood ran out along the grooves of the carving, filling them line by line until the whole sygl stood out red on the gray, the same shape as the one under his hand. Then it soaked into the stone and was gone, and the top of the pedestal was dry and gray again.

Beside them, a section of the wall slid aside with a low grinding sound. Behind it a short flight of stone steps climbed up into warm light.

Bal turned his hand up. There was a single red dot in the center of the sygl. He had barely felt it.

“Every time?” he asked.

“Every time,” Amelia said. “It only opens for blood it knows.”

She went up first. Bal hitched his pack higher on his shoulder. It had been dragging at him since the buggy. He followed her. Behind him the stone slid back into place and the wall was a wall again.

The steps ended in a long, low room with a fire already burning on the right-hand side. In front of the fire stood a couch upholstered in deep blood red, and next to it a wing-backed leather chair, and a coffee table set before them both. Across the room were two doors. Otherwise it was fairly plain, and smaller than he had expected for a whole dormitory.

“Each dorm is enchanted in different ways,” Amelia said, wandering ahead of him to the fire. “One of the enchantments on mine is that there are no set rooms. Say we get another student. Another bedroom appears. Need a dining room? One will appear. If you want it to be permanent, normally you have to ask the Lead Mage.” She threw him a look over her shoulder. “Which is you. Or a High Priest. Which is also you.”

She laughed and dropped onto the red couch, and her hair fanned out over the cushion behind her.

Bal let his pack slide off his shoulder by the end of the couch and sat beside her. The couch was soft enough that he sank farther than he meant to.

“Will we get more students?” he asked.

“Oh, yes,” Amelia said. “Especially once word gets out that I’m back.”

She lay there a little longer with her eyes on the ceiling. Then she sighed and straightened, pulling her legs under her.

“Okay. Official stuff, before I have to go and deal with the family.” She held up one finger. “Do you remember how you got down here?”

Bal ran it back in his head. The little door behind the stage, the narrow corridor, the stairs going down, the paintings. “Yes.”

“Good. I may not always be with you, so you need to know your way around.” Another finger. “You’ve only got four classes as a first year. I’ll get you the list once I’m through dealing with them. There’s a curfew, but as Lead Mage you’re exempt from it. The library’s on the second floor, because I’m sure you’re going to go hunting for it.”

“I was going to ask.”

“I know you were. Make friends with the other Heirs if you can. If you can’t, don’t worry about it. They’ll come around.” She leaned in. “And lastly. You like secrets, right?”

Bal gave her a sideways look. She had asked it much too sweetly. “Yes,” he said, carefully.

Amelia’s smile spread. “Me too. If you figure out any more enchantments in here, or any other secrets in the school, I’ll reward you.”

Bal couldn’t help smiling back. A whole castle full of secrets, and someone to bring them to. “I can’t wait.”

“Me either.” She uncurled from the couch and stood. “Now I really do have to go. If you need me, push your magyk into your sygl, and I’ll be there.”

She hugged him, quick and cool around the neck, and before he had properly hugged her back she straightened, snapped her fingers and was gone. The cushion beside him slowly puffed back up.

The fire popped. Bal stayed in the quiet for a while.

* * *

He spent the next few hours exploring his dormitory.

There was not much of it, at first. The fireplace, the couch, the chair, the coffee table. One of the two doors across the room opened on a bathroom, white tile and a deep tub on clawed feet, and he closed that one again. The other opened on a bedroom, and he froze on the threshold.

It was easily twice the size of the one he’d had at the orphanage. That one had been his along with twenty other boys. This room had a four-poster bed with a canopy, and the bed was so big it looked as though five people could have slept in it without anybody kicking anybody. Across it lay a soft blanket, black, with a single rose embroidered on it in red so dark it was nearly black itself. A chest stood across the end of it, as wide as the bed.

Bal fetched his pack from the main room and lifted the lid of the chest. It was empty and smelled of cedar. He lowered the pack in, and it sat at the bottom looking very small, the whole of what he owned with room to spare all around it. He let the lid down.

He pressed a hand into the blanket. It was as soft as it looked.

Then he went out and closed the bedroom door behind him.

A dorm with no set rooms. Bal planted himself in the center of the main room and thought about what that meant. If a room appeared when you needed it, then what did he need? A library, obviously. That was the first thing. And if he was going to live here, a kitchen. And somewhere to eat whatever got cooked in it.

As he thought it, the wall facing the fire shifted. Three new doors stood in it, side by side, as though they had always stood there and he had only now thought to look.

Bal stared at them. Then he went straight to the first one.

It was a kitchen. There was a wood-burning stove against one wall with a stack of split logs beside it, and two big chests off to one side, and above the chests a row of shelves crowded with little jars of spices. He unstoppered a jar of something brown, sniffed it and sneezed. He opened the chests. One held meat, wrapped in paper and cold to the touch. The other was full of vegetables, carrots still with dirt on them and a sack of onions. The kitchen had a stove in it and food in it, which was what kitchens were supposed to have, and he stood there pleased with it anyway.

The second door was the library.

It was smaller than the room with the couch, with tall bookshelves lining the walls and five chairs drawn up in front of another fireplace. The fire was laid but not lit. The shelves were bare from top to bottom. Bal ran his hand along the nearest. Not even dust.

His shoulders dropped. Then he looked again at the five chairs, and the empty hearth, and the long bare shelves, and pictured them full. It would be a good room to read in, once he had something to put on them. Fredrick’s books would fill about a foot of one shelf. That was a start.

The third door was the dining room. A large mahogany table took up most of it, dark and glossy enough that the ceiling showed in it, with polished wooden chairs set all around. Bal walked the length of the table and back. Passing the salt would take a long arm.

Standing between the two rooms, he had a thought. Why walk your dinner from one door to the next? He pictured the kitchen and the dining room as one room, the stove and the long table together.

The wall between them went soft at the edges and was simply not there anymore. Now there was one large room, warm from the stove at one end, with the mahogany table at the other.

Bal laughed out loud, alone, in his own kitchen.

He returned to the fire, stood in front of it and took in the doors in turn. The bedroom. The bathroom. The library. The big new kitchen. He thought, as plainly as he could, *stay*.

He couldn’t have said what changed. Nothing moved. But when he looked at the new doors afterward they had a settled look, the look of doors that were going to be there tomorrow.

His stomach growled.

* * *

He could have cooked. There was a whole chest of meat in the kitchen. But somewhere in this castle there was a dining hall, and he was curious to find it.

He took the stone steps down, and the wall slid aside to let him out. He retraced his way past the paintings, up the narrow stairs, and along the corridor, and when he got near the main part of the school he heard people talking and laughing.

The corridor let out into a large courtyard in the middle of the school. Students filled it, sitting around in groups on benches and the edges of planters. It didn’t take long to work out how they were sitting. Most of them were with others of their own sygl, Waves with Waves, Terra with Terra. A few of the braver ones had drifted over to chat with somebody from another group.

Bal pulled his sweater around to look at the back. The blood sygl had come up there at some point during the claiming, dark red on the dark wool. Verin had said it would. He hadn’t even noticed.

Some of the students had noticed. A few heads turned as he stepped out of the corridor. A few conversations stopped, then picked up again more quietly.

“Hey, you’re that Blood guy, right?”

The voice came from behind him. Bal turned quickly.

A girl stood there with her hands in her pockets. She was a little taller than he was. Her hair was pale blue, a cold, bright blue like the sky at the edge of a storm, and her eyes were the same color exactly. She wore a small smile.

It was the Tempus girl who had ridden ahead of them on the road that morning. So the crackling had been her hair, after all, and not the sweater. The two boys must only have shared her ride.

While his eyes were on her hair, it did it again. Down near her shoulder a strand stirred on its own, and then the whole of it bristled, and a faint snapping ran through it like someone shaking out a wool blanket in winter. Then it lay flat.

The girl rolled her eyes.

“It’s an inheritance thing,” she said. “After Raizen did our question-and-answer thing, he told us any inheritance traits we had could start showing up today. From what I understand it’s one of the rarest ones, if not the rarest. It’s supposed to give me more control over storms.” She shrugged. “But who knows.”

Her eyes went over his shoulder to the courtyard, to the groups and the half-stopped conversations, and then back at Bal.

“No one rolling out the red carpet for you yet?”

Bal only blinked at her.

“I mean, has nobody tried to talk to you yet?” she said.

“No,” Bal said. “Nobody.”

“Oh. Well.” She took a hand out of her pocket and stuck it out. “I’m Viridia Vizer. Heir of Raizen.”

Bal shook it. Her grip was quick and firm.

“Bal,” he said. He tipped his chin at the courtyard. “They probably don’t know what to think about me. It’s not something that happens every day.”

“Yeah, that makes sense.” Viridia glanced around at the groups herself. “Honestly, though, once people get over the shock, they’ll probably start coming up to you. Just remember not everybody’s going to be friendly. You’ve got a lot of years of hate and stuff to get past.”

“Yeah, I know,” Bal said. “Thanks for the warning, though.” He gave her a closer look. “Aren’t you afraid of what they’ll think, you talking to me?”

Viridia snorted.

“They wouldn’t say anything to me about it if they did. Kids with an inheritance are considered the elite of a class. This year I’m the only one who’s manifested one so far. That could change. Even if it did, I wouldn’t care. The petty politics most of these kids try to play are stupid.”

“So you don’t think Raizen is the greatest thing ever,” Bal said, “like the rest of them?”

Viridia made a face.

“Nah. They’re people just like us. They’ve got faults just like us. They just live longer than the rest of us.”

“That’s not an opinion you hear often,” he said. “Most people don’t criticize them. Not that I’ve heard, anyway.”

“I’m not like most people,” Viridia said.

“I can see that.” Bal let his eyes go up to her hair, pointedly. “The electric blue kind of gives it away.”

Viridia stuck out her tongue.

He was still looking pleased with himself when she grabbed him by the sleeve.

“Come on,” she said, already towing him off between the groups. “Let’s go get lunch.”

* * *

Other students were drifting toward it too, and they followed the stream through a pair of tall doors into the dining hall.

It was a huge room, with high windows down both sides and five long tables running the length of it, each with a bench on either side. A sygl was inlaid in the wood at the head of every table. The Waves students were filling up the table with the Waves sygl, the Terra students the Terra table, and so on down the hall.

The Blood table was the last of the five, nearest the back wall. It was empty.

Bal walked down to it, sat on the bench, and looked up to find Viridia sliding onto the opposite bench.

He raised an eyebrow at her.

“Did you want to eat alone?” Viridia asked.

“Not particularly,” Bal said.

“Okay, then.” She planted her elbows on the table.

On each silver plate lay a piece of paper. Bal read his. It gave instructions for ordering a meal. Write what you want on the back, it said, then lay the paper on your plate. After today any piece of paper would do.

Who washed the plates, if they went away on their own? Was there a room somewhere in the castle full of dirty plates, piling up? Somebody had to do it. Somebody always did.

There was a pencil beside the plate. On the back Bal wrote, in large letters, *LARGE PEPPERONI PIZZA*.

He laid it on his plate. The plate vanished. In its place was a big silver dish, and on the dish a whole pepperoni pizza, steaming, the cheese still bubbling at the edges.

Bal reached for a slice. Across the table Viridia already had a burger in both hands and was taking a large bite out of it.

They sat in silence for a while, both of them busy with their food. The pizza was good. It was so much better than anything the orphanage cook had ever made that Bal ate the first slice too fast and burned the roof of his mouth, and then ate a second one, and a third.

“You nervous about your classes?” he asked, once he’d finished the third.

Viridia had finished her burger. She reached across the table without asking and helped herself to a slice of his pizza, and took a bite before she answered.

“A little, yeah.” She wiped a smear of grease off her chin with a napkin. “I heard you can’t leave the forest until you bond a familiar.”

“Why wouldn’t you be able to bond one?” Bal asked. “As long as you know the incantation, it’s not that hard.”

Viridia lowered the pizza. Her eyes narrowed.

“You’ve already got a familiar,” she said. It came out like an accusation. “They told all of us not to even try it until we had the class. How’d you get one already?”

Bal couldn’t quite keep the smirk off his face. “Mine’s a special case. Honestly, I hope I get to see your face when you meet my familiar.”

“Why wouldn’t you get to?” Viridia took another bite. “What is it, a wolf or something?”

“No, it’s me,” said Amelia.

She was right there next to Bal, elbow to elbow with him. She had not been there a second ago.

Viridia inhaled her bite of pizza and choked on it.

She coughed, and coughed harder, one hand flat on the wood. Bal half rose to go around and pound her on the back, but she waved him off. At last she grabbed her cup and gulped water until she could breathe again. Her eyes were streaming.

“Wait,” she croaked. “What? I don’t understand. Lady Amelia, you’re his familiar? What does that even mean?”

In answer, Amelia lifted her bangs off her forehead with two fingers.

The brand was there, a small blood sygl matching the one in Bal’s hand, glowing a soft red against her pale skin. Viridia stared at it.

“But how is that possible?”

Amelia let her bangs fall. She helped herself to a slice of Bal’s pizza, and explained between bites.

“As an Immortal, and a vampire, I’m technically a magical creature,” she said. “That’s why the bonding was able to work.”

“Yeah, but bonded creatures always want something in return.” Viridia was still staring. “What could he have that you’d want?”

Amelia stopped chewing.

She left the slice on the dish, and when she turned to Viridia her red eyes were perfectly level.

“Don’t ruin the good impression I’ve gotten of you so far,” Amelia said. “Insulting him is the one thing that’s liable to set me off.”

Viridia opened her mouth, and shut it again. Color came up in her cheeks.

“No, no,” she said, both hands up and waving. “I didn’t mean it like that. I wasn’t being disrespectful. I only meant, you’re Immortal. What could a mortal offer you that you couldn’t get for yourself?”

Amelia studied the pizza.

“You’d be surprised,” she murmured.

Bal felt it was about time somebody changed the subject.

“This is our big secret until class,” he told Viridia. “You’ve just gotten a preview. Imagine how the rest of the class is going to react.”

Viridia burst out laughing, and some of the stiffness went out of her shoulders.

“Yeah, that’s going to be hilarious,” she said. “They’re all going to have these lame little familiars, and you’re going to have a beautiful vampire familiar.”

Amelia’s head came up.

“You think I’m beautiful?” she asked.

“Of course,” Viridia said, perfectly straight-faced. “I’d die to have skin like yours.”

Amelia only studied her. Viridia looked back and didn’t blink. A Tempus boy at the next table over had stopped with his fork in the air and had his eyes on them, and nobody at the Blood table paid him any attention.

Amelia’s mouth twitched first. Then she put her head back and laughed, loud enough that half the hall turned around.

“Okay,” she told Bal. “You can keep her.”

She swallowed the last bite of her pizza and slid out from the bench.

“I’ll be in the dorm when you’re done hanging out, and we can go over the stuff we didn’t get to earlier. Have fun, you two!”

There was a soft pop, and Amelia’s place on the bench was empty.

Bal and Viridia traded a look across the pizza.

“What did she mean,” Viridia asked, “you can keep me?”

Bal shrugged.

“Honestly?” he said. “I have no idea.”

* * *

They stayed and talked a while longer, until the hall had mostly emptied around them and the silver dish held only crusts. Then Viridia climbed off the bench and stood.

“I should get back to my dorm,” she said. She waved goodbye from the end of the table and went off down the hall and out through the tall doors.

Bal sat on at the empty table. Then he headed back to his dorm.

There were still students milling around in the courtyard, and more of them in the corridors, standing in their groups. Bal walked through them with his hands in his pockets. A boy in a Terra sweater looked at the sygl on his back as he passed, then turned and kept studying his face. Farther on, an older boy with his arms folded tracked him the whole length of the corridor with a flat, unfriendly stare.

No one said anything. No one bothered him.

He went down the narrow stairs, and the noise of the school fell away behind him. The paintings went by in their heavy frames. At the end of the corridor the pedestal stood waiting, the blood sygl carved into its top.

Amelia was behind that wall. So was whatever she had put off telling him about the High Priest thing.

Bal pressed his hand to the stone and felt it prick.


CHAPTER EIGHT

Too Bad She’s a Tempus

The wall opened, and at the top of the stone steps Amelia had her feet up on the coffee table.

She was slouched so far down on the red couch that her shoulders were nearly level with the cushions, her ankles crossed on the polished wood and her dress fallen back around her shins. She looked as though she had been there all afternoon and meant to stay until spring. When she heard the stone grinding shut behind him, she rolled her head along the back of the couch to find him, and smiled, and waved.

Bal went over and dropped down beside her. Somebody had built the fire up while he was out. It was throwing heat across the floor and putting a shine on the leather of the empty chair.

“How’s your first day?” Amelia asked.

Bal leaned back. The couch took him in up to the ribs.

“Not bad,” he said. “I’m glad I’ve made a friend already. Viridia seems cool.”

“I like her too.” Amelia flexed her bare toes against the tabletop. “She’d do well in the Blood sygl.”

Bal shut his eyes. The fire was warm on the left side of his face and the couch was warm everywhere else, and he had been awake since Verin’s cottage.

“Yeah, I think she would,” he said. “She’s not really a fan of the other originators. Too bad she’s a Tempus.”

“Yeah,” Amelia said. “Too bad.”

It was nothing she said. It was how she held on to the last word, past where it should have ended.

Bal opened his eyes and looked at her.

Amelia was looking right back. She had her eyebrows up and her lips pressed together, and she was waiting for something. Bal waited too. Nothing arrived. In the fire a pocket of sap hissed and spat.

Amelia sighed, took her feet off the table and straightened.

“Okay,” she said. “I can’t really blame you for not seeing where I was going with that. It ties into the High Priest thing anyway.” She turned on the cushion to face him and tucked one foot under her. “Do you remember what I said earlier? About there being more than one way to get back at my family?”

“In the corridor,” Bal said. “With the paintings.”

“Well. Imagine how Raizen would feel if someone with his rarest inheritance gift came over to the Blood.”

Bal tried to imagine it. He had seen Raizen’s face do two things so far. It had done nothing at all while Raizen read out his names, and it had done something sharp and unguarded for half a second when Amelia clapped. He tried to fit a third expression onto it, the face of a god finding out where the girl with the storm in her hair had gone, and found he would very much like to be in the room for it.

“I wasn’t really planning on anyone special,” Amelia said. “Not yet. But she’s too good to pass up.”

Bal pushed himself up out of the cushions.

“So you want me to convert her,” he said slowly, making sure he had it right. “That’s the thing your family locked you away for, last time. I’d be doing exactly that.” He searched her face. “Are you sure that’s what you want?”

Amelia’s chin came up. All the play had gone out of her mouth.

“They know I’m going to do something to get back at them,” she said. “This is the last thing they’d ever expect. Plus, we need more students.” She tipped her head at the room around them, at the one couch and the one chair, enough seats in all for three people if one of them didn’t mind the arm. “And the fact that she’s also really pretty is just another perk for you.”

Bal jerked upright so fast the couch bounced. His mouth fell open. He couldn’t seem to get it closed.

Amelia burst out laughing.

“I saw you looking at her,” she said. “It’s okay. I don’t mind.” She leaned back into the corner of the couch. “I was looking myself.”

Bal shook his head. Nothing he could say to that would come out right, so he left it where it lay and went back to the part he could do something about.

“How exactly am I supposed to do that?”

“As a High Priest, you can,” Amelia said. “You alone. Only a High Priest can convert someone’s sygl.”

She drew his hand over and turned it palm up on her knee. The sygl glowed in the middle of it, faint and red. With the tip of one fingernail she drew a line across the heel of his palm without breaking the skin.

“You cut your left hand here,” she said. “Then you cut theirs, and you put the two together and mingle the blood. Then you have to send your blood into them. Push it in.” She pressed her thumb into the middle of his palm. “It takes a bit. But once their body gets used to your blood, the sygl on their palm will change. After that they’re officially one of my followers.”

His hand lay in hers. It sounded simpler than writing out a pizza order.

On the edge of his bed at Saint Andre’s, she had told him it could never be done to someone who didn’t want it. So it would be up to Viridia. Hers to choose.

“That was another reason I made you High Priest,” she admitted. “It wasn’t only to keep you safe.”

Bal chuckled.

“It’s fine. I figured there might be other reasons.” His hand was still open on her knee, where she had left it. “Look. You’ve given me what I’ve wanted my whole life. Plus more excitement than I could ever have imagined. I don’t mind doing these things for you.”

Amelia lit up. It went across her whole face at once, and she pulled both knees up under her dress and hugged them.

“Okay,” she said, smiling. “So you’re okay with Viridia joining us?”

Bal pictured Viridia over the pizza at lunch, her pale blue eyes going bright when she laughed at the familiar joke. He heard her again, saying the gods were only people, out loud, in the middle of a courtyard full of their followers.

“Definitely,” he said.

* * *

After that, Amelia gave him his class schedule.

She had brought it back with her, a single sheet of heavy paper folded in thirds, and she watched him read it. Familiar class came first thing in the morning, then Rune working, then lunch. After lunch he had a class called Warfare, and after that one called Crafting.

The runes didn’t worry him. His engraving kit was lying at the bottom of the chest in the bedroom, and he knew more about runes already than about almost anything else. The other two were only names. He read it again and it said the same thing. Warfare could mean anything from swords to maps. Crafting could mean anything at all.

He opened his mouth to ask her, and closed it again. He wanted to walk in tomorrow and find out for himself.

He folded the schedule back into thirds and left it on the table where her feet had been.

The rest of the evening went quickly.

Amelia wanted to know everything about Saint Andre’s, and Bal found there was more of it to tell than he’d thought. He told her about washing dishes in the orphanage kitchen for the better part of a year to pay for a rune kit. He told her about Mrs. Brown, who could catch anybody at anything, and why he had never minded being caught. She already knew about the porridge.

When he ran out, he asked about her.

“How old were you?” he said. “When you got turned.”

“Eighteen.” Amelia rested her cheek on her knees and looked at him sideways. “Same as you.”

“Who did it?”

“I never found out. Whoever it was bit me and left me lying in the middle of a road, and a stranger found me there.” She watched the fire. “Back then everybody knew vampires existed, but there weren’t many of us. Even fewer then than there are now.”

“The stranger,” Bal said. “What happened to him?”

“He was a farmer. I killed him.”

She said it plainly, the same as she had said eighteen. Then she waited to see what he would do with it.

“I was delirious,” she said. “I needed blood, and he was there, and I didn’t know what I’d done until afterward.”

Bal let that sit. Eighteen, alone on a road, not knowing what she was. Then he asked the question that was actually in front of him.

“Is it still like that? The hunger?”

Something in Amelia’s shoulders came down.

“No,” she said. “It’s only that bad when you’ve just been turned. It ebbs away the longer you’re alive.” She stopped. “Or undead, I suppose.”

And she laughed, short and surprised, as if the word had tripped her on its way out.

“So how do you go from a regular vampire,” Bal asked, “to a Vampire Goddess?”

“Magical energy. A lot of it.” Amelia spread the fingers of one hand. “It takes an enormous amount to cross the threshold between mortal and Immortal. There’s no set amount. You just have to keep consuming it until it happens. I had a leg up, because I could absorb the magic from the people I fed on.”

“I was the newest originator,” Amelia went on. “When it finally happened, I was every bit as surprised as the others were. The moment I crossed over, I was somewhere else. A stone temple, with all of them standing in it, staring at me.” Her mouth twisted. “I didn’t have a clue where I was, and I asked. Aren’s the oldest, so he was the one who explained it. What had happened to me, and what I was now.”

“What did he say?”

“That I’d be given a sygl, and it would start appearing on mortals’ palms. The universe’s way of giving me followers.” She leaned over and touched one fingertip to the middle of his palm, to the red mark there. “Each follower gives me some magical energy. Enough of them, and it can sustain me forever.”

Her finger rested on the sygl.

For eighteen years there had been no palm there at all. Only a stump that itched whenever he looked at her roof.

He didn’t say any of that. Amelia took her hand back.

“There was always a war going on,” she said. “Somebody had always done something to somebody else. I stayed out of it. I’d rather spend my time with my own followers than play their games with them. They tried to pull me into one of their wars, once.” She shrugged one shoulder. “So I went down there myself and wiped out half the battlefield. After that, they decided to leave me alone.”

“Half,” Bal said.

“About half.”

“And that’s why they’re afraid of you.”

“That’s why. I don’t play by their rules. They play their followers off against each other. Pawns, in their wars. They never go anywhere near the fighting themselves. So when I walked into the middle of one, they understood I wasn’t going to play their game at all.”

The fire sparked and crackled. Neither of them said anything for a while. A log burned through in the middle and settled. The rest of the dorm was empty and silent.

“You’re the first person I’ve told my full story to,” Amelia said.

She said it to the fire.

“To be honest,” she said, “it feels good to talk about it.”

“I get that,” Bal said. “It’s easy, talking to you.”

She smiled at him.

“What will you be doing tomorrow,” he asked, “while I’m in my classes?”

Amelia thought it over.

“Well, after your familiar class, I’ll probably head out and see some of my other followers. On your home plane. Let them know I’m back.” She smoothed a crease out of her skirt. “Most of them won’t ever be able to apply to this school. But I’d like them to know I’m here, at least. I’m not like the other originators. I do try to keep in touch with my followers when I can.” She left the skirt alone. “Other than that, I’ve got a few other errands to run.”

The people she meant were somewhere back on his home plane. Grown men and women with red marks in their palms, keeping their left hands in their pockets at work. Some of them would have been told she was a myth their whole lives. Tomorrow somebody was going to open a door and find her standing there.

“Speaking of classes, though,” Amelia said, unfolding herself from the couch, “you need to get some sleep. A loud bell will wake you when it’s time to get up.”

“How loud?”

“Loud.”

Bal got up too. Before she could step away, he caught her hand. It was cool. He held on to it and smiled at her.

“Out of all the sygls I could have had,” he said, “I’m glad I ended up with yours.”

Amelia’s red eyes went very bright. She opened her mouth, and for once nothing came out of it. Her fingers tightened on his, hard enough that he felt her nails. Then she let go.


CHAPTER NINE

The Familiar Class

Breakfast was eggs, bacon, toast and a bowl of oatmeal, and Bal had to borrow paper to order it.

Any paper would do now, but he had come down to breakfast without any. Viridia watched him pat both his pockets, then tore a page out of the back of a notebook and slid it over.

“Write small,” she said. “I want the rest of that page back.”

He wrote small, tore the unused half off and handed it back, and laid his half on the plate.

“Let me see your schedule,” she said.

He’d picked it up on his way out. She unfolded it, laid it beside a sheet of her own, and ran her eyes down both. Twice somebody at the Tempus table leaned out to see who Viridia was eating with, and leaned back in. The second time, Bal caught a scrap of what they were saying over there. The clap, and what Raizen’s face had done at it. A boy with his collar turned up said a god didn’t get made to jump in front of the whole school and then forget it, and nobody at his end of the bench told him he was wrong.

“Familiar class,” Viridia read. “Same. Warfare, same. Crafting, same.” She tapped the second line of his. “Rune working, different.”

“So all of them but one.”

“All of them but one.” She handed the schedule back. “Lucky you.”

The oatmeal was thick and hot, with brown sugar melted into a crust on top. The eggs were good and the bacon was better and he ate both and then he ate her last piece of toast when she pushed it at him. At Saint Andre’s, breakfast had been something you got through.

When the plates were empty, they went to class together.

* * *

The familiar class was at the back of the school. They followed a long hallway past closed doors and tall windows, and the further they went, the fewer other students there were around them.

“So where’s your familiar?” Viridia asked. “I figured she’d be waiting at the table with a slice of my breakfast.”

“She’s got things to do today. I’ll call her when I need her.” Bal tried to remember how Amelia had put it, sitting on the couch with her feet up. “She thinks it’ll make the best impression if everyone sees I can call an originator to me whenever I’ve got a problem. She knows I wouldn’t, though. I prefer handling my own problems.”

“I get that,” Viridia said.

She let it be, and the hallway went on.

The classroom was the last door on the far right. Inside, on the far wall, was a second door, a heavy wooden one with an iron latch, and that one led outside. Some of them, Bal supposed, were bound to bring something large out of the forest, and it made sense to call it out there instead of explaining doorways to it.

The room itself wasn’t much. Twenty desks and chairs stood in four rows of five in front of a chalkboard. Down the left-hand wall were two tall windows, and through them was the forest, close and green and dark under the trees. Someone had written MR. PORTER on the chalkboard in large, careful capitals.

They were two of the first students in, and they took two desks together in the back corner, under the second window.

Mr. Porter was already at his own desk at the front. He was feeding cheese to a raven.

It was a big bird, bigger than any raven Bal had seen sitting on a gutter, and it stood on the corner of the desk with its feathers sleeked down and its head tilted, watching the teacher’s fingers. Mr. Porter broke a yellow cube off a block wrapped in wax paper. The raven took it from him, neatly, and lifted its beak to the ceiling to swallow it. Then it tilted its head again and waited for the next one.

The others came in by ones and twos. Nearly all of them looked at Bal as they came past, at the blood sygl on his sweater and then at his face. A few gave his corner a wide berth so they would not have to walk by his desk.

When the last chair was filled, Mr. Porter wiped his fingers on a handkerchief, folded the wax paper over the rest of the cheese, and stood.

He was a man in his fifties, with gray hair that had gone thin across the top and a brown tweed jacket buttoned over his stomach. His glasses caught the light from the windows whenever he turned his head, and while they did, you could not see his eyes at all. He walked to the front of the chalkboard and looked out at them.

“Why do you need a familiar?” Mr. Porter asked.

Nobody answered. Twenty students turned to one another in case somebody else had brought the answer.

“A familiar has many uses,” he told them, since nobody had. “Not the least of which is companionship, which should not be discounted by any means. A familiar can help to carry the magical load of a difficult spell. It can carry messages. One could even save your life.” He lifted a hand toward the corner of his desk. “Take Huginn here.”

The raven heard its name and fluffed itself up.

“Huginn has been with me for close to fifteen years,” the teacher went on. “In that time he has become a great friend. He has saved my life twice and helped me in numerous other ways. He also gives me a different perspective when I’m confronted with a problem.” Huginn made a small, dry noise in his throat, agreeing or asking for cheese. “It is for this reason that none of you will be able to leave this class without a familiar of your own.”

A boy in the front row put his hand straight up. Bal had heard someone in the hallway call him Tyler.

“We can’t leave the class?” Tyler asked. “Like, ever?”

Mr. Porter shook his head.

“I’m sorry. I misspoke. You will not be able to leave the forest before you have bonded a familiar.”

From the noise the class made, nobody thought that was much better. Mr. Porter did not give them time to say so. He went straight on into how a bond was made.

The incantation Bal already knew, and Mr. Porter spent hardly any time on it. Most of the lecture was about the part the household enchantments book had covered in two lines: what you offered the creature, and how to be sure it was enough for the bond to be balanced.

“There must be balance,” he warned, and looked along the rows to make sure they had all heard it. “I cannot stress that enough.”

When he was satisfied, he waved his hand.

A soft chime came from every desk at once. On each one, a silver ring had appeared.

Bal’s was lying in the groove at the top of the desk where a pencil was supposed to go. It was plain silver, a little heavy for its size, and resting in his palm it gave off a faint, slow pulse, warm as a stone after the sun has gone off it. Around the room people were turning their rings in the light and talking at once.

Mr. Porter cleared his throat. The talking stopped.

“Those are bonding rings,” he said. “Every so often they release a pulse of magic, and a creature may respond to it. That does not mean it will be willing to form a bond. It will only make it curious.” He let that sit. “Be as safe as you possibly can. Do not try to approach any creature you are not equipped to deal with.” The glasses flashed as he turned to take in the back of the room. “Students have died in there.”

Nobody talked after that.

Bal weighed the ring in his palm. Then he laid it in the pencil groove where it had come from, and raised his hand.

Mr. Porter’s eyes narrowed behind the glasses.

“Yes, Mr. Norheart. You have a question?”

“What are we supposed to do if we already have a familiar?” Bal asked.

He didn’t check what the teacher’s face was doing. Out of the corner of his eye was Viridia, with her chin on her folded hands, waiting, enjoying herself enormously.

Mr. Porter went red. It started above his collar and climbed.

“All students were ordered not to bond a familiar before attending this class, Mr. Norheart,” he said. “I take it you think the rules do not apply to you. I assure you that is not the case.” He came out from behind the front row and stood in the aisle. “But since you feel you have such a grasp on things that you do not need my instruction, why don’t you show us your familiar? Is it a rodent, perhaps? Or a toad?”

Bal couldn’t help it. He laughed.

It was a short one, and by the time he had it back under control Mr. Porter had gone from red to purple.

“Well, come on, then!” he snapped. “We don’t have all day. Show us your mastery.”

The whole room had turned around in their seats. Twenty faces, and Huginn’s.

Bal raised his palm to the ceiling and pushed his magyk through the sygl.

The magic left him and ran out along the thread, a long way, to wherever she was.

A second later Amelia was sitting on his desk.

She sat cross-legged in the middle of it, in the black dress, spine straight, hands loose on her knees. The brand showed clear on her forehead, red and plain as anything. The silver ring lay in its groove, an inch from her knee. Her eyes on his were wide and bright and perfectly innocent.

“You called, Master?”

Beside him Viridia made a sound like a kettle and doubled over her desk.

It wasn’t Amelia who had set her off. It was Mr. Porter, and no wonder. All the purple had drained out of the man at once, and nothing had come in to replace it. He was the color of the chalk.

“Lady Amelia,” he stammered.

Chairs scraped everywhere. Students were up out of their seats and backing away, and the ones in the back two rows did not stop until they had their shoulders to the wall. Tyler was crouched behind his own chair. Up front, Huginn opened his beak, shut it again, and stood perfectly still.

“What are you doing here?” Mr. Porter asked.

Amelia’s head came round toward him, slowly. By the time it had finished coming round, the innocence was gone from her face. What remained was calm and cold, and every bit of it was pointed at Mr. Porter.

“Are you implying,” she asked softly, “that I need permission from you to go anywhere I wish?”

Mr. Porter was sweating now. From the back row Bal could see it shining at his hairline. His hands came up, both of them, and waved in front of him.

“No, no,” he said. “No. I was only curious what led you to grace us with your presence, that’s all.”

“This is the familiar class,” Amelia said. “Is it not?”

“It is.”

“Then that is why I am here.” She tipped her hand toward Bal without taking her eyes off the teacher. “My Master called for me, so I came.”

Bal was doing his best to keep a straight face, and losing.

“But you’re,” he began. “And he’s a…”

He stopped. Bal watched him go looking for the end of that sentence and find nothing anywhere that would not get him killed.

Amelia slid off the desk.

She strolled toward him up the aisle between the desks, in no hurry at all. The students in her path found other places to be. She came to a stop an arm’s length from Mr. Porter, close enough that he could have touched her, if he had wanted to, which he did not. He had to tip his head down to look at her. It did not help.

“Think your next words through,” Amelia said, “very carefully.”

Mr. Porter’s mouth opened and stayed open, wordless. It was a good choice.

Bal decided he had better step in before someone got hurt. By someone, he meant Mr. Porter. By hurt, he meant killed.

“It’s okay, Amelia,” he said, and he left off the Lady on purpose. “I’m sure he didn’t mean anything by it. Head on back to your errands, and I’ll meet you in the dorm later.”

It was like watching someone flip a switch. Amelia spun around to face him, and the cold was gone from her. She was beaming.

“Okay, Master!”

She gave him a little wave over the heads of the class, and then the aisle where she had been standing was empty.

Once she was gone, everyone in the room breathed out, except the two in the back corner. Bit by bit the class came back to their desks and sat, and they sent Bal the kind of looks you might send a dog that had turned out to be a bear. Viridia had her face back. Her mouth kept trying to get away from her at the corners.

This would be all over the castle by dinner.

Mr. Porter did not sit down. He stood at the front with one hand on the edge of his desk and gave them a few last instructions. He stumbled over most of them and said two of them twice. Then he went to the door at the side of the room and lifted the iron latch and pulled it open on the forest.

“Don’t come back,” he told them, “until you’ve bonded something.”

They filed out past him. Viridia stopped by Bal’s chair on her way.

“See you in Warfare,” she whispered, and followed the others into the trees.

Mr. Porter said something under his breath to Huginn. The raven dropped off the desk, flapped twice, and skimmed low over the students’ heads after the class to keep an eye on them. Mr. Porter shut the door.

With only the two of them left, the room went very quiet. Mr. Porter stayed by the door a while before he turned around.

“Ah,” he said. “Um. Seeing as you already have a familiar, I could excuse you from this class, if you wish, Mr. Norheart.” His hand went to the knot of his tie. “You are welcome to stay, of course,” he added quickly.

“I’ll stay,” Bal said, “if you don’t mind.”

It was plain from Mr. Porter’s face that he did mind, a great deal.

“After today, at least,” Bal said. “Could I go to the library now, if that’s okay?”

Mr. Porter assured him that was more than okay.

Bal stood. The silver ring was still in the groove of his desk, where Amelia’s knee had been. Somebody in this class might need a spare. He left it lying there and took the hallway door.

* * *

The library was upstairs, as promised: straight up the main stairs, a right at the top, and then the second door on the left.

Partly he was curious what books a school this old kept. But he also had a plan. Last night, on the couch, Amelia had talked about still wanting revenge on the others for what they had done to her. Somewhere between that and the bell he had made up his mind to look into ways he could help. Something besides converting followers.

He could have asked her. She would have told him anything he wanted to know. He wanted to do the reading himself first, and come to her with an idea or two of his own.

The library held thousands of books. The shelves went up two stories to a narrow iron gallery, with ladders on rails to reach it, and the whole room smelled of paper and warm dust. Bal stopped inside the door and turned a slow circle.

There was a librarian at a desk near the front, a woman with a pencil behind each ear. Thankfully she was the helpful sort. He told her what he was after: the originators, and what laws governed how they dealt with their own followers, and with each other’s followers. She went off into the stacks without a word and returned with an armful.

Bal read for the next hour at a table under a window.

Most of it was about wars. The longest account was of a war between Naisura and Kenmei. It went on for chapters, battle after battle, and in the end Naisura won it with one stroke. One of his high priests caught Kenmei’s command tent in stone, sealed it in whole, and sank it underground. After that Kenmei’s army fell apart, and the rest of it was wiped out soon after.

Bal read that part twice.

He thought of a temple behind an orphanage that nobody could get into, built by another of Naisura’s high priests. He turned the page.

He looked for Amelia next. There was hardly anything. A line or two, and then nothing, page after page. It looked to Bal as though the others had tried their best to leave her alone, and he could guess why. She had never kept to their rules.

From what he could tell from the books, the rest of them did. And the rules were not kind ones. The originators looked at their followers as pieces on a game board, to be moved up and traded off and lost. Everyone’s life could be spent except their own.

Bal had always looked up to the originators.

Some people were born with magyk, he knew that, and not all of them got sygls. He had grown up thinking the ones who did were chosen. That each of those marks was a piece of an originator, given on purpose, by someone who wanted the world to be better and had picked that person to help. He had wanted one so badly. He had sat on a roof and pushed magic through a stump for years on the hope of it.

Now here it was in their own histories, in plain print, and it matched what Amelia had said at the cottage about eight high priests outside a temple. The oatmeal sat heavy in his stomach.

He shut the book.

The clock on the wall said it was nearly time for his next class. Bal carried the stack back to the front desk and asked the librarian if she could set aside any other books she thought he might like.

She promised she would, and made a note on a card, and took the pencil from behind her left ear to do it.

* * *

Before Rune working he made a detour, down to the dorm. The wall let him in. In the bedroom he opened the chest and dug his rune engraving kit out of the bottom of his pack, still wrapped in its dish towel. He left the towel in the chest and tucked the flat wooden case under his arm.

The rune working class was taught by a short, skinny man named Mr. Winters, who wrote his name on the board and then the reason for the class underneath it. The point, he told them, was to make them familiar with all sorts of runes, what each one was for, and how to use them properly.

Bal undid the brass catch on his kit and laid the case open on the desk beside his paper.

He spent that first class copying runes off the chalkboard. The ones he knew from Fredrick’s books he skipped. The rest he copied stroke by stroke, slowly, getting each angle where it was supposed to be. Among them was a narrow, hooked rune Mr. Winters called the avoidance rune.

Mr. Winters came down the rows while they worked. At Bal’s desk he stopped and bent over the paper.

“That’s precise,” he said, and tapped the avoidance rune with one finger. “Very good. That’s an essential one to know if you’re ever traveling at night in unfriendly territory.”

He moved on. Bal studied the rune a while longer, the hook of it and the clean line through the middle, and then he copied the next one: a squat purification rune, for turning whatever liquid it touched into plain water.

He was almost glad when the bell went for lunch.

Almost, because he had liked every minute of the runes. He closed the kit, fastened the catch, and carried it out with him, and all down the hallways to the dining hall he wondered whether Viridia would be at lunch, or whether she was still somewhere out in that forest, holding up a silver ring and waiting for something to come. He remembered Mr. Porter’s warning about the forest, and walked faster.


CHAPTER TEN

Hopper and the Rapier

Bal hurried down to lunch with the rune kit tucked against his ribs, and he spotted Viridia before he was properly through the doors.

She had their table to herself and was writing on a scrap of paper. She finished, laid the paper on her plate, and the plate went away and returned loaded. Chicken nuggets, a heap of them, and a pile of french fries, with a paper cup of ketchup tucked in at the edge.

So she had made it back. All ten fingers, by the look of her from the door, and no sign that anything had chewed on her.

Bal swung his legs over the bench across from her and parked the kit on the bench beside him. This time he had come with paper. He tore the blank bottom off his page of rune notes and borrowed her pencil. Chicken nuggets and french fries looked like the right idea, so he wrote that and put the strip down in front of him. His plate went. It returned with the same as hers, cup of ketchup and all.

The first nugget was too hot and he ate it anyway.

“Lady Amelia really shook up the class,” Viridia said, dragging a fry through her ketchup. “No one expected that.”

Bal chuckled. “I’d have been surprised if they had. What were they saying?”

“Some of the guys were making the usual comments.” Viridia rolled her eyes. “How it’s hot, you having a beautiful woman at your beck and call who calls you Master.” She bit the fry in half. “But quite a few of them think it’s kind of cool, you having that kind of connection with your originator. None of us are ever going to have that.”

She gave that a second to settle.

“I do think Lady Amelia is the coolest, though.”

“I agree,” Bal said. “What makes you say that?”

Viridia reached for the salt shaker and salted her remaining nuggets. She was generous about it. When she was done she stirred the pile with a fry and salted the ones she had missed, until the whole pile glittered.

“You know what a bloodwolf is, right?”

Bal had to think. He had read about them once, in a bestiary, long enough ago that he needed a minute to find the page again.

It returned to him a piece at a time. Bloodwolves were rare. They had very few pups, and their pelts were tough enough to turn the edge of a longsword, so anyone who had seen one at all had something to brag about for years. Bonding one was something else altogether. Their fur was a light silver, with blood red laid across the top of it, along the back and over the shoulders. They were also quite a bit larger than an ordinary wolf.

“Yeah,” he said slowly. “Are you saying you saw one?”

Viridia pushed the shaker back to the middle of the table and dropped her voice.

“So after Porter let us out,” she said, “everybody went charging off into the trees in one big clump, and I hung back. I was standing there trying to decide which way to go. And then Lady Amelia was there. Not right next to me. A little way behind. She had to say my name twice before I turned around.”

“I thought she had errands.”

“Apparently I was one.” Viridia waved the next nugget at him. “Anyway. We walked, and we talked for a while. Once I’d gotten used to her a bit, she asked me whether I wanted to show up everybody else.”

“What did you say?”

“I laughed. I told her I didn’t think anybody was going to top your performance.”

Bal thought about Mr. Porter’s face going from purple to nothing at all, and decided that was fair.

“She said that was fair too,” Viridia said. “And then she asked me again. Would I like to show everyone else up. By then I was curious where it was going. So I said yes, I would. She told me to give her my ring and follow her.”

“Where to?”

“Uphill, mostly. It was a good thirty minutes. We came out where the forest runs up against the side of the mountain, and there was a cave there. A big one. You could have driven a buggy into it.” She tapped the table in front of her plate. “She told me to wait right there. Then she walked into the cave by herself, with my ring.”

“And you waited.”

“I waited. Not long. Long enough that I started working out what I was going to tell Porter.” Viridia’s eyes had gone somewhere past his shoulder, and she was smiling at whatever was there. “Then she came walking out again, and there was a bloodwolf walking behind her.”

Bal forgot all about the nugget in his hand.

“It’s not full grown,” Viridia said. “Look at the paws, though. She’s going to be huge. Her paws are already bigger than my hands.” She held one up, fingers spread. “She sat down a ways off from me. Lady Amelia came over on her own, and she said, ‘The bloodwolf’s name is Hopper. She’ll agree to a bond with you. All she requires is bloody meat to eat, and the possibility of being around me.’”

“Hopper,” Bal said.

“Hopper.”

“Around Amelia?”

“That’s what I said. The meat part I understood. The other part, I just gaped at her, so she explained it.” Viridia talked with her hands now, building it in the air over the nuggets. “The bloodwolves are partly hers. She made them. She took a bunch of big plains wolves and gave them her blood, and that’s where the breed came from. They don’t need blood like a vampire does. It helps them grow, though. And they look at her as a mother. Sort of.” She dropped her hands. “So being near Lady Amelia now and then is a big deal to Hopper. It’s part of what makes the bond balanced.”

Bal leaned away from his plate.

Amelia wanted him to convert Viridia. She had said so last night, by the fire. Now Viridia had a familiar who would want to be near Amelia while the bond lasted, and Amelia was mostly wherever Bal was. Which meant Viridia would be too, for the time being, and she had walked back through those trees without the faintest idea of it.

Bal had to hand it to her. A whole morning of errands, and somewhere in the middle of them Amelia had gone up a mountain and found Viridia the best present in the forest. It happened to come with a reason to keep visiting.

He had a feeling Viridia wouldn’t mind, when she worked it out.

“You should have seen them,” Viridia said, “when I walked out of the trees with her behind me. The whole class went dead quiet. One of the other Tempus kids said Raizen wasn’t going to like it. Me having a bloodwolf for a familiar.”

“What did you tell him?”

“Nothing. I laughed. Raizen doesn’t pay any attention to his followers one way or the other. That’s what the rest of them don’t get. They all think if they do well enough, he’ll notice them.”

She pushed her plate away. A few salted nuggets were still on it.

“At least your originator talks to you,” Viridia said. “She really is nice. I honestly didn’t expect that. Unless somebody disrespects you, anyway.” She laughed.

Bal shrugged, a little sheepishly. “I think she’s protective. It’s sweet.”

“Oh, it is,” Viridia agreed.

* * *

Their next class was Warfare. They left the hall, turned right, and went out through the big front doors into the afternoon. From there they walked around the outside of the castle to the back, where the training grounds were.

The grounds were wide and flat, with six training areas marked out on them in packed sand, each one roped off from the next. Racks of weapons stood along the edges of every one. Swords, mostly, and spears and axes, and a few things nobody had ever taught Bal the names of. The sun had been on the sand all morning, and the heat came up off it through the soles of his shoes.

In the middle of the nearest area a young man was working through sword forms by himself, waiting on the class to arrive. He was broad through the shoulders, with the same build and the same easy, settled stance as Naisura, and Bal would have believed it if someone had told him they were father and son. He had his shirt on, at least. That was one difference.

They arrived last of anyone. Everyone’s eyes followed them across the sand as they went and joined the end of the line.

The young man finished his form, lowered his sword, and came to stand in front of them.

“In this class,” he said, “you will learn to protect yourselves. My name is Zoran.”

He didn’t waste a word after that. He swept his free hand around at the racks.

“Take your time choosing a weapon. Pick each one up. Imagine using it. Take the one that speaks to you. Once you have one, come back to this line, and I’ll pair you off to begin practicing. Then I’ll take each of you through some exercises to get used to your new weapons.”
