He stepped back, and that was all the dismissal they got. The whole line broke at once and scrambled for the racks.

* * *

They took the last rack in the row, because nobody else was headed for it. Bal leaned his kit against the end post, where nothing would step on it.

A minute later a girl joined them there, a few pegs down.

She was shy-looking, with brown hair pulled back in a tail, and glasses that kept sliding down her nose no matter how many times she pushed them back up. She had freckles across the bridge of her nose, under the glasses. Bal noticed, idly, that the sygl on her sweater was Kenmei’s. Nobody else in the class had come within a weapon’s length of him. By now there had to be stories going around the castle about him, and whatever the stories were, she must have heard them and come anyway.

“I’m Bal,” he told her, reaching for a great axe while he talked. “This is Viridia.”

The axe was massive. He got it free of its pegs and tested the weight of it at arm’s length, and the head of it tipped toward the sand on its own. Bal was strong for his size. He was not strong enough to see himself swinging this thing at anyone. He hung it back up.

“L-Lora,” the girl said. “Lora Aegis.”

She had gone straight past the axes. She took a rapier from its pegs, stepped out onto the sand, set her feet, and started an exercise with it.

It was brief and plain. Point out, a step forward, the blade turning over, a step back. Then the same again, mirrored. She did it without hurrying and without stopping, her glasses slipping a little further down with each lunge, and they did not trouble her.

Bal watched her go through it twice. He liked how she moved. Then he took the next rapier along and copied her.

The rapier was four and a half feet long, made of good steel, with a guard that curled around the back of his hand. It was much lighter than it looked. He set his feet where hers were and pushed the point out when she did. He drew it back when she did. While he copied her he imagined himself using it, as Zoran had said, and that came easily. The blade went where he sent it the moment he sent it there. By the third time through, his hand had quit gripping the hilt and started holding it, and nobody could have told him when that happened.

He could have kept at it all afternoon.

At the end of her exercise, Lora noticed him. She studied his feet, which were where hers had been. Then his point, which was nearly there too.

“I, um. I’ve had practice,” she said. “My father trained me. He was with Kenmei’s army when he was younger.”

Bal had read about that army that same morning, in a book by the library window: a command tent sealed in stone and sunk, and everybody left outside it finished soon after. He didn’t ask her which war her father had marched in, or how many of the men beside him had come home again. You didn’t ask that of someone whose name you had learned a minute ago.

“That’s amazing,” Bal said, and meant it. “Would you mind partnering with me?”

Lora blinked at him.

Viridia, meanwhile, had pulled a spear off the next rack over and was holding it upright beside her, looking it up and down. She thumped the butt of it on the sand once, and kept it.

“If you’re not put off by all the rumors about my originator,” Bal added, as the three of them started back toward the line in front of Zoran.

“No, it doesn’t bother me,” Lora said quickly. “I’m just glad to have someone to practice with.”

Bal was about to ask why on earth nobody had wanted to practice with her. Zoran got there first, sorting the line into pairs as fast as he could point. He took one look at Lora’s grip and one look at Bal’s, and put them together. Viridia went off to the neighboring sand with a Terra boy.

“You,” Zoran said to Bal. “Guard and parry. You’ll learn the forms.” He turned to Lora. “You’ve used a rapier before.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Then you attack him. Slowly.”

Zoran showed Bal the guards. There were only a few, high and low and out to the sides, and all of them returned to the same middle position, point up, elbow in. Bal learned them standing still. Then Zoran went on to the next pair, and Lora came at him.

Slowly, as she’d been told. The point came toward his chest. Bal moved his blade across and met it, and steel slid on steel, and her point went past him to the left.

Again. High. He was late. She stopped her point a hand’s width from his collar and waited until he had the parry, then drew back.

Again. Low. He had that one.

Again.

Again.

Fifteen minutes in, his forearm was burning. Bal kept the point up anyway, and after a while it shook, and he kept it up shaking. Lora did not speed up and did not slow down. Each time she came in, she came in the same, until his blade knew where to meet hers before he had finished thinking about it.

They didn’t talk much. There wasn’t much room for it. Between rounds, while Zoran was busy with someone else, Bal learned a little about her. Lora was usually quiet, and mostly kept to herself.

“I like books better than people,” she said, during one of the breaks, and then went red. “Mostly. Not you. I mean, I don’t know you. I mean…” She stopped and started over. “Books don’t mind if you don’t say anything.”

“Then we’ll get along fine,” Bal said. “Quiet’s okay with me.”

She peered at him above the rims of her glasses for a second. Then she raised her rapier, and he raised his.

* * *

When Zoran finally called time, Bal’s arm felt about twice its proper weight. He hung the rapier back on its pegs, third from the left, so he would know which one it was next time. Lora’s went up beside it. Then he collected his rune kit from the foot of the post.

“You can sit with Viridia and me,” Bal said, “whenever we eat. If you want to.”

Lora wiped her palms down the front of her sweater.

“I’ll think about it,” she said.

She went off around the opposite corner of the castle, by herself, walking fast. She didn’t look back. Bal watched her go until the corner of the wall took her.

Viridia came up beside him, swinging her arms. The spear was back on its rack. She had sand on one knee, which bothered her not at all.

“That Terra boy,” she said, “kept trying to knock my spear out of my hands. The whole time. He’s built like a door. I hit him in the shins twice.” She considered. “Three times. He said the third shouldn’t count.”

Bal let her tell him all of it while they followed the castle wall around toward their last class of the day, and they stepped out into the courtyard by the stage.

Amelia was there, waiting for them.

She sat on the stage’s edge with her palms flat on the stone. The long dress was gone.

She was wearing a school uniform.


CHAPTER ELEVEN

Break Their Toys

Her uniform was the same as Viridia’s, with differences. Where Viridia had Raizen’s mark, the blood sygl sat on the back of Amelia’s tight black sweater, red on black, the same mark Bal wore on his. Below the sweater was a short black skirt. Where every other girl in the school wore plain white socks, Amelia’s were long and striped black and red, and they came up past her calves.

She looked good in it. Bal could admit that much. He also could not keep from looking, and Amelia knew it. She hopped down off the stage, landed lightly, and her smile got wider with every step she took toward them.

“So how have your classes been?” she asked.

Bal glanced sideways. Viridia was having a hard time of her own. She kept fixing her eyes on the castle wall, and they kept drifting back to the striped socks. Bal found this far more entertaining than his own problem.

“Pretty good so far,” he said. “We’re on our way to the last one now. How are your errands going?”

Amelia’s smile faded a little at a time.

“That’s partly why I’m here, unfortunately,” she said. She faced Viridia. “I’m sorry, but I’m going to have to steal him for a while.”

Viridia flapped a hand at her. “It’s fine. Do you want me to tell our teacher?”

“No. I’ve already taken care of it.”

“Okay, then.” Viridia was already backing toward the castle doors. “You two have fun!”

Bal’s attention went after her. He admired the swing of her skirt as she took the steps, and her legs too, and he kept on admiring until the doorway swallowed her. Then he turned back around and found Amelia studying him with her head on one side.

“What?” he asked, and laughed, because his ears had gone warm.

Amelia smirked. “Oh, nothing. Just admiring the view.” She let that hang for exactly as long as it took him to work out which view she meant. “So. Ready?”

“Ready for what? What’s going on?”

She didn’t answer. She took his hand instead, and the courtyard went out.

* * *

Wind hit him first. It was cold and smelled of cut grass, and it came straight up a long slope and flattened his sweater against his ribs. Bal had the engraving kit pinned under one arm, and he clamped down on it hard before he knew he had done it. His ears popped. The ground under his shoes was springy and uneven, nothing like flagstones, and for a second his knees could not agree with it.

They stood on top of a hill. Below them, filling the valley from one side to the other, was an army.

Never in his life had Bal seen so many soldiers in one place. Tents ran off in rows to the far tree line, gray canvas going pale with distance, and between the rows were cookfires and wagons and horse lines and narrow lanes trodden down to dirt. Flags stood up all through the main camp on tall poles, and each carried the same jagged yellow mark, snapping in the wind. It was Raizen’s. Tempus. Only minutes ago that mark had gone up the castle steps on the back of Viridia’s sweater. Here it flew over every tent in the valley.

Somebody had sewn all those flags. Hundreds. Bal wondered if they had a person whose whole job it was.

“He doesn’t already know we’re trying to steal Viridia from him,” he said, once Amelia let go of his hand. “Does he?”

The army below was in the middle of marching drills. Block after block of soldiers came across the open ground in step, and two blocks would swing toward each other and fold together into one, and then that one would turn and fold into the next. It was slow, and very tidy. Whenever a whole rank turned at once, the sun caught on their swords and spearheads and ran down the line.

“No, it’s not that,” Amelia answered. “I had a feeling one of them might try to go after my followers soon, now that I’m free. So I’ve been out looking for the other originators’ armies. I’ve only found two so far. Raizen’s, and Naisura’s.” She folded her arms against the wind. “Naisura’s doesn’t look like it’s done anything in months. Last year he went after Nera and got beaten very badly. He’s probably still licking his wounds.”

“So why would Raizen come after you,” Bal asked, “if he doesn’t know about Viridia?”

“The games they play.” Amelia started and then stopped, and frowned down at the camp. “I hate that they think of war as a game. But they do. And their fights are ranked, I suppose is the best way to put it. Almost nobody goes after someone too far above them.”

“Like Naisura going after Nera.”

“Exactly like that. So Raizen’s only real choices right now are me or Naisura. He could probably beat Naisura. But I’m free again, and the job of dealing with me has fallen to Raizen.”

Bal worked it through.

“Because you’re ranked below all of them,” he said. “Beneath their notice.” A block of soldiers far down the slope missed its turn and had to go around again. “So I’m surprised they all came after you together like they did, last time. Knowing what they’re like now. It’s hard to picture them working together on anything.”

“Ah. That was because they felt I was stealing from them.” Amelia watched the soldiers too. “They do not share. What’s theirs, they feel should stay theirs. Me winning a few of their followers over was an insult to them, personally. They couldn’t let an insult like that stand.”

Down in the valley a horn blew, flat and long, and every block stopped where it stood.

Bal took them all in. Men with spears. Men with swords. Somebody’s sons, somebody’s fathers, standing in rows on a hillside so that an originator who never thought about them could feel he’d answered an insult. Only this morning he had met armies like this one in the library, in neat print. Battle after battle, and nobody in the books had ever asked the people in the rows.

He would not step aside while innocent people were killed for the originators’ amusement. He knew that already.

“So where does that leave us?” he asked.

Amelia smiled at him sidelong. It was a hard smile, and it showed her fangs.

“We play their game,” she said, “and break their toys.”

* * *

The horn blew again below them, two short notes, and the blocks began to come apart.

“I’ve moved quite a few of my followers to a hidden temple of mine,” Amelia told him. “It was built about a thousand years ago, by a follower of mine, which surprised me as much as anybody. His name was Peter.”

She said the name gently, as if it might break.

“He was the only blood sygl ever born into his family. All the rest of them had always gotten Terra’s sygl, every generation, and they were stoneworkers, so it helped them enormously. Peter was sixteen when his sygl came, and he’d already learned the whole family business. And then his palm came up red.” Amelia’s arms tightened across her sweater. “They shunned him for it. So he went wandering up into the mountains. A few days later he found the perfect spot for a temple. He wasn’t a Terra, and his family had thrown him out, so he built a temple to me instead. Every stone in it was laid by his own hand.”

Sixteen, and cast off for the very mark on Bal’s own palm. The sygl in his hand seemed to sit up and want attention.

“How can you know all that?” he asked. “You were locked away.”

The corners of Amelia’s mouth turned down.

“I suppose you should hear it anyway,” she said. “The normal offering in one of my temples is blood. Every temple of mine has a stone bowl and a sharpened stone knife. A follower cuts themselves and lets a little blood drip into the bowl as an offering. Then the priest seals the cut and tries to help them with whatever’s wrong. And whenever someone makes an offering, I take in some of their recent memories, for a short time.”

The wind pushed her hair across her face, and she let it stay.

“It took Peter twelve years to finish his temple,” she said. “As a final dedication, he killed himself on my altar. He let his lifeblood run out so he could live with me forever.” She was quiet for a breath. “All of his memories are still with me. From his first day to his last. What his family did to him hurt him very badly. And he was glad, at the end. Glad he’d been able to make something that would last long after he was gone.”

Twelve years. Bal could not think of anything he had kept at for twelve years.

He thought of a boy alone in the mountains, sixteen, with a red palm, setting one stone on top of another for a god who would never come to see it. And then the god herself, standing on a cold hill, carrying the whole of him around inside her, birth to death, with nobody to tell.

Bal stepped forward and pulled her into a hug.

Amelia went stiff in his arms. He held on anyway. After a moment her arms came up, slowly, and went around him, and her chin settled on his shoulder. She was cool right through, sun or no sun. They stood like that on top of the hill, above the whole of Raizen’s army, and neither of them was in any hurry to let go.

When they stepped apart, she would not quite meet his eyes, and she was smiling.

Her followers were afraid of her. The other originators couldn’t be bothered with her. Bal liked her, and from how long she had held on he understood that it was worth something to her.

“What can I do to help?” he asked.

* * *

She told him.

The Blood army had to be trained, and someone had to think up tricks to help it beat the other originators’ armies. Leaving out the originators themselves, a High Priest was the head of an army. Under the High Priests came the regular priests, and under them everyone else.

“You’ll need to meet with them every so often,” Amelia said, “so they can see you’re involved. I can’t interfere, not if I want to look impartial. I’ll give you advice. But I can’t deal directly with my own lesser followers, or with the enemy’s army. Everything has to go through you.”

Bal let that sink in.

“You do know I’m only eighteen, right?” he said. “Do you really think grown men are going to listen to an eighteen-year-old about war?”

Amelia’s eyes went hard.

“No. I expect them to listen to their High Priest,” she said. “If they do not, it will be handled.”

Bal made a quiet note to himself to settle whatever he could before she killed anybody.

“Okay,” he said, in his most reasonable voice. “Then that’s what I’ll do. I’ll get some books on military strategy from the library and start reading up. Do you have more people to move to your temple?”

“Yes. It’ll take me the better part of a week. I have to move them, and all the supplies they’ll need while they’re there.”

Bal turned back to the valley. The soldiers had moved on to weapons drills, pair facing pair across the trampled grass, and the clash of swords rolled up the slope to them, late and small.

“When do I need to meet them?”

“That’s up to you, really. Most of the upper command are already there.”

Bal squinted at the sun. It had come a fair way down the sky while they talked.

“Do we have time before I’ve got to be back for dinner?”

“You just want to go hang out with Viridia,” Amelia said, in a singsong.

Bal shrugged. He didn’t bother denying it.

“Well, let’s go, then,” she said, and hooked her arm through his. Grass and wind and the whole army were gone between one breath and the next.

* * *

From the way Amelia had talked, Bal had expected something modest. One man had built it, after all.

The window in front of him said otherwise.

They had come down in a bedchamber, and the bedchamber had a tall arched window, and through it the temple fell away beneath him in tiers of pale cut stone. It filled the floor of a canyon. On three sides the mountains went straight up, bare and gray and streaked with snow at the top, and the only way in was a single narrow pass on the fourth side. Even a small army could hold that pass. Bal understood at once why Amelia had made this place her base.

There were windows everywhere he looked, rows of them, set into wing after wing. The rooms could have held several hundred people without filling up. The priests’ quarters alone were large, and two whole sets of rooms belonged to High Priests, and he was standing in one of them.

Far down in the lowest courtyard, small figures were crossing back and forth with crates on their shoulders. Somebody was hanging washing from a line strung between two pillars. A handful of the followers Amelia had already brought, making a start on living here.

Twelve years. Looking out at it, he could believe all twelve.

“I promoted two of my followers to priest yesterday,” Amelia said behind him, “to help you. They’re next door. I’ll let you meet them, and then they’ll go and talk to the commander.”

Bal turned around. The room was dark red and old gold. There was a desk, a washstand, a chair with carved arms, and a great bed hung with curtains, and Amelia had already thrown herself backward onto the middle of it. She lay with her arms flung out and her striped socks crossed at the ankle, making herself at home.

“You’re not coming with me?”

Amelia held up an empty hand. Then it was not empty anymore. There was a book in it.

“No,” she said, and opened the book on her stomach. “This part is yours to do on your own. Oh! Before I forget again.”

She clicked her fingers at him without looking up from the page. Something cold slid onto his middle finger. It was a slim white ring, and set into it was a single blood red ruby, round and dark, like an eye.

“That ring is your proof that you’re a High Priest,” Amelia went on. “It also enables you to do various other things.” She was grinning behind the book.

Bal caught her meaning. He left his kit lying on the coverlet, waved to her, and went next door.

* * *

The priests’ room was a little smaller than the High Priest’s, and no less richly done. Two men waited in it, both somewhere in their twenties, in scarlet robes with white sashes tied around the waist. They had been sitting. Now they were up and staring at him, and then at the door he had come through, and then at him again. A stranger had just walked out of the High Priest’s bedroom, and he was a kid in a school sweater.

Bal lifted his hand and showed them the ring.

He watched it land. Both of them hurried over.

“High Priest!” said the one on the left, and then the one on the right, a heartbeat behind him. “We didn’t know you were coming.”

The one on the left introduced himself as Thad Moren. He was a slight man, pale, with long black hair tied back in a ponytail and a sharp nose. The other was Xavier Giles. He was big in the shoulders, tan, and the backs of his hands were crossed with old pale scars that looked to Bal like a blade’s work. He had clear blue eyes. They went over Bal once, quickly, and Bal had the feeling they did not miss much.

Both of them were surprised at his age. They did not hide it very well. But Thad said, and Xavier agreed, that if Lady Amelia had chosen him, that was good enough for them.

There was a table against the wall. Bal waved them over to it and they all sat.

He told them why they were here. He told them more than he would tell the commander, though not everything. He told them that the other originators had locked Amelia away over some insult they thought she’d done them, and that it had taken her all this time to break free.

“I want that story to get around,” he said. “The whole army. Make sure they know she didn’t abandon them by choice.”

Thad’s hands had gone flat on the table. Xavier said nothing. His eyes had fixed on a bare patch of wall and did not come off it.

Bal went on. The goal was to get everyone trained so they could go after Raizen, soon, because Raizen was already training his own army to come after them.

“The only thing helping us,” Bal said, “is that this temple is hidden. We’ve got surprise on our side. Raizen won’t expect us to have started gathering an army already.”

They went on a while about smaller matters. When they all got to their feet, Thad dug in the pocket of his robe and came out with a dark stone, flat and smooth and about the size of his palm.

Two runes were engraved into one face of it in white. One was the light rune. The other, worked in close beside it, was the rune for pair. Bal liked the cut of the lines.

Thad tossed it to him. Bal caught it against his chest.

“If we have to reach you,” Thad said, “we’ll signal you with that. It’ll glow a soft red until you tap it against the one we’ve got. If you want us, send your magyk into it. That sets it off.”

The back of it was blank. Bal slid it into his pocket.

“Thanks,” he said. “Go on and see to your duties.”

They both bowed a little and went out, already talking low to each other, off in search of the commander.

* * *

Amelia had not moved. She lay in the middle of the bed with her book held up over her face.

“You know,” she said, and turned another page, “I think she really does like you. She won’t come out and say it. Not yet, anyway. But you’ve impressed her.”

Bal’s head was still full of priests and armies and rune stones. It took him a second to find where she had gone.

“Oh,” he said. “You mean Viridia?”

He perched on the edge of the mattress, next to his kit. Impressed her. He went back over the day, trying to find the part that could have done it. Mr. Porter’s class, maybe, and Mr. Porter running out of words. Unless it was something else entirely, something he had said at lunch without noticing, and she had carried it home and turned it over. That was a worse idea, because then it could have been anything.

“Mm-hm,” said the book. “She does say some interesting things about you.”

Bal frowned at her. “Says? Says where?”

Amelia turned the book around and showed him the cover. Across the front, in neat ink, was a name he knew.

“You’re reading her diary?”

Amelia laughed at his face.

“What? I like knowing people’s secrets. What better way than reading someone’s diary?” She shut the book with a snap and rolled up onto her elbow. “Ooh. Do you keep a diary?”

“No. That’s never really been my thing.” Bal considered. “I think it’s mostly girls who do that.”

He thought a little more.

“Do you have a diary?”

Amelia went pink. She rolled onto her other side, so that he had nothing of her to look at but the back of her head.

“Maybe,” she said to the bed curtains. “You’ll never find it, though.”

Bal grinned at the back of her head. Well, that settled it. Now he had no choice. Somewhere, in some drawer or under some loose stone in the world, there was a book, and he was going to find it.

When Amelia rolled back, Viridia’s diary was no longer in her hand.

“We’d better go,” she said, “if you mean to meet Viridia for dinner. I’ll see you back at the dorm afterward.”

She reached for him. Bal raised a palm, and she stopped.

“You know you can eat with us, right?” he said. “Viridia likes you. So do I. There’s no reason for you to hide away.”

Amelia lowered her eyes to the white ring on his finger and kept them there.

“Thank you for asking.” Her voice had gone small. “Just give me a little time.”

Bal didn’t push. He gathered up the rune kit and laid his hand in hers.

“Let’s get back,” he said.

Somewhere ahead of him was a long table under the castle’s high ceiling, and a girl with pale blue hair cutting into her dinner. He had read none of her diary. Not one word. He would have to sit down opposite her anyway, knowing it was out there with his name in it, and what his face ought to do about that, he could not begin to guess.


CHAPTER TWELVE

I Like You Too

Lora was the first to see him come in.

She sat about halfway along the Waves table with a good length of empty bench to her left and her right. The rest of her table had bunched up at the window end, all talking over one another, and the gap between them and Lora was wide enough for three more people. When Bal came in, her head came up. She found him through the crowd and smiled, quick and shy, a smile that was gone nearly as soon as it started. Then she pushed her glasses back up her nose and returned to her food.

Bal smiled back. She didn’t see it.

*That’s someone else who needs a little time,* he thought.

He crossed to the last table, his own, as empty as ever except for one place across from his, where Viridia was working a knife through a steak.

He’d brought paper this time, a folded sheet in his pocket. He wrote out eggs, toast, bacon and oatmeal, breakfast at dinnertime, and dropped the sheet onto the silver. The silver went, and returned full and steaming.

Viridia eyed it. Then the window, where the sky had gone orange, then his plate again. She let it go.

The bench on his side was empty from end to end. Yesterday Amelia had appeared from nowhere in the place at his elbow and eaten off his plate. Tonight she was back in the dorm, or off in the mountains moving people into a canyon, and the place at his elbow stayed a plain stretch of wood. Whoever she was moving tonight had her mark on their palms and a crate or two of what they owned, and had left home because Amelia had a feeling about Raizen and did not mean to wait and learn whether she was right.

Viridia let him get started on his eggs first. Then she jerked her chin at Lora’s table.

“She saw you,” she said. “I went over and asked her again, before you got here. Whether she wanted to sit with us.”

“And?”

“She said no.” Viridia sawed off a corner of steak. “She was nice about it.”

“She’s really shy,” Bal said. “She’ll need a while to open up, that’s all.”

And there was the table itself. Nobody sat at it but Bal and one Tempus girl choosing to sit with him, and the whole hall could see it. Anyone who sat down here would be choosing a side. It would make her a target.

Bal stopped with a forkful of eggs in the air.

“The others haven’t tried anything with you,” he said, “have they?”

Viridia shrugged.

“Not really. A lot of snide comments, so far. Nothing physical.” She put a bite in her mouth and talked around it. “Some of the Tempus kids won’t talk to me anymore.” Her mouth curled up at one side. “It’s funny they think I care. Some people have been coming up and asking about you, though. Some from other sygls, even.”

Bal got his oatmeal down before he answered.

“Asking about me? Why?”

Viridia looked at him like he’d lost his mind.

“Bal, not only are you a High Priest, you’ve got your originator for a familiar. You can talk to her every day, any time you want. Nobody else has anything close to that. The most the rest of us get is once a year, when school starts.” She pointed her knife at him. “They want to know what it’s like.”

“Then why don’t they come and ask me?”

Viridia laughed at him.

“Because Amelia’s made it pretty obvious she favors you, and nobody wants to piss her off. And if they got too interested in you, other people might start treating them how my fellow Tempus students treat me.” She returned to her steak. “You’ll just have to give them time.”

It was hard to argue with her when she was right.

Over at the Terra table a big boy had balanced a bread roll on top of his water cup and was trying to balance a second roll on top of the first. Two of his friends were holding their breath for him. The second roll went into a girl’s soup, and the friends cheered anyway.

“I’m trying to get Amelia to start eating with us,” Bal said. “It’d be good for her. And if people saw her in here having dinner, it might take some of the mystery away. Oh, and so you’re not caught off guard. Amelia may or may not have read your diary this afternoon.”

Viridia had her cup up, in the middle of a long drink.

It went down wrong. She jerked the cup down so fast that half of it slopped over the rim and across her steak, and then she was coughing into her elbow, folded sideways on the bench with her free hand groping at the air. Bal passed her his napkin. She snatched it out of his fingers and coughed into that instead.

“She did *what*?” she managed, once the coughing let go of her. Her voice came out thin and wet.

Her face showed nerves and nothing else. Amelia hadn’t been teasing him on that bed. She had been telling the truth.

Bal’s face would not stay straight.

“Yeah,” he said. “I was off doing some priest stuff, and when I got back, she was lying there reading your diary.”

“How’d you know it was mine?”

The color was already rising in her cheeks.

“You’re really cute when you blush.” He meant every word of it, and it only turned her pinker. “Don’t worry. All I saw was your name on the front, and she told me what it was. Your secrets are safe with her.”

Viridia let out a small noise in her throat and went to work on the steak. She cut the piece in front of her in half, and then cut the halves smaller, and then smaller again, and didn’t eat any of it. The knife went on squeaking against the silver. She had made it down to crumbs and was still keeping her eyes on them.

He could have let it go there. He didn’t.

“And Viridia?”

He waited. It took her a while. Her eyes lifted off the plate, slowly, and found his.

“I like you too,” he said.

Her fork dropped.

It hit the edge of the plate, rang off the silver, and went over the side of the table. Bal heard it land on the stone under the bench, two little bounces and then nothing. Viridia’s hand stayed in the air where the fork had been.

“But you,” she began. “But you *said* she only told you it was my diary!” She said it so loud the Waves boy two tables down glanced over.

Bal took the hand that had dropped the fork.

“Hey,” he said. “It’s okay. That honestly is all she told me. I promise. And I’m glad she did, because otherwise I might never have worked up the courage to tell you I like you.”

Viridia wiped her eyes with her other hand.

“That was still mean.”

“I’ll find some way to make it up to you,” he promised.

“You better,” she muttered.

She kept her hand where it was, inside his, on the table between the oatmeal and the ruined steak, and neither of them went under the bench after the fork.

* * *

The hall cleared a table at a time. Outside the high windows the orange drained from the sky, and the lamps along the walls were lit. Benches scraped. Plates vanished in front of people as they stood. The Terra boys left in a crowd, one of them with a bread roll in each pocket. At some point Lora’s bench emptied too, and Bal never saw Lora go. Viridia talked about nothing much, her spear partner’s shins, the steak, and he answered her, and afterward not one word of it stayed with him.

He walked back to the dorm with his right hand still warm. He could have taken the stairs three at a time. Maybe he did. He didn’t remember them. He remembered lying down in the dark, and not much after that.

It was barely light when something started pounding on the wall of his dorm.


CHAPTER THIRTEEN

New Spring

The whole wall shook with it. In the next room the fire irons chattered in their stand, and grit pattered down on the canopy.

Bal sat up with his heart going.

Amelia hadn’t moved. She was curled on her side next to him with her black hair spread over two pillows and one hand tucked under her cheek. When she had come in, he could not say. The embroidered rose was bunched up under her chin.

The pounding came again. It was not a polite sort of knock. Somebody had made a fist and meant it.

*Please don’t wake up,* Bal thought at her, and eased out from under the blanket by inches. The floor was cold. He got the door shut behind him without a click and hurried down the steps and put his hand flat to the wall at the bottom. It slid aside.

Viridia stood in the corridor with her fist still up. Lora was behind her, half a step back.

They were not in uniform. Viridia had on a blue skirt that had been let down at the hem at least once, and a laced vest over a white blouse. Lora wore brown trousers and a jacket with patches at both elbows, and her hair was loose for once, down past her shoulders. Neither said a thing. They stared at him, the pair of them, and their faces were going a slow and steady pink.

Bal followed their eyes down.

Boxers. He was in his boxers. Only his boxers, and the corridor was cold too.

“Come in,” he said, very fast, and waved them up the steps past him. “Sit down. Anywhere. I’ll be right back.”

He made it into the bedroom before either of them reached the top. Amelia was still asleep. He found his uniform where he had left it over the chest and got into it the quickest he had ever gotten into anything, the sweater on backward the first time, and reappeared with his hair still flattened from the pillow.

They were standing at the hearth, still pink.

“Sorry,” Bal said. “About how I answered the door. I wasn’t really awake yet.”

They all started in at once. Bal stopped, put up a hand, and waved for them to go first.

“Pretty sure nobody’s allowed in other people’s dorms,” Viridia said, turning slowly to take in the room. “It must get lonely in here. With it only being you.”

“Yeah. It’s pretty big,” Bal said. He caught her eye. “It’d be nice to have other people around.”

Viridia found something on the mantel that needed her full attention.

“So what’s all this?” he asked, and pointed at their clothes.

* * *

“Well, the thing is,” Lora said, “all the teachers got pulled by the originators today.”

She tucked her hair behind her ear, and then her hand stayed up by her ear, as if it was not sure where else to go. He guessed she was not used to being part of a group.

“Apparently it happens sometimes,” she went on. “When it does, they give us a free day. Viridia asked me at breakfast whether I wanted to walk down to the village with her, and I said yes.” Her eyes flicked to Bal, there and gone, asking whether that was all right.

He smiled at her. Her hand came down from her ear.

“And then she said we should drag you along,” Lora finished.

Bal glanced toward his room. One originator, at least, had not pulled anybody anywhere this morning. When he left her she had been asleep with her mouth a little open.

“I wouldn’t mind going,” he said, “as long as the two of you are with me. Let me go get changed.”

Viridia looked pointedly at the uniform he had barely finished putting on. He went anyway.

In the bedroom he got out of the uniform a good deal more slowly than he had gotten into it, and into his own trousers and an old shirt from Saint Andre’s, washed thin. He dug his backpack out of the chest, the same one he had carried off the orphanage roof, and emptied it.

Amelia could find him anywhere she liked. A note was pointless, really.

He tore a strip off the back of yesterday’s rune notes and wrote on it with the stub of pencil from his kit.

*Gone to the village with Viridia and Lora. Back before curfew. Bal*

He read it over. It did not say much. He wanted her to hear it from him anyway, and not have to go looking. He folded the strip once and stood it up on the pillow beside her hand, and let himself out, easing the door shut on her.

* * *

They were not the only ones on the road. Students in their own clothes were strung out along it in twos and threes, and once a knot of them went past at a jog, as though New Spring might close if they did not hurry.

“Were either of you going to the village for something in particular?” Bal asked. “Or was it to get out of the castle?”

“Shops.” Viridia patted a pocket of her vest, which clinked. “I brought some money in case I see something I want.”

“What about you?”

Lora jumped. She had been walking on his other side, silent, for most of a mile, and her face said she had planned to go on that way the whole five miles.

“Oh. Um.” She pushed her hands into her jacket pockets. “I’m hoping to look at the job and bounty boards. There’s a faction I’ve been reading about. It’s more of a guild, really. It only takes you once you’ve finished a certain number of requests. It’s a good way to make money, but that isn’t really the point.” She was speeding up. “The point is, the guild has spellbooks you can’t find anywhere else. Some of them you’re not even allowed to see until you reach a certain rank. Can you imagine?”

Her hands had come out of her pockets partway through. She had not said so many words in a row since he met her, and she had not stumbled over a single one of them.

He caught Viridia’s eye over Lora’s head. Viridia’s eyebrows went up and stayed up.

“Would you have to take the requests on your own?” Bal asked.

“No. You can register with the local Dawn faction as a team. They give every member a wooden card with the team’s name on it. Each time you turn in a job or a bounty, the number on your cards goes up.”

Bal looked over at Viridia. She mouthed *yes* at him and did not bother to make it subtle.

“Okay,” Bal said. “We’ll stop by there, so you can join if you want.”

“Oh! No, I’m not sure I’m ready,” Lora said quickly. “I need to study more.”

Bal laughed. “It’s fine. We’ll stop by anyway.”

The road was long and then it kept on being long. A farmer’s cart passed them with a dog asleep on a load of turnips. When the first roofs showed through the trees ahead, the sun was high, and breakfast felt like yesterday.

* * *

The village was called New Spring. The road brought them straight into its square. Shops stood on every side of it, with stalls set up in front of them on the cobbles. Most of the shops had signs hanging out over their doors that showed what was sold inside, a boot or a loaf or a pair of scissors. The ones without a sign had big glass windows instead. The square smelled of woodsmoke and horses and frying onions.

Bal followed the onions.

A man at a cart was selling hot rolls with meat baked inside, pulling them off a griddle with his bare fingers and shaking his hand after every one. Bal bought three. He handed one to Viridia and held one out to Lora.

“Oh. I can buy my own,” Lora said.

“I already paid for it.” Bal kept holding it out. “Why waste it?”

Lora looked at the roll, and then at him, and could not find the hole in that. She took it.

Viridia had no such scruples. She had already eaten a third of hers and was walking on.

The roll was the best thing Bal had eaten in his life, or at least that morning. The meat inside was peppery and too hot, and juice ran to his wrist.

In the far corner of the square a crowd had gathered, and the crowd was going *ooh*.

A vendor stood on an upturned crate behind a table of weapons laid out on a red cloth. He held a longsword above his head. As they watched, he gave the hilt a twist, and the blade burst into flame from guard to point. The fire ran up the steel with a soft, hungry sound, and the heat of it reached Bal’s face from three rows back.

“Enchanted!” the vendor told them. “Never needs a match! And for those of you who prefer a little more snap, I’ve got some here that throw lightning!”

Bal edged up to the table and found the price tag tied to the nearest hilt. He read it and winced.

The flame climbed the blade once more. A sword that made fire was only a sword with something written on it. He was fairly sure he knew how to write. It would take some experimenting.

“Bal,” Viridia said. “Come on.”

* * *

They wandered.

Viridia stopped at nearly every window with anything bright in it. At one she lingered over a silver pin shaped like a leaf, and then left it. Lora walked beside Bal.

Partway around the square Viridia halted at a shop with a painted sign of a cat curled up inside a bird cage.

“Wait here,” she said. “I’ll only be a minute.” She ducked in under the sign. A bell went somewhere inside.

Bal leaned on the post outside. Lora stood next to him.

“Are you having fun?” he asked.

“Yes,” Lora said, and sounded surprised to hear it. “I am. You and Viridia are surprisingly easy to be around.” She addressed the next part to the shop window, where a ferret in a basket was asleep on its back. “If you couldn’t tell, I don’t really do well with other people.”

“That’s okay,” Bal said. “Not everyone does. I grew up in an orphanage. You get used to meeting new people there.”

Lora gave him a sideways look over her glasses.

“So it doesn’t bother you? That I’m shy?”

“Not at all,” Bal said. “You are who you are, and I accept that.”

Lora smiled. It was a real one, slow and wide, and it went up into her freckles.

“You really are beautiful when you smile,” Bal told her.

Lora spun around and put her face into the corner where the post met the wall, and kept it hidden there.

Viridia had come out of the shop in time to hear it. She stood on the step with a cloth bag in her hand and smirked at Bal.

He shrugged. “I only tell the truth,” he said. “What’d you buy?”

Viridia opened the bag and drew out a collar.

It was wide dark leather, big enough to fit a pony. She turned it inside out. A rune had been stitched into the lining in silver thread, small and tight and very neat.

“It’s a size adjustment rune. The man said it’ll shrink Hopper down to about the size of a dog. If I want to change her back, I push my magyk into the collar, and she’s big again.” She ran her thumb along the stitches. “That way she can come into the castle with me. If she wants to.”

Bal leaned in to see the rune. Every corner of it was where it ought to be.

“That’s a really thoughtful gift,” he said.

Viridia tucked the collar away in its bag and swung the bag on its string.

* * *

Two shops along, while the girls were bent over a tray of hair combs in another window, Bal spotted the ring.

It sat on its own in a little velvet box at the front of a jeweler’s display, a plain band of bright silver. A hand-lettered card beside it said the ring let its wearer move and shape the earth, briefly. The one drawback was that it had to be recharged after every use, and recharging it took a good deal of magykal energy.

Bal read the card twice. Still. One use was enough, if nobody saw it coming.

He checked the price. Then he checked where the girls were. Lora was holding a comb up to the light and Viridia was explaining to her why it was ugly.

He was in and out of the jeweler’s in under a minute. The ring went into the right-hand pocket of his trousers, and when Viridia turned around he was back on the same cobble.

The bookstore was the last shop before the road out. A metal bell on a spring dinged over the door as they came in, and a stout woman behind the counter looked up from her ledger and told them to let her know if they needed anything.

Bal stood in the first aisle and argued with himself. He had been planning to look for books on strategy, and on the wars the originators’ armies had fought. But the school had a whole library full of exactly that, two stories of it, and a librarian who was already setting things aside for him. Then he thought of the library in his dorm, and its long bare shelves. That room was going to waste.

He went to find the history section.

Lora and Viridia drifted off together toward the back, talking low. Bal took his time. After fifteen minutes he had four books, big ones, one each on the battles of Aren’s, Kenmei’s, Naisura’s and Raizen’s armies. On his way to the counter he passed a shelf of alchemy, and a book on it nearly as thick as it was wide, bound in green cloth with brass corners. He thought of Lora, and took it down.

At the counter he paid for all five. The stout woman turned Raizen’s history over to find the price and told her ledger that he was the third person this month to want that one. For years before that, nobody had asked. Bal answered that it was for school, which was nearly true, and she wrote it down. The alchemy book went straight into his backpack before anyone saw. He carried the four histories stacked in his arms and went to find the girls.

* * *

He heard them before he reached them. They were one aisle over, behind a tall shelf of advanced spellbooks, and they were talking about him.

Bal stopped where he was.

“You don’t think anything’s going on between Amelia and him?” Lora asked.

“Who knows? But from that ‘you can keep her’ thing she said, when we all ate together, I’d say she’s not the jealous type.”

“I guess.”

A page turned. Then another. Bal stood very still, with the four books getting heavier in his arms.

“Do you mind, though?” Lora asked.

It was a while before Viridia answered. Long enough that Bal thought she might not.

“Honestly?” she said. “No. Where I grew up, your whole life was pretty much planned out for you the day you were born. Who you’d marry, even. Then I got my Tempus sygl.” Bal heard her breathe out. “It was like I’d finally been set free. Hardly anyone in my village ever got a sygl. It had been decided, way back, that if anyone ever did, they’d be allowed to leave. No matter what.” She was quiet again. “Joking around with Bal. Playing around. I can’t really describe how that makes me feel.”

Nobody spoke. Bal’s eyes were on the spine of a book in front of him. *Wards and Their Failings.* He did not take in a word of it.

“What about you, though? He did call you beautiful.”

A book shut with a snap, and slid back into its place on the shelf.

“I was teased a lot, growing up,” Lora said quietly.

Bal shifted his grip on the histories and held his breath.

“My father and our weapons master both think I’m a prodigy with a rapier. When I’ve got one in my hand, it’s honestly an extension of who I am.” Her voice was steadier than it ever was in company. “Most girls wanted to play with dolls, or dress up. I loved my books and my rapier. Our town held tournaments now and then, to show off everybody’s martial skills. Once I was old enough, my father begged me to enter. Girls weren’t forbidden to. It was more of an oversight than anything. No girl had ever tried.”

“So you did.”

“So I did. Not a single other boy had ever seen me train. Neither had their fathers. They’d only heard about me secondhand. That was part of why they mocked me. They couldn’t believe a girl could be that good. Especially a girl of thirteen.” Neither girl was turning pages now. “War was a man’s game, they kept telling me.”

“So what happened?” Viridia asked.

Lora was smiling. He heard it in her voice, plain as anything.

“I beat them,” she said. “Every single one.”

A small, delighted noise came from Viridia.

“After that, the comments got worse. And the petty things. So I mostly stayed home. By day I trained, and by night I read. That’s been my life for the last five years.” Lora paused. “So it’s taking some getting used to. Someone actually wanting to be around me.”

* * *

Bal backed up.

He went quietly back down the aisle and around its end. When he judged he was far enough off, he let the top history slide off the stack.

It hit the floorboards flat, with a bang that rattled the stout woman’s teacup in its saucer.

Lora’s head came around the end of the spellbook shelf. She saw him crouched there reaching for the book, and smiled.

Bal looked up and gave her his most sheepish face.

“Sorry if I startled you.”

“It’s okay,” Lora said. “We were only talking. Find everything you needed?”

“Yep.” Bal stood with the stack. “Also, there’s a surprise for you at the dorm.”

Lora’s eyes went over his four books, spine by spine. None of them was the surprise. Bal was very glad, right then, that the green book was sitting in the bottom of his backpack.

Viridia came around the shelf after her.

“Get everything?” she asked, eyeing the histories.

“Yep. But let’s head over to the Dawn faction as soon as Lora’s bought her book.” He hitched the stack up. “I’m getting hungry again.”

Lora had chosen a thick brown volume with a broken column stamped on the cover. She paid for it, and while the two of them headed for the door Bal swung the backpack off his shoulder and fed the histories in on top of the green one.

Just inside the door he stopped Lora.

“Here,” he said. “Let me put your book in my bag.” He turned to Viridia. “Your collar too. There’s no point in both of you carrying things when I’ve got this.”

Viridia handed the collar over gladly. Lora hesitated, with her new book held against her front.

Viridia gave her a light elbow.

“Come on,” she whispered, loud enough for the whole shop. “It makes him feel useful.”

Lora laughed. It was a bright, sudden sound, and it bounced off the shelves and made the stout woman look up. She held out the book.

Bal took it. As he did, he looked her in the eye.

“You don’t have to be afraid of me,” he said. “I promise. I’m not like anyone else you’ve known.”

Then he pushed the door open, and the bell dinged, and he held it until the girls were through before he followed.

* * *

The Dawn Hall had its name in gold letters on a large and very ornate sign over a three-story wooden building at the top of the square. A deep covered porch kept the rain off anyone waiting. Inside were tables, where people sat talking over mugs and maps, and a long counter down the whole right-hand side.

Two women stood behind the counter in matching gray jackets with a sunrise stitched on the breast.

“Welcome to the Dawn Hall,” said the woman on the right as they came up. “My name is Soren, and this is Kim. What can we do for you today?”

Kim lifted a hand to them and went on sorting a box of papers.

Lora stepped forward before Bal could speak.

“Do you have any jobs posted?”

“We do.” Soren took her time looking at the three of them. “But I haven’t seen you in here before, so I have to ask. Are you a Dawn member?”

Lora’s face fell.

“I thought you didn’t have to be a member to take certain jobs,” she said. “Like going out to collect herbs. That sort of thing.”

“That’s true,” Soren said. “But why would you not join first, if that’s your aim? I can promise you there are benefits to being a member.”

Lora’s head snapped up.

“Everyone I’ve talked to says you have requirements before you can join.”

“As an individual member, that’s true. We want our members to understand what they’re getting into when they join the guild.” Soren folded her hands on the counter. “For a team it’s different. All we ask of a team is one job, taken right away when you join. Then the usual, one job or one bounty every week. That’s not counting the guild dues every month, but we can take those out of your reward money if you’d prefer.”

At the word *team*, Lora’s head went down again, and stayed down.

Bal glanced at Viridia. She jerked her head at the counter. *Go.*

“I would like to enroll as a team.” Bal stepped up to the counter.

Lora started to speak. Viridia put a hand on her arm and shushed her.

Soren’s gaze went from one girl to the other and back. Then she gave Bal her attention.

“Very well,” she said. “I take it these two will be the members of your party?”

Bal turned around. Lora and Viridia had their heads together and were arguing in furious whispers. Lora kept huffing through her nose.

“Yep,” Bal said to Soren. “They are.”

Soren’s mouth twitched. She reached under the counter and came up with three thin wooden cards, pale, each no bigger than Bal’s palm.

“And the name you’d like for your team?” she asked.

* * *

Bal went over to the girls.

“It’s okay, Lora. You want this,” he said. “This way we can have your back when you go out on a job. Joining the Dawn is something you’ve wanted. Why are you fighting it?”

“Because I don’t want you both to have to join,” Lora said, “only because I can’t get in on my own right now.”

Viridia groaned at the rafters.

“That’s what friends are for,” she said, and raised a finger before Lora could start again. “You *are* our friend, right?”

“Yes,” Lora said to the floor, in a very small voice.

“Then we’re doing this.” She swung toward Bal. “What did she say?”

Bal stayed with Lora.

“It really is okay,” he told her. “I think it’ll be fun. Besides, it gives me an excuse to hang out with both of you more.”

Lora blushed. Bal tried Viridia instead.

“She wants a name for our team. I figured that was something we should decide together.”

“Agreed.” Viridia faced Lora. “Well? Any ideas?”

Lora saw she was not going to change either of their minds, and gave in.

“I like ‘The Heirs,’” she said. “But that seems kind of boring. I only thought of it because we’re all different sygls.”

“It’s not bad, though.” Viridia tapped her chin. “What about…” She stopped mid-tap. “Sygls Best.”

Lora and Bal both stared.

“I mean, if we’re going to do this, we might as well aim for the top.”

Lora grinned. “I like it,” she said, and meant it.

“Bal?” Viridia prompted.

“I think we’ve got our new team name.”

On his way past her to the counter he reached out and gave Viridia’s hand a quick squeeze.

“You did good,” he said, low, and kept walking.

Soren was waiting with the cards.

“Have you decided?”

“We have,” Bal said. “The consensus is Sygls Best.”

Soren took up what Bal took for an ordinary pencil and wrote *Sygls Best* on the face of the first card, in an even, flowing hand. Where the point passed, the letters came up white and sank into the wood, clean and bright. She did the second card, and the third.

Bal glanced back. The girls had taken one of the tables and were sitting with their heads close, talking quietly.

“Soren, can I ask you something?” he said. “There’s someone else I want to invite to the group. She isn’t here right now. Could you make a card for her too?”

“Of course. I’ll need her name.” Soren reached under the counter for a fourth card. “Once you give it to her, tell her to push her magyk into it. That will attune it to her.”

She lettered the team name on it. Then she flipped the card face down on the counter, and waited with the pencil over the bare wood.

“What’s her name?”

Bal froze.

If he said Amelia, Soren would put it together in about a second. There was exactly one Amelia anyone had been talking about this week, and she was his familiar and a goddess. But Amelia was all he had. He had never heard her last name.

He hoped very hard that she would not mind.

“Amelia Norheart,” he said.

Soren’s eyebrow went up. Norheart was his own last name, and plainly she knew it. She considered him over the pencil. Bal held her gaze.

Not a word. She wrote it.

*Amelia Norheart.* The letters went white and settled into the grain, one after another, as if the wood had been waiting for her.

Bal slid the card into the left-hand pocket of his trousers, away from the ring, and carried the other three to the table.

* * *

He did his own card first, to show them how it worked. Viridia pushed her magyk into hers and it warmed in her palm, and her name came up white on the back, in the same flowing hand.

Lora’s took a second longer. When *Lora Aegis* showed white on the back, she beamed.

“I can’t believe it,” she said. “I’ve wanted this for *so* long.”

“Well, if you want to keep it,” Bal told her, “you’d better go pick us a job. Remember, Soren said we have to take one the day we become official.”

“Right!”

Lora was gone, hurrying across to the counter with her card held in both hands. Soren drew a thick stack of papers out from underneath, and the two of them bent their heads over it.

Bal dropped into the chair Lora had left. It was still warm.

“I like seeing her excited like this,” he said. “It must have been tough where she grew up.”

“It was.” Viridia let it rest at that, and he did not ask. “I’m glad we did this. We’re going to have to finish it fast, though. Free day or not, there’s still a curfew.”

“I’ll get you both back in time,” Bal said. “If we have to, I’ll walk you back myself and come down and turn the job in on my own.”

Viridia laughed out loud.

“You do realize I said *we* have a curfew? That includes you.”

“Actually,” Bal said, “I don’t have one. I’m Lead Mage of the Blood sygl.”

“But how can you be…” Viridia started, and stopped. “Of course. You’re Lead Mage because you’re literally the *only* mage.”

She put her forehead down on the table.

“I wonder if anyone else has figured that out,” she said to the tabletop. “All the other Lead Mages are fourth years. And here you are, the first Lead Mage in history that’s a first year.” She sat up again. “Ours is a jerk. He doesn’t allow loud talking in the common room. No kissing. And he actually tries to make us keep a study time.”

Bal heard very little after the second one.

“No kissing? What does he do if he catches you?”

“Five demerits. And five automatically means he gets to make you do any chore he wants. Clean the bathrooms. Muck out the stables. Anything, really.”

Bal raised his right palm, solemn as an oath.

“I promise you can kiss anytime you want in my dorm,” he told her. Then, with a smirk, he added, “As long as it’s me.”

Something mischievous got into Viridia’s face.

“What if I wanted to kiss Amelia instead?”

Bal had no answer.

Lora saved him, bounding over with a sheet of paper. She saw Bal’s face and looked to Viridia for an explanation.

“Later.” Viridia pointed at the paper.

* * *

“Okay,” Lora said, perching on the third chair. “They know we go to the school, so we don’t actually have to do this today. But it has to be done and turned in by the end of the weekend.”

Bal got his mouth working again. “What’s the job?”

“There are some rare flowers growing about a day’s walk from here. The local alchemist needs three of them.” Lora turned the paper his way. *Aurora felis (3)*, it said at the top, in a cramped hand. “To get to them you have to go through some deep woods, and the alchemist is an older lady. She doesn’t feel well enough to go out and get them herself. Soren says there might be wolves, and bears, and that sort of thing. But I told her we could handle it.”

All her fluency drained out of her at once.

“Is it okay?” she asked. “What do you two say?”

“You picked a good one. I’m glad we can help her out,” Bal said.

Lora folded the paper in half and handed it to him.

Bal took it, surprised. Lora saw his face.

“You’re the leader,” she said. “Without you, I’d be on my own.”

Bal checked with Viridia.

“She’s right. If it wasn’t for you, I’d never have thought about joining the Dawn. I might not have met Lora.” She smiled at Lora, and Lora, shyly, smiled back. “So I agree. You’re the leader.”

Bal tucked the job into the backpack, in between Lora’s present and Viridia’s collar.

“If you say so,” he said. “But like Viridia said, we’ve got to be home before curfew. Is there anything else either of you wants to do before we head back?”

Neither of them did.

“I think we’re good,” Viridia told him.

They went out past Kim and her box of papers, into the square. The afternoon had gone gold while they were inside, and the man with the meat rolls was scraping his empty griddle.

The road home was the same five miles it had been that morning, and the pack was heavier now, with six books and a collar in it.

Bal walked a little behind the two of them. His hand had found its way into his left pocket. It closed on the corner of the card, and the whole way out of New Spring the letters were there under his thumb, smooth in the grain. He did not know what she would say. He did not know whether she had ever wanted a last name, or what she would do with his.


CHAPTER FOURTEEN

The Card

“They put the dead in the walls,” Lora was saying. “Not in a graveyard. In the walls of their own houses. So your grandmother was always right there behind the plaster while you ate your supper.”

“That’s horrible,” Viridia said.

“It’s not, though. Not to them. The book says they’d knock on the wall before a big decision. To ask.”

“Did anybody ever knock back?”

Lora started to say no, and stopped, and considered it, and the sun had gone another finger lower over the trees before she had finished telling Viridia every reason the answer was complicated.

Bal kept a few steps back and let them get on with it.

She was talking from the book she was partway through back at school, not the new brown one riding in his pack. It was about a people who had been gone so long that nobody agreed on what they had called themselves. Lora knew where they had built their roads and what they had eaten and how they had counted their years, and she was telling all of it to the back of Viridia’s head and the side of Viridia’s face.

Bal would have bet his four new histories that Viridia couldn’t have told an aqueduct from a well. She asked anyway. She asked who had dug the wells, and whether the dead in the walls had minded the damp, and once she stopped dead in the road to argue that anybody who built a city on a floodplain deserved what they got. Lora argued back. Nobody was being polite.

Bal remembered what Lora had said about the boys in her town, the tournament, the comments after. Somebody had looked at this girl and decided she was a thing to laugh at. Now she was walking backward in the road ahead of him to keep Viridia’s face in view while she explained about the floods, and he decided that whoever they were, they were not getting another turn at her.

Lora had reached the part with the earthquake. Viridia asked which came first, the earthquake or the floods, and Lora said that was the right question, and Viridia was so busy being right that she walked into a puddle.

The last mile was uphill. Bal’s feet hurt and the meat rolls were miles behind him and nobody said much on that stretch. On the climb his mind wandered off to yesterday’s hill without asking him, to the cold wind and the gray tents and the soldiers wheeling in their blocks below. It would not sit beside grandmothers in the plaster and a girl who had walked into a puddle for being right. The two refused to belong to the same week, and they were in it anyway. Near the top Lora remembered one more thing about the earthquake, and they were off again, clear to the castle gate and in under it and up the stairs.

It was nearly two hours after leaving New Spring that they came down the long gallery to the gray pedestal where it ended.

“Watch this,” Bal said.

He set his palm on the carving. The little prick came, and the stone drank, and his blood threaded through the grooves till every line of the carving ran red, then sank out of sight. Lora bent so close to watch it that her glasses nearly touched the stone. Viridia wrinkled her nose at the whole business, and then the wall ground aside, and she went up the steps first anyway.

The room upstairs had changed while they were out. There were two couches around the fireplace now, the old red one and a second one facing it, a darker red, with a second low table in front of it.

Amelia had the old couch to herself, a book open on her knees. It was a small book, bound in blue, with Viridia’s name inked neatly on the cover, and Bal had last seen it the day before, held over her face on the High Priest’s bed.

She looked up at the sound of the wall. Her gaze slid past Bal to Viridia, and she jumped.

The book was gone. Her knees were bare of it, and her hands were empty, and she was off the couch and across the rug and had her arms around Bal’s neck before he had got the strap of his pack off one shoulder. She smelled of the fire. She hugged him hard and let him go and went straight back to her place.

“Well, you’ve all been gone all day,” she said. “Did you have fun?”

Bal decided the diary was a conversation for later, with its author out of the room.

Behind him the girls had not moved off the top step. Lora was half behind Viridia. Viridia got hold of her by the wrist and towed her in off the step.

“Amelia, this is Lora,” she said. “Lora Aegis. Lora, Amelia.”

Amelia raised her eyebrows at Bal, a question with no words in it. Bal shrugged at her, sheepishly, and that was enough for her to laugh.

“Well, you already know me,” she said to Lora. “How are you liking it, being around Bal?”

Lora said, “Oh,” and nothing after it.

All the talk from the road was gone out of her. She stood on the rug with her hands pressed flat to the sides of her trousers, plainly at a loss whether she was supposed to bow, or kneel, or what a person did with a goddess who had asked her a friendly question.

* * *

Bal dropped the pack on the rug and sat beside Amelia.

“Look,” he said, to both the girls. “I’m about to tell you something. But it has to stay between us for now. Okay?”

They gave him the same puzzled look, the pair of them, and said okay.

Amelia drew a breath to speak. Bal raised two fingers off his knee, and she let the breath go. He laid his hand over hers instead and kept it there on the couch between them.

“Amelia’s family betrayed her,” he said. “They thought she did something she didn’t do. That’s why she was locked away for close to three thousand years. When I bonded her as my familiar, it was a kind of loophole. It let her get out. I’ve offered to release her from the bond since, and she doesn’t want me to.”

Lora had ended up on the edge of the new couch. Viridia stayed standing behind her.

“Her life hasn’t been easy,” Bal went on. “None of ours have, really. But because she’s an originator, it’s hard for her to make friends who aren’t only there because they want something from her. So please. Treat her like you would anyone else. Let her get to know you.” He turned to Amelia. “I know it’s something you want and have no idea how to ask for. I’m right, aren’t I?”

Amelia had closed her eyes.

One tear got out and hung on her lashes, red in the firelight, and Bal brushed it away with the side of his thumb before it could fall. His other hand stayed on hers.

“She was alone all that time,” he said. “She needs friends. So do I.”

Lora’s mouth opened. Then it closed. Her fingers were knotted in her lap, and she was working at something, and Bal waited, and let her have all the time she needed.

“I need friends too,” she said at last.

She said it to her knees, so softly it nearly went into the fire.

* * *

Bal pulled the pack to him and opened it.

The histories came out first, the four of them, and went in a stack on the new table. He fished out Viridia’s cloth bag and passed it up to her where she stood, and found Lora’s brown book with the broken column on it and put it in Lora’s lap. The folded job paper could stay put. That left one gift in the bottom of the bag, and he let it wait.

“And you know what friends do?” he asked Lora.

Lora waited.

Bal grinned. “They give gifts?”

He lifted out the last book, the green one with the brass corners, and offered it to her.

Lora’s hands came up for it before the rest of her had caught on. It was heavy enough that it dragged her wrists down into her lap, on top of the brown one.

“But when did you…” she began, and stopped, because her own book was right there underneath it and this was not her book.

She opened the cover. She turned pages, quickly, then more slowly, then quickly again, checking it was alchemy to the last page. Her eyes had gone bright with unshed tears behind her glasses. Then she closed it and hugged it flat against her chest with both arms.

“Thank you,” she said.

She watched him over its brass corners, in case he had anything else in there.

Bal dug in his right pocket.

“Viridia. Give me your hand,” he said. “The left one. And close your eyes.”

Viridia rounded the couch with a look that promised trouble if this was a trick, and dropped down by Lora, and put out her hand, and did as she was told.

The ring went onto her middle finger.

“Oooh,” said Lora, very softly.

Viridia stared down at the plain bright band on her hand, speechless.

“I know it doesn’t look like much,” Bal said quickly. It did not. It could have been any bit of silver bought for a few coins. “But it should be plenty powerful.” The jeweler’s card propped beside it in the window, he explained, had promised it could shift and shape the ground for a short while, and warned that it needed a costly recharge after each use. “So you’ll want to save it. For when it counts.”

“It’s perfect,” she said. “Thank you.”

She tilted the ring to the fire, so the light ran around it, and went on admiring it, and was not listening to anybody.

* * *

That left Amelia.

She was sitting very straight at her own end of the cushions, her hands clasped hard on her knees. Bal had seen her nervous only once before, in the doorway at Verin’s cottage, working up to a question she was afraid to ask.

“Amelia. I’m sorry I didn’t buy you anything.”

Something went across her face, quick and sad, and was gone. He nearly missed it.

“But I hope you like it anyway,” he said.

Now she only looked confused.

From the other pocket he drew out the card. It was warm from his hand. He had rubbed at the corner on the walk home, without meaning to, and he gave it to her face up, so that what she saw first was the pale wood and *Sygls Best* in white across it.

“Turn it over,” he said quietly.

Amelia flipped the card.

Then she went still. The fire went on popping. Lora breathed. Viridia’s ring caught the light and lost it. Amelia held the card, and nothing in her stirred, not her lashes and not her breath, as if the hearth had always had a statue of a girl beside it and nobody had noticed until now. Under her thumbs the white letters lay in the grain. *Amelia Norheart.*

Then her eyes closed. She tipped sideways against him, slowly, and her shoulders started to shake.

She did not make much noise. She cried with the card held tight to her chest in both fists and her face pressed into his shirt, and Bal put his arms around her and held on.

“You belong here,” he whispered into her hair, by her ear. “With us.”

Across the hearth both girls were staring. Neither of them could have seen what was written on the card. Viridia’s hand with the ring on it hung in the air halfway to her lap. They knew enough.

Bal rubbed her back while she cried, until the shaking eased and then stopped.

She drew back. Then she kissed him, very softly, on the mouth.

“You are a good man,” she told him.

She settled in again beside him, and then scooted closer, until there was no couch left between them.

“I’m sorry about your shirt,” she said.

“My shirt?”

Bal looked down.

It was the Saint Andre’s shirt, the one washed so thin the elbows let daylight through. All down the front of it, where her face had been, it was soaked through dark red, a great wet patch of it over his heart. Anyone who came in now would have thought him stabbed.

“It was worth it,” Bal said.

* * *

They stayed up talking until late. Viridia asked whether the second couch had been there that morning, and Amelia said the dorm had made it, and Viridia said the dorm had terrible taste, and Amelia agreed with her. Lora found the chapter on poultices and read the first line of it out loud, and then the second, and then had to be stopped. Nobody brought up the card. Amelia stayed tucked against Bal’s side through all of it, and now and then her hand went to the pocket of her dress, where she had put it, and rested there.

Then the curfew bell went, loud enough to be heard through every wall in the castle, and the girls had to go.

Bal walked them down the steps. At the bottom he hugged Lora, and then Viridia, and both of them thanked him again, Lora into his collar and Viridia against his ear, and he kept the red stain on his front clear of both of them. He stood and watched them go along the painted corridor together, Viridia with her ringed hand held out in front of her and Lora carrying her two books, until they turned the corner. Then the stone ground shut.

Upstairs the room was empty. The fire had burned down. The histories were stacked on the new table, and the pack lay open on the rug with the job paper still folded in the bottom of it.

He went through to the bedroom.

Amelia was already there, sitting up against the pillows with her knees drawn up. The card was on the nightstand on her side, propped against the lamp with the name facing her, and his note from that morning folded beside it.

“Come on.” She patted the bed beside her. “It’s time for bed.”

Bal pulled the ruined shirt off over his head. For the second time that day he was down to his boxers, and now his own face went pink. He climbed in beside his beautiful Vampire Goddess, and she pulled the blanket up over them both.


CHAPTER FIFTEEN

Rumors

“I’ll be gone all day,” Amelia said.

The room was still gray. She sat on his side of the bed, dressed already, with a hand resting on the blanket over his chest. Behind her the card was still propped by the lamp on the nightstand, where she had stood it last night, the name turned toward her pillow.

“Let me know if you need anything,” she said.

She bent and kissed him on the mouth, without any hurry about it, and he left his eyes shut afterward to make it last. When he opened them the hand was gone off the blanket. Her side of the bed was smooth.

The card hadn’t moved.

* * *

Every teacher in the castle had come to one conclusion overnight. Yesterday had been a free day, and today was going to pay for it.

Mr. Porter set the class an essay on the duties of a familiar before he had sat down, and watched Bal the whole time they wrote it. At the bell he bent and said something low to Huginn, and the raven left by the open top of the window without its cheese and flew off over the forest, steady and straight, until Bal lost it against the trees. Mr. Winters filled his chalkboard, wiped it, and filled it again. In Warfare the instructor ran them through forms in the sand until nobody had any breath left to spend on talking. Lora, three places down the line, watched Bal with the point of her rapier dead level from the first form to the last. Even in Crafting the only sounds were files on metal and somebody’s stool creaking.

Nobody chatted. Nobody got the chance. Bal passed Viridia once in a corridor between classes, and she pulled a face at him over an armload of books, and that was all either of them said to the other until dinner.

* * *

He was in his place and halfway through a plate of pot roast when Lora came in.

She paused in the doorway with her books in both arms. Her gaze went first to her own sygl’s table, where her stretch of empty bench was waiting as usual, and then down the whole length of the hall to him.

She came the long way, straight down the middle.

The noise did not stop in one go. It died behind her as she passed each table, so that she walked at the front edge of a quiet that kept growing. The Terra boys stopped. The Tempus table stopped. When she reached the last table, a spoon went down on a plate two tables over, and it was loud. Even the teachers had stopped eating.

Lora set her books on the bench. She slid in next to Viridia, facing him, tore a corner from a page of her notes, wrote her order and laid it on her plate. The plate went off empty and returned with a bowl of soup and two rolls.

She buttered the first roll right out to the crust at every edge, while the whole hall watched her do it.

Viridia returned to her spaghetti. Bal ate his pot roast. Somewhere behind him somebody coughed, and the talk came back in pieces, lower than before and nowhere near as busy.

The three of them didn’t look around. The easy feeling from yesterday evening had come to dinner with them, and they kept it close and ignored the rest.

“So what was it you gave Amelia?” Lora asked.

Bal finished his milk and waited.

It filled from the bottom, as it always did, white climbing the inside of the cup as if someone under the table were pouring. He had looked under the table, his first night. There was only the floor and his own shoes. He still didn’t know how the cup decided it was full.

“I gave her a Dawn card,” he said. “A team card, with her name on the back. I think what hit her so hard, though, was that I put her down as Amelia Norheart.”

Lora stopped, the roll forgotten in her hand. Then she bit and chewed and went on chewing.

“Why do you think it hit her that hard?” she asked, once she could.

“Look what her family did to her,” Viridia said. “They betrayed her. She honestly had nobody. Bal showed her that now she does.” She stuck her fork into her spaghetti and wound it, and kept winding long after it was wound. “I would have felt the same way.”

She took the bite. When it was gone she said, “I’m happy to be friends with her. With all of you, really. I just hope it stays this easy.”

“Stays easy how?” Bal asked.

Viridia shrugged.

“Just some rumors I heard this morning. Word is they’re going to start enforcing a rule. Unless you’re in class, or off school grounds, you can’t mingle with the other sygls.”

Bal looked past her. Over at Lora’s old table a boy found his food again the moment Bal’s eyes reached him. One of the teachers had not bothered to turn his back at all.

“Do you think they will?”

“Probably,” Viridia said. “They weren’t happy when it was only me hanging around with you. Now we’ve got Lora too, and her a Waves sygl? Yeah. It’s a good possibility.”

“What could they do to you if you refused?”

Viridia winced. It was plainly the question she had been hoping he would skip, and she made him wait for the answer.

“It depends on whether it comes as a school rule,” she said, “or just something all the Lead Mages try to do. If it’s a school rule, they could expel us. If it’s only the Lead Mages, it’s demerits.”

At *expel*, Lora’s butter knife stayed in her fist, upright on the tablecloth with its point to the ceiling, and Bal doubted she knew it was there.

Going by the teachers’ faces, the school would make it a rule.

Bal drew in a long breath and eased it out. If the teachers did this, he could think of one way to stay around the two of them, and it was not the kind of thing you said over soup.
