“There’s something we could do,” he told them. “But I’d rather wait to tell you what it is until we need it.”

Viridia leaned in. Lora’s eyes went very wide behind her glasses. They both said all right, and they both meant it, and they were both plainly dying to know how anybody got around the school’s own rules.

Lora noticed the knife in her fist, and used it on her second roll.

* * *

After dinner the three of them walked to the Blood dorm together, past every open door in the corridor.

The new door was in the wall beside the library. Bal had put it there that morning, between waking up and finding his socks. He had stood barefoot at the hearth and pictured Lora with the green book, reading the poultice chapter aloud until somebody stopped her, and then he had pictured where she would actually do it. A long bench scrubbed pale. A stone sink with a pump. Beams overhead with herbs hung upside down to dry. A window, so whatever she boiled had somewhere to go.

Lora opened the door and smelled dried mint and did not come out again for hours.

Bal and Viridia kept to the couches. Viridia spread her runes homework over the low table and chewed her pencil over it. Bal took the old couch and the history of Raizen’s wars.

From the herb room came the squeal of the pump. Much later Lora’s voice said “No, no, no, no” to something that hissed.

Viridia finished her last rune and sat back. She watched him turn pages for a while.

“How come you’re reading that?”

Bal kept his place with a finger. “Because they’ve started training again. And they aren’t going after Naisura.”

Viridia was quiet.

“You mean they’re going after Amelia’s followers,” she said. “Don’t you.”

Bal turned the page.

Any other night he would have waited until the girls had gone home to open this book at all. Raizen was Viridia’s originator. But if he ever did have to offer to convert them, he wanted Viridia to understand exactly what they were up against first. He left the book open in his lap and explained it as he saw it. None of the originators were happy that Amelia was free. They had decided Raizen ranked lowest of the four, so dealing with her was his job. They knew perfectly well they could not trap her again as they had before. Going after her followers was all they had left, short of actually fighting her, and none of them wanted that.

Viridia listened to all of it. She didn’t argue. She didn’t ask anything else either. Her pencil lay across her finished runes and did not move again, and when Lora came out smelling of scorched ginger to report that the second recipe had worked, it was Bal who had to ask her how.

Amelia came back a little before curfew. One minute the arm of the far couch was empty, and the next she was perched on it, asking Lora what had burned.

* * *

Later, with the girls gone and the lamp turned low, Amelia lay on her side facing him.

“Why was Viridia so quiet when they left?”

He explained the rumor and the rule that might be coming, and what he had said to Viridia over Raizen’s book.

“Honestly,” Amelia said, “that rule sounds like something Kenmei would suggest. When the originators pulled the teachers, they probably told them Viridia was spending time with you.”

“Yeah. More than likely.” Bal laced his fingers behind his head. “I did tell Lora and Viridia I had a way around the rule. I didn’t tell them what it was. Hopefully it doesn’t come to that yet. With Raizen already getting ready for a war, if I convert Viridia now, he’s going to lose it.”

Amelia had never in her life cared how Raizen felt about anything, and she did not start now.

“He’s going to lose it whenever it happens.”

“Yeah, but it’s still early,” Bal argued. “I hope I don’t have to offer it yet. If it comes to that, I will. I honestly don’t want to lose either of them.”

The blanket moved. Amelia came across the bed until she was against his side.

“Just do what you have to do,” she said. “We will deal with whatever happens. Together.”

She laid her head on his chest. Her hair was cool and heavy and smelled faintly of somewhere that was not the castle, wind, maybe, or pine. Bal unlaced his fingers and put one hand on her back.

On the nightstand the card leaned on the lamp’s brass foot. In the next room, down in his pack, the job paper was still folded in half where he had tucked it. The weekend was a few days off. Saturday, then. Saturday they would go find Lora’s flowers.


CHAPTER SIXTEEN

Whitehaven Road

The next day Lora asked whether anyone thought it would rain on Saturday.

Nobody did, particularly. The day after, she asked again, in case something had changed, and Viridia told her it would only rain if she kept asking. By midweek she had given up on the two of them and was going out onto the front steps before breakfast to check the sky for herself. She came back in with her glasses fogged and her cheeks pink from the wind, and reported that it looked fine.

She had also decided that nothing on earth was going to keep them inside the castle that weekend, least of all homework. Every evening she marched the three of them up to the Blood common room straight after dinner and sat them down at the low table until every page was done. She did her own twice as fast as either of them and then read the questions over their shoulders. When Viridia complained, Lora pointed out that a teacher could keep a student in on Saturday for unfinished work, and that she had read the rule herself. Viridia stopped complaining and finished her history.

Bal spent two of those evenings in the library first.

The library had very little on the Saegen Woods. The librarian found him a book on ley lines, and in the back of it, a single paragraph. A ley line ran straight through the woods. Some of the animals there had been absorbing the magykal energy for generations, and they had grown bigger than they ought to be, and meaner. How much bigger, the paragraph didn’t say.

Bal read it twice. Then he copied the paragraph into the back of his rune notes, so that he would have it with him.

It was Amelia who surprised him.

On Thursday night she was on the end of the old red couch while the girls finished their runes, and when Lora started in on what time they ought to leave, Amelia said, very lightly, that maybe it would be simpler if she stayed behind this time.

Lora stopped with her pencil in the air.

“Why?” she asked.

“It’s your first job,” Amelia told them. “I would hate to be in the way.”

Viridia put down her book. “You’ve got a card,” she said. “With the team on it and everything. We want you to come.”

“You’re part of the team,” Lora said, and then added, quickly, “If you want to be.”

Amelia looked from one of them to the other. Then something in her face gave way, and she told them she would love to, so warmly that Lora went pink.

On Friday, after Warfare, Bal hung back while the line broke up and went to ask Zoran whether the three of them could borrow some of the school’s weapons for a trip off the grounds.

Zoran said nothing for so long that Bal began composing a second, better way of asking.

“Bring them back clean,” Zoran said.

Bal went to the last rack and took down the rapier he had trained with since his first class. It came with a plain leather scabbard and a belt, and the belt needed a new hole punched in it before it would stay on him. Viridia took down the long spear she always used, and carried it back to the castle on her shoulder.

* * *

Saturday came up cool and clear. Lora’s sky had been right.

They met at the castle gate at first light. Viridia crossed the dewy courtyard with Hopper trotting at her knee.

Bal knew Hopper only from Viridia’s stories. With the collar on, she was the size of a big farm dog. Her fur was silver, and across her back and shoulders it ran blood red, as though someone had laid a shawl over her. Her paws were too large for the rest of her. She came past Bal without a glance and made for Amelia, and leaned her whole shoulder against Amelia’s skirt, and stayed there.

“Well,” Viridia said. “I see where I rank.”

Lora arrived last, at a run, and Bal saw she had a rapier at her hip that had never hung on any rack of Zoran’s. The guard was dark steel, and the grip had been wrapped and rewrapped so often that it had gone smooth.

“That’s yours,” he said.

“My father had it made for me. After the tournament.” Lora drew it a few inches to show him the blade. “It’s enchanted. It never needs sharpening.” She slid it home, and for once she didn’t mind the attention.

Bal faced Amelia. “We could still get you something. Zoran wouldn’t mind.”

Amelia was scratching Hopper behind the ears. The offer caught her off guard.

“I am my own weapon,” she said. “Besides, I don’t plan on fighting unless something really bad happens. Each of you needs to feel comfortable defending yourselves.”

Bal thought that over and could not find anything wrong with it.

New Spring was on the road out, and Bal had them stop. The job had come through Soren’s counter, but the flowers were for the alchemist, and he wanted her to know they were on their way. Her shop was down a lane off the square, with bundles of something gray and dry hanging in the window, and the smell of it reached them before the door did.

Ms. Summers was a small, stooped woman wrapped in two shawls. She heard who they were and why they had come, and she got slowly up off her stool.

“I normally hire a team to see me through those woods,” she said. “I’m not up to it this year.” She looked at the four of them, and at the wolf on her step, and decided that they would do. “Mind the river. That’s where they grow.”

Lora asked how the flowers ought to be cut, and how long they would keep once cut. Ms. Summers’ face lit up. She answered at length, and Lora wrote it all down on the back of an old receipt against the doorframe, every word, and buttoned it away in her breast pocket.

Leaving the square, they bought food for the night, and Hopper took a great interest in the jerky. It all went into Bal’s pack.

* * *

The woods lay about twenty miles off. They would have to pass through one more town, Whitehaven, and after that cross a mountain pass before they reached the trees.

They came into Whitehaven around midday. It was smaller than New Spring and busier, with a row of food stalls down the middle of its one wide street, and the smoke from all of them blowing the same direction.

“How much farther are the woods?” Viridia asked. She was holding out her money to the man who ran the meat pie stall.

Bal checked the sun, and then his map.

“At least four more hours,” he said. “Maybe five.” He paid for his own pie.

The pie man took the coins off Bal’s left palm by their edges, careful as somebody lifting a coal off a hearth, and wiped his fingers down his apron afterward. Nobody else at the stall saw it. Bal decided not to mention it with Amelia standing right there.

He had offered, back at the first stall, to pay for all of them. Nobody would let him. Lora had said “No” before he got to the end of the sentence, and Viridia had said it was bad enough he carried everything.

The pies were hot and the crust was the flaky kind that ended up on everybody’s shirt. Hopper sat at Amelia’s feet and caught the flakes. They walked the length of Whitehaven while they ate, looking in the shop windows, and then set out again.

At the town limit, past the last house, Viridia put her hand on Hopper’s collar.

Hopper grew. She grew as if she were unfolding from inside herself, legs lengthening, chest deepening, until her shoulders were above Viridia’s waist and her head was level with Bal’s ribs. She shook herself all over, and her red fur lifted and lay flat.

“I’m glad you thought to buy that collar,” Bal said. “I’m not sure how the people back there would have taken a full-sized bloodwolf walking down the street.”

Clear of the town, Hopper left the road at once and ranged ahead of them, out to the edge of the trees and back again, nose down and then up, checking everything for them. Lora said her raven would have liked this. It was sick, though, and she had left it at the castle rather than risk it getting hurt.

* * *

They were a few miles out of Whitehaven, about halfway to the pass, when they stopped to rest. There was a stretch of thick woods on the right side of the road, and a flat rock on the left that was good for sitting.

Bal was getting the water skin out when Hopper growled.

She had turned to face the woods. She was down low on her front legs, and the growl came up from somewhere deep in her chest and kept coming, and all the hair along her back stood up in stiff tufts like a porcupine’s quills.

Every weapon was out before they had finished standing.

Seven men stood at the edge of the forest, silent, watching them. Six were in their twenties or thirties. The seventh was much older, with gray hair cropped short and a big, heavy body. The six wore leather armor that was decent enough. The older man wore a shirt of steel mesh that had seen better days. It was pitted in places, and there was dried blood on the rings.

They had longswords. They had axes. Two carried crossbows, and both crossbows were already up and pointed.

“Your weapons,” the older man said, “if you please.”

He pointed his sword at the dirt by his boots. He wanted them thrown down there.

The crossbows were the problem. Two bolts would cross that ground far faster than any of them could. Charging was pointless. He let his eyes drop, as if he were working out how to toss the rapier and still keep a little pride, and the sun flashed off something silver on Viridia’s left hand.

The ring.

He shifted his weight toward her and kept his voice low.

“Can you use the ring to shake the ground under them? It should give us a chance to get to them before they can shoot.”

Viridia didn’t answer. Her lids drooped. She was already reaching for whatever lay inside the band.

Bal hoped none of the seven was Terra. This was Kenmei’s domain, strictly speaking, so it was possible. There was no way to tell. All seven had weapons in their hands, and a hand holding a weapon did not show you its palm.

He moved his free hand behind his leg, where it was in her view, and opened it.

* * *

The ground jumped under the bandits’ feet.

It bucked like a wagon bed going over a rut, and kept on bucking, and seven men who had been standing still had to start trying to stand. A crossbow bolt went wild somewhere up in the branches.

Bal was already running.

He ran at the leader, because he had done the talking, and because he was the biggest. Off to his right Hopper went past him like a thrown rock. A bolt hit her side and bounced off, and then somebody screamed, a high awful scream, and everybody but their leader jerked at the sound of it.

The leader brought his longsword around to meet Bal, and after that he was all Bal saw.

A rapier was not a hacking blade. Lora had told him that the first week, and Zoran had told him after her. You kept moving. You took the small hits when they were there to take and you never stood still long enough for somebody to land a big one. Bal had practiced nothing else in Warfare.

He kept moving. He parried and stabbed and circled. The big man was slow, but whenever his sword met Bal’s, it went up Bal’s arm and into his teeth, and he did not tire. Bal put the point into his shoulder and felt it skid on the mail. He put it into the meat of his upper arm and felt that one go in. The big man grunted and came on.

Somewhere to the left a great axe was whistling. Bal circled and caught it at the edge of his sight. A young bandit with an axe was swinging it in wide wild arcs at Viridia, and Viridia was darting in and out between swings and stabbing him, and each time she drew blood he roared and swung harder. The axe came round flat and caught her spear and tore it clean out of her hands. The spear went spinning end over end. Then gray and red hit the axeman from the side, and Hopper had him, and he did not get up.

Bal circled. Parry. Stab. Parry.

Lora was over beyond the leader’s shoulder now, on Bal’s next turn. Three men had her surrounded. Then two did. Then one, and then that sword was coming again.

Something turned under Bal’s heel. A stone, a rut. He stumbled.

He felt the sword coming before he saw it. He threw himself sideways and twisted, and the blade, which had been meant for his spine, opened his back from one shoulder blade to the other instead. It was cold first. Then it was very hot.

He got his feet back under him.

“Bal!” That was Lora, off to his right. “Should I take him?”

He looked toward her voice.

The gray-haired man’s sword opened his left forearm from the wrist halfway to the elbow.

“Do not interfere,” Bal said through his teeth.

He meant all three girls. There was no breath to spare for saying so.

The man showed his teeth. There were not many of them, and those he had were brown.

“Want to kill me yourself, huh?” He lunged low, trying for Bal’s legs, and Bal skipped back. “Don’t blame you. Not with those pretty girls watching. I’ll have some fun with them, once I’m done with you.”

Bal stepped back, out of reach, and dragged his sword wrist across his eyes. It came away wet, sweat and something else.

The girls were all in a line on the road behind him.

Lora had her rapier lowered and her eyes on the fight. Viridia had her spear back and was up on her toes. Next to Lora stood Amelia. She had come one step off the road toward him and no farther, and her two hands were holding each other back.

Bal took in all three for the length of one breath.

Then he stepped forward with his rapier high, jabbed at the bandit’s face, and slashed. The bandit parried. They traded blows, high and low, across the bucked-up dirt, for what seemed an hour and was probably a minute, and then Bal dropped straight down onto his knees and put the point of his rapier through the old man’s unprotected right knee and out the back of it.

The man howled. His leg folded and he fell, still swinging, the longsword whipping round in a wild flat circle. He tried to rise and his knee would not have it. He settled for swinging, round and round, to keep Bal off.

Bal rose and stayed well clear of the circle. The fight was over. There was no sense in walking into that sword to prove it.

Hopper was standing a little way off with her head low.

“Would you like to finish it?” Bal asked her.

If a wolf could grin, Hopper did. She was across the ground in two bounds. The screaming stopped.

* * *

Bal stood in the road and got his breath back.

His shirt was wringing wet with sweat, and the back of it hung open. He peeled it off, slowly, and used it to wipe the blood off his arm. There was a long gaping hole in the back of it. He would have to sew that before he wore it again. He wiped his face with the front of it, which was the only part left that was any use, and went to the girls.

Lora let him come right up to her. Then she put both hands on his chest and pushed him down onto a fallen tree at the side of the road.

“Sit,” she ordered. “And don’t talk.”

Her pack was already open. She had salve in a little clay pot, and bandages, and she went around behind him to see to his back first.

Amelia was standing off from the rest of them.

Bal moved over on the log to make room. She came and took the space, very straight-backed.

“What’s wrong?” he asked her.

Lora’s hands went on working at his arm. It was some time before Amelia answered.

“I think that was the hardest thing I’ve ever done,” she said. “I wanted to hurt that man for daring to hurt you.”

She had spent close to three thousand years locked away.

Bal reached over for her hand. Hers held on.

“Thank you for not jumping in,” he said. “You too, Lora.”

Lora glanced up from where she knelt. Then she returned to his arm.

“I have to be able to protect myself,” Bal said. “It was hard for me too, knowing any of you could get hurt. And I’m proud of you. All of you. You took care of yourselves.”

The salve smelled of crushed mint and something underneath it that he couldn’t place. Wherever Lora had put it, the pain had eased off and gone somewhere far away, and Bal laughed, because it was gone and he had not expected it to go.

“I’d much rather I got hurt than any of you,” he said. “Great job on not getting hurt.”

Hopper came down from the trees to the log. She smelled of wolf and hard work. Bal rested his good hand lightly on top of her head, between her ears.

“Thanks,” he told her. “You did good.”

Once he was bandaged up, they went through the bandits for anything worth taking. Bal wiped the rapier clean on a dead man’s sleeve and slid it back into its scabbard. Viridia kept her spear in her hand.

They found very little. A few coins. The crossbows, which none of them knew how to use, so they left them.

On their leader’s right hand was a ring. It was a heart, in red gold, small and well made.

“That’s a heart ring,” Lora said, behind Bal. “They come in pairs. Two people each wear one, and each ring beats in time with the other person’s heart. If yours ever stops, it means the other one is dead.” She paused. “Or that they took it off.”

“It’s expensive,” Viridia said.

“Very.”

You didn’t expect to see one on a bandit. Not even the leader.

Bal left it on the dead man’s hand.

They talked about it as they walked on, looking for somewhere to camp. They wanted to cross the pass and come into the woods by morning light.

* * *

About two miles on, a trail turned off the road into the woods.

Amelia’s place beside him was empty, with no noise at all. She was back before Bal had got his pack off his shoulders, picking a leaf out of her hair.

“There’s a fire pit,” she said. “Buried, so it’s been used. I think travelers stop here a lot.”

Bal left his pack with his bedroll by the pit and went to look for firewood. He went bare-backed in the bandages, because his shirt was a rag, and the evening air found its way in under every strip.

Viridia saw where he was headed and came along.

They walked in silence for a way. Viridia did the picking up, branches and sticks off the forest floor, and laid them across Bal’s arms, which he held out for her, stiff as a pair of shelf brackets.

“I didn’t expect we’d have to fight so soon,” she said finally.

“Me neither.” The ring was still bothering him, and he could not have said why. “We knew it was a possibility, though. I just didn’t expect bandits. I thought it’d only be things in the Saegen Woods we had to worry about.”

The cut on his arm gave a burn under the bandage, as if it had heard.

“I can’t believe nobody else got hurt but me.” Bal chuckled. “Don’t get me wrong, I’m glad none of you did. I’m only surprised it wasn’t worse.”

“Lora’s a menace with that rapier of hers.” Viridia laid a branch across his arms with some force. “I thought I was doing okay. Until Hopper had to save me anyway. Then I turn around to see how everybody else is doing, and Lora’s already taken care of three of them by herself.” She stopped, still not quite believing it. “And Amelia. She looked about ready to have a heart attack, watching you fight that big man. Can a vampire have a heart attack?”

Bal had no idea.

“Anyway, all she said was ‘I do not like this.’ And then, ‘He is already hurt.’ Before I’d even seen it.” Viridia pulled a dead branch off a stump. “She hates that she can’t protect you. Lora had it easier, I think. I heard her muttering about all the mistakes you were making.” She laughed. “Nonstop.”

While it was happening, the fight had felt endless, all that dodging and all those careful strikes. It had probably been over in three or four minutes. Bal was reasonably sure he could have handled one of the younger men without much trouble. But he would be damned if he let any of the girls take on the big one and get hurt doing it.

They had been lucky, too. If Hopper had not gone for the crossbows first, it could have gone a lot worse.

Soon his arms were full, and they turned back.

* * *

Somebody had laid out all the bedrolls around where the fire would be. They lay in a ring, an arm’s length apart, except for one, which had another set close beside it.

Bal guessed which two those were. Nobody owned up to doing it, and he let it be.

Viridia stood her spear against a tree, and Bal got the fire going. Lora had a needle in her pack, and he sewed the hole in his shirt shut by firelight with stitches so big that Viridia winced at them, and pulled it back on over the bandages. Dinner was the bread, the jerky and the cheese from New Spring that morning. Hopper got the end of the jerky.

The talk around the fire was all the fight, and how to do better next time.

“Arrows,” Bal said. “Those crossbows scared me.” He chewed on the idea along with the jerky. “I could make us shirts. With runes worked into them that turn arrows away.”

Lora sat straight up.

“I’d have to charge every rune,” he said. “Each one only works once. So you’d want a lot of them. I’d just have to buy the materials.”

Viridia announced that she would wear anything, as long as it wasn’t brown.

Lora finished her cheese. Then she brushed off her hands and stood over Bal until he understood that she meant him.

“Up,” she said. “You stumbled because your back foot was too far behind you. Here. Look.” She put him in his stance and kicked his heel in an inch. “And you keep dropping your point after a lunge. Every time. He saw it by the third one. Again.”

She went through every mistake he had made, in order. He asked her whether it was cheating to drop to the ground like that, and what you did if your opponent was taller and also left-handed, and she answered all of it patiently, however silly it sounded once it was out of his mouth.

All that while Amelia sat across the fire, hugging her shins, and said almost nothing. Bal noticed. He was sure the others had too.

When Lora was done with him, he went around the fire and held out his hand to Amelia. She took it, and he pulled her up.

“We’re going for a walk,” he told the others.

* * *

He kept hold of her hand into the forest.

At that time of night the only sounds were the insects and their own feet in the fallen leaves. The fire dropped behind them to a smudge of orange between the trunks.

“What’s on your mind?” Bal asked, once they were well away from camp.

Amelia took her time answering. Bal waited. He did not mind waiting. It was a good night for it, still and windless.

“I feel useless.” Her voice was flat and quiet. “I finally have people I think of as friends, and I can’t help them. I was afraid someone would die. I can’t remember ever being afraid of that before. When he cut your back,” she said, and her eyes shut, and opened again, “it took every bit of will I have not to run over there and tear that man in half.”

Bal walked with that a while.

“Amelia, sometimes we have to fight our own battles,” he said. “I’d never fought someone who really wanted to hurt me before. And I’d do it all again, to protect any of you.”

The leaves crunched. Somewhere off to the right something small ran away from them.

“I know that in the big scheme of things, there’s little I can offer you besides my company. The things that can hurt the others can’t hurt you. And you’re still just as important to me as they are. If your family came after you, think how that would make me feel. There’d be nothing I could do to help you. I don’t like that. But I trust you to do everything in your power to stay safe.”

He stopped her there, among the black trunks, and turned her to face him.

“Our group means a lot to me. You mean a lot to me. I don’t need you here for what you can do for me. I want you here because I like you.”

She held still.

“Besides,” he said, “you can always jump in if you see one of us about to get killed. Say a crossbow bolt had hit somebody. You’ve just got to believe we’ll do everything we can to stay alive.”

He hoped she would take all of it to heart. He left her to it, and led her back toward the orange smudge between the trees.

* * *

The other two had already crawled into their bedrolls when they got back to the firelight. Lora had her glasses folded on a rock beside her head. Viridia was a lump with blue hair coming out of one end.

Amelia pushed her bedroll over until it lay against Bal’s, while he put more wood on the fire.

Before he lay down he went to Hopper at the edge of the light.

“You going to be our guard?” he asked.

Hopper chuffed at him, once.

That settled it. Bal went back and slid into his bedroll. Amelia’s hand found his under the blankets, cool as the night was.

Beyond the trees the mountains stood up black against the stars, and in the black there was a notch where the road went through. They would be in it an hour after first light. The woods were beyond it.


CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

Saegen Woods

The pass was full of gravel.

They reached it about an hour after they broke camp. High gray stone rose on both sides, and over the years it had shed itself onto the floor of the gap, loose rubble with sharp pieces mixed through it that had sheared off the cliffs clean and kept their edges. They had to pick their way. Viridia used the butt of her spear as a walking stick. Hopper ranged ahead and trotted back to them, over and over, and did not put a paw wrong from one end to the other.

It was not a long pass, half a mile or so. The gravel crunched and slid under their boots until the walls fell back, and they stood at the crest of a hill. A worn path ran down it to the woods below.

The woods were very green and very thick. From where they stood, the treetops looked solid enough to walk on.

Bal checked the map.

“Well, this is it,” he said. “Where did Ms. Summers say the flowers were, in there?”

Lora unbuttoned her breast pocket and unfolded the receipt. It had gone soft at the folds from riding against her overnight. She read it over, then raised her head to study the forest.

“There.” She pointed east. “Do you see the river? She told me about five hundred feet in, on the bank.”

He found it, a bright thread through the green. They could go around and cut straight in from the east, which looked shorter. Or they could follow the river right to the spot. They needed to fill the water skins anyway.

“River,” Bal decided.

* * *

Going down the hill, he asked Lora about the flowers. She had the alchemy book, after all, and he wanted her guess at what Ms. Summers might use them for.

Lora had read the entry more than once. She didn’t stop to think.

“Aurora felis is rare,” she said. “It goes into healing potions, mostly. The flowers are blood red and yellow, and they only bloom every three years, so you have to get them soon after, or you’ve missed it. That’s part of why she wanted to hurry and send somebody, instead of waiting until she felt better.” She stepped over a root without breaking stride. “And once they’re cut, you have seventy-two hours. They have to be prepared and used inside that, or they start losing their potency.”

“So anything made with them costs a fortune,” Viridia said.

“Pretty much. What goes in with them isn’t cheap either.” Lora hesitated. “She offered to help me make two of the potions. As part of the payment.”

None of them needed the money for anything in particular, except the guild dues. They had agreed to it back in New Spring without much of a discussion.

She was already halfway into Ms. Summers’ shop in her head. She had bought plants and supplies to stock the herb room Bal had put in for her, and now she was going to watch a real master work from start to finish.

* * *

The river was quick and clear and not very deep. Brightly colored fish held against the current in it, with the sun bouncing off their scales.

They stopped first to fill the water skins. It made Bal’s teeth ache and tasted faintly of stone. Viridia drank straight from her cupped hands, and Hopper waded in up to her ankles and drank more than all of them together. Then they turned upstream and followed the river in under the branches.

“I’m surprised they’re normal size,” Viridia said as they walked. “I figured, with the ley line, they’d be bigger.”

“It’s over that way.” Amelia pointed off to the west. “So the fish wouldn’t have been touched by it. With beasts, and even with vampires, you can take some of another creature’s magykal energy by consuming it. If a creature kept killing and eating its prey, it could get quite large indeed.”

Somewhere out there in the west, then, something had eaten something that had eaten something, for generations.

“Fortunately,” Amelia added, “there’s nothing in these woods that can’t be killed. The closest thing would be Hopper here, with that pelt of hers.”

Hopper heard her name and trotted over in case she was wanted.

* * *

It was not long before they found the spot. On the bank of the river, among the tall grass, grew six of the red and yellow flowers.

They were smaller than Bal had pictured. Each sat at the top of a single stem, red at the heart and yellow at the edges.

Six meant Lora could keep three for herself, once the other three went to Ms. Summers.

“Why didn’t Ms. Summers tell us to bring back all of them?” Amelia asked.

Lora tucked the receipt into her pocket and got down on her heels in front of the flowers with her knife. She cut each stem low, carefully, and eased each flower into its own glass vial. The vials had ridden from the castle in her pack, wrapped in cloth.

“She only has the ingredients to make three. So it was no use being greedy.” Lora pushed the cork into the last vial and stood up. “One potion is worth what she’d make in a month selling her other potions. I think the only reason she offered us two is that I’m interested in alchemy. Her face lit up when I started asking her questions.”

She held the vials, all six, in the crook of her arm.

“She’s lonely, I think,” Lora said.

He gave her his back so she could stow the padded vials in his pack. He felt her working at the buckles, and the small weight settling in.

“I’m surprised she doesn’t have an apprentice or something,” he said, and led them out toward the tree line, still following the river. “An alchemist is important. If something happened to her, the whole town would suffer.”

Nobody answered that. The water went on talking to itself beside them. Bal noticed, after a while, that it was the only voice left. The birds had stopped, and he had missed them going.

He drew a breath to ask if the others had noticed.

* * *

Something big and furry hit him from the side.

He went off his feet and clear over the water. The water went by underneath him. A tree stopped him, all in one blow, and his head snapped back against the bark.

For a while the world was green and full of bells.

He got one knee under him. Then the other foot. The tree helped. Across the water somebody was shouting his name, Lora, he thought, and the ground kept tipping to throw him down again, and his hand went hunting for his rapier and could not lay hands on the hilt.

He was not badly hurt. The thing that had hit him was.

When his eyes would focus again, it was over, and he had to put it together from what was in front of him. Amelia had gone after it the moment he was hit. Its claws had done nothing to her. It had tried to run, and she had not allowed it. Now she had both arms locked around its massive neck, and she was squeezing, and it was not moving anymore. Above its head her face was white and set with fury. She looked as if she would have liked to kill it a second time.

It was a bear. He saw that once she let it drop into the grass. It was the biggest bear he had ever heard of, let alone seen.

Amelia came straight to him. She got an arm around him and lifted, and carried him back to the other bank, and his boots never touched the water. He was glad. He was still woozy, and he did not much want to try swimming.

* * *

On their own bank the girls went over him. Viridia tipped his chin up and found the knot coming up under his hair. Lora felt down his arms and asked how many fingers she was holding up, and accepted three, though he suspected it had been two. The bandages from yesterday had held. His back had something to say about the tree, but it had been complaining since the road.

Next they checked the vials.

They were fine, all six. Lora had put them in the side pocket of his pack. If she had put them in the middle, between him and that tree, no amount of padding would have saved them.

“Side pocket,” Lora said, a little faintly, and pressed her fingers to her mouth.

Amelia kept sending glares over the water at the body in the grass.

“Uh. Amelia,” Bal said.

She turned. “Yes?”

Her voice had lost its playfulness. Her head was in the fight, he figured.

“Um. I know the bear couldn’t hurt you,” he said. “But it did manage to hurt your clothes.”

Amelia’s face went blank.

He waved a hand at his own chest.

She checked her front, and spun her back to all of them in one motion. The bear had torn her dress open high on the bodice. Both girls had seen it already, while they were fussing over him, and had kept quiet about it, which Bal found interesting.

Amelia fixed her dress. Her hands worked fast at it, and when she had it held together she walked straight past him, heading out of the woods. Her cheeks were flaming red.

They followed her out. She walked by herself, well ahead, and none of them tried to catch up.

* * *

At the pass, Bal left Lora and Viridia and lengthened his stride until he was walking beside Amelia.

“Thanks for taking care of the bear,” he said, as the walls rose around them. “I hate to think what would have happened if Viridia and Lora had had to fight it.”

“I honestly never heard it,” Amelia told him. “I was listening to Lora when you got hit. He must have been very old to grow that big.”

Bal bent and scooped up some stones. They were good ones, round and smooth, egg-sized.

“At least it couldn’t hurt you,” he said lightly. “You looked pretty angry.”

He tossed the first stone up, and the second after it, and then the third, and caught them coming down, and threw them again. He had learned it as a boy, with one hand. Two made it easy. The stones rose and fell in a little wheel in front of his face.

“I think you’d have killed it again if you could,” he said.

“You have no idea. Seeing you hit that tree, I snapped. It had tried to run.” She smiled, grimly. “But it didn’t get far.”

They walked. The stones climbed and dropped into his palms, slap, slap, slap, and climbed again.

Amelia watched him juggle for a stretch of the pass.

“So you like to juggle, huh?” she asked.

Her glance flicked past his shoulder, to the girls behind them, and returned to him.

Bal was sure she wasn’t talking about the rocks.

He let the stones fall. They clattered onto the gravel and lay among all the others, and in a moment he could no longer tell which had been his.

“It’s not something I planned on doing,” he said. “And if the ones being juggled had a problem with it, I’d hope they would tell me.”

He walked on a little. Amelia waited.

“I would also hope,” he said, “that each one knows how much they matter to me. And that I do not feel more strongly for one over the other.”

Gravel crunched behind them, close.

When he turned, Lora and Viridia were nearer than he had thought, only a couple of paces off. Viridia said something to Lora, too low to hear, and the two of them slowed and let the distance open up again.

Amelia said nothing. She walked with her arms folded, thinking about what he had told her, and he let her think.

Bal ran through it again in his own head. They left the pass and went on along the road, and a few miles went by in quiet. Behind them Lora and Viridia were talking in low voices, with Hopper trotting happily at their side.

* * *

They had decided to walk straight back instead of camping a second night. It was an uneventful walk, though his head ached most of the way. Twice before Whitehaven he felt in his pocket for Thad’s stone. It was cool and dark each time. Somewhere out there Raizen’s men were drilling for a war, and all the stone could tell him was that nobody had sent for him yet. They came through Whitehaven in the late afternoon, and at its town limit Viridia touched Hopper’s collar, and Hopper shrank back down to fit the streets.

It was full dark when they reached New Spring. They went straight up the square to the guild hall to turn in the flowers.

The windows were lit. Dawn members came and went at all hours, so the Hall never closed. Soren was on duty, which was a relief. She had helped them on their first visit.

Lora unbuckled the side pocket of Bal’s pack, and three of the vials went onto the counter in a row. Soren drew them over to her side and began counting out the reward.

“Oh. Um. The reward’s changed a little,” Lora told her. “Ms. Summers is paying us partly in potions.”

“Thank you for letting me know.” Soren made a few notes in the ledger on her desk, in that even hand, and counted again. This time the amount came out right, minus what she held back for their guild dues.

* * *

Dinner at the castle had been over for hours. They bought steaming bowls of stew at a restaurant on the square and ate at a table by the window, and Bal had a second bowl, because the first had disappeared without his noticing.

It was well after curfew when they reached the castle, and every hall was quiet. In the entrance hall Lora hugged Amelia, and then Bal, holding her own three vials out to one side in their cloth to keep them safe. Viridia hugged them both after her, quick and hard. The two of them went off toward their dorms together, talking under their breath, and their footsteps faded down the corridor.

Curfew had no hold on a Lead Mage.

Bal walked Amelia home to the Blood dorm, and he walked slowly. The castle was dark around them. They did not talk. Back in the pass the girls had been closer behind him than he’d thought, and he had no way of knowing how much of it they had heard.


CHAPTER EIGHTEEN

Six Weeks

Over the next six weeks they took a job almost every Saturday, and some weeks they found a reason to take one on Sunday too.

The money hardly mattered. They needed the road. The castle had gone sour on them by degrees, and by the second week Bal could feel it before he was through the doors at breakfast. Conversations stalled when he came near and started up again behind him. A Waves first year he had never spoken to stepped off the stairs to let him by, as if Bal might be catching. Twice in the corridor near the library, someone said something loud enough to carry and low enough to deny. Both times it was the same advice. He ought to stay with his own kind.

His own kind, as far as the castle went, was Bal. There was a dorm full of it.

Lora and Viridia heard worse than he did, he suspected, and told him less. Viridia came to dinner one night with her mouth in a flat line and kept silent through the whole meal. When Lora asked her what was wrong, she said “Nothing,” in a voice that shut the subject, and Lora let it stay shut.

The classes, at least, went well.

In Warfare, Zoran stopped behind him one afternoon while Bal was working through his forms. Bal waited for the correction. He had learned to brace for it.

“You’ve come on fast, Norheart,” Zoran said. “Good work.”

He moved along the line. Bal was left with his rapier pointed at nothing until Lora coughed at him from across the sand.

It was Lora who did most of the work, though. The cuts from the road to Whitehaven had healed to thin pink lines, but ever since that fight she had taken his training as her own personal job. She wanted him able to protect himself, and she wanted it even harder than he did. Three mornings a week she was waiting at the practice yard before breakfast with her own rapier and her hair already pinned up. She drilled him on footwork until he could do it along a single crack in the flagstones without stepping off. She drilled him on keeping his point up until he dreamed about it. When he finally got through a whole exchange with her without a touch landing on him, she told him it had been adequate, and was pleased with herself all through breakfast.

Her three Aurora felis flowers had not gone to waste, either. Ms. Summers kept her word and helped her brew the two potions before the seventy-two hours ran out.

* * *

The thing he was proudest of happened in Crafting.

For weeks he had been working runes into whatever he made in there. He carved them into the handles of wooden spoons. He engraved them on hinges, on a brass box with a stiff lid, on a set of buttons that never did anything at all. Most of it failed. Some of it worked for an hour and then quit. He kept at it anyway, because he could not stop thinking about the vendor in the New Spring square, up on his crate with a longsword raised, and the fire running up the steel.

He forged the dagger himself. It took most of two classes to get the edge right and nearly all of a third to engrave it. The runes ran along the flat of the blade in a single line, small, close, and as precise as he could make them. He went over every stroke twice.

It was a Tuesday, or it might have been a Thursday.

He held the dagger above the stone bench, away from everything that could burn, and pushed his magyk into the grip.

Nothing happened. Then a thread of flame woke at the guard and crept up the blade. It was blue at the bottom and yellow at the top, no taller than a finger, and it barely reached the point before it gave out. Nobody at the next bench even raised a head from their filing.

It was a weak version of the vendor’s sword. The vendor would have been embarrassed for him.

Bal kept the warm dagger across his knees until the bell rang, and he would not have traded it for that sword or for any other blade on the red cloth.

That evening he lit it for the others in the dorm, over the hearth to be safe. The flame climbed its finger’s height and died. Lora asked to see the runes and read them off the blade rune by rune, under her breath, checking each against her notes. Amelia held it by the guard and turned it to the firelight, and said it was lovely work, and Bal believed her.

* * *

The arrow shirts took him longer. He bought the materials in New Spring and worked the runes in over several nights. Then he charged each rune himself, a few each evening until his hand ached, because a rune would only turn one arrow. After that the girls wore them under their clothes on every job. Viridia’s was green.

* * *

The lake was in the last week of the six.

They found it coming home from a job, in a fold of the hills where none of them had thought to look. It was long and narrow and very still, with reeds along one shore and a slope of pale rock along the other. The water was so clear that the bottom showed, dropping away from the rocks into blue, and then into nothing.

They stopped to drink and fill the skins. Hopper went out until the water reached her chest.

The water behind her rose.

It rose in a long smooth hump with no splash at all, as if the lake were standing up, and then it broke, and there was a head under it. The head was as big as a rowboat. It was green-black and wet, with a long mouth that went nearly back to where its neck should have been. The neck kept coming. Bal had his rapier out, and he knew while he drew it that it was useless. Lora had hers out too. They were standing on a rock with a thin sword each, looking up at something that could have swallowed them both without bothering to chew.

Hopper was out of the water before Bal saw her move.

The snake turned its head after her. Its body ran away behind it under the surface for a distance Bal did not want to guess at.

Viridia stepped past him to the edge of the rock and lifted her right hand.

The lightning left her palm with a crack that hit Bal in the teeth. It struck the lake beside the snake’s neck, and the whole lake lit from inside, every reed, every stone on the bottom, the snake’s full length laid out in it like a road. The smell came after, sharp and hot.

The snake sank, silent. Some time later it floated up again, slowly, belly first, and stayed up.

Viridia lowered her hand.

“Well. I won’t be filling my water skin here.”

Amelia, who had not moved from the bank, applauded.

Until that day Bal hadn’t seen Viridia use her own power.

* * *

The war was a different matter.

The stone Thad had given him lived in Bal’s pocket, and over those weeks it glowed red again and again. Each time, Amelia took him to the hidden temple so he could tap it against Thad’s and hear the news. The news was bad every time.

Raizen had found his strategy. He had released a statement through his High Priest, in his own territory. It said that Raizen and the others had shut Amelia away because she had started to go mad, and had attacked them, and they had been left with no choice. It went on. Since Raizen had been the one to suggest it, it said, now that she was free, it would be his followers who were in danger.

That one statement did a lot of work. Raizen’s army had always been going to follow orders. Now it was afraid of a mad vampire coming after it, and it trained harder for being afraid. And the villages, which had never liked anyone who bore the blood sygl, had gone past not liking. Blood sygls who would not leave their villages were being beaten. Some had been killed.

Thad told him all of it plainly, standing in the same narrow room each time with the same dark circles under his eyes, a little deeper at every visit. Then he waited. He wanted orders, and to hear how they could help.

Amelia could not get involved. So Bal went to the commander of the army.

* * *

The commander’s name was Fern. He was short and stocky, with a long gray beard and intelligent gray eyes, and he carried himself in a way that made his room feel like his before you were properly through the door. A long table filled the middle of it. A desk in one corner was buried in paper, and a lamp had been left burning there in the middle of the day. On the table lay a map of Raizen’s territory, weighted at the corners with stones.

“There’s not much we can do,” Fern said. “It’s in Raizen’s territory. We could send the army in, but it would be pointless.”

He did not dress it up.

Bal stood over the map. The wood around its edges was very dark and very old, and his fingers drummed on it while he read. The map showed where each of Raizen’s troops was posted. Amelia had found them all. It had no mark on it anywhere for a village.

“The problem is,” Bal said, “even if we could evacuate them, we don’t have anywhere to put them.”

“No,” Fern said. “We don’t.”

The death toll was growing every day. Bal had made his peace with the fact that people were going to die in this war. It had taken him a while to get there, but he had. That did not mean he was going to stand by.

“Could we do that to them?” Fern was thinking it through out loud. “See how they like it?”

“We will not respond in kind.”

Fern met Bal’s eyes and left the idea where it lay.

“Do we know where any of their priests are?” Bal asked.

“We do. Raizen has only three regular priests, and one High Priest. The High Priest hardly ever leaves his palace in Pashar.” Fern set a thick finger on Pashar, then moved it. “The others travel. But the regular priests get together once a month, at an inn in Londra.”

Bal’s eyes came off the map. “How did you find that out?”

“Spies, of course.” Fern sounded faintly surprised to be asked. “We’ve got a team with no sygls. They pose as laborers. A man with nothing on his palm can walk into any town he likes, and nobody gives him a second look.”

Fern’s people had learned the priests met monthly to share news and gossip. And to drink, Bal suspected.

“Are your men still in Raizen’s territory?”

“No. They’ve only been back a few days, though. They wanted more instructions.” Fern frowned into his beard. “They hate seeing what’s happening over there.”

Bal’s fingers had stopped. He measured the distance from Pashar to Londra with his spread hand, and then measured it again.

“Send them back,” he said. “Learn how often the priests meet with the High Priest.” With the priests gone, the High Priest would be easier to deal with. “Do we know if the High Priest has any special inheritance gift?”

“Wait here,” Fern said.

He went to his desk, picked up a stack of papers and thumbed through it, licking his thumb every few pages. Halfway down he found the one he wanted. He left the rest on the desk and returned with it.

“His name is Toma,” Fern read. “Fifty-six. No known family, but several concubines that he keeps at his palace. He’s considered a moderately powerful mage. No inheritance gift.”

The map had not changed while Fern was gone.

Aren’s territory bordered Raizen’s. Amelia had assured him Aren would not step in. That much he could put out of his mind.

Toma was another matter. Bal couldn’t work out why Raizen would make someone his High Priest with no gift at all. Granted, Bal didn’t have one either, as far as he knew. But his case was different.

He knew a fair amount about Raizen by now, from the books. Raizen did not like a direct fight, and avoided one whenever he could. He sent his troops out in squads to strike and then pull back, strike and pull back, over and over, wearing the enemy down. That was partly how Naisura had beaten him in the last war. Naisura’s army had marched straight at him and kept coming, and never gave Raizen’s squads the time to retreat or regroup.

Bal stood over the little marks that were Raizen’s squads until Fern said his name.

* * *

He had asked Amelia once how they would even know if they had won. Short of killing Raizen’s whole army, he had meant.

She had been at the foot of his bed, propped on her elbows. She explained it as though it were a game she had seen other people play for ages.

Once war was formally declared, neither originator could make another High Priest, nor any new priests. Once an originator’s priests and High Priest were dead, the war was over. Technically, anyway. The victor could go on killing the other’s army if they wanted. It did not matter, at that point.

The part about the army had stayed with him longer. The war could be over and men could still be killed in it, simply because the winner felt like going on. He had known straight away that he would never be that kind of winner.

And once the war was finished, the victor could demand something from the originator who had lost.

“Have you decided what you’d demand?” Bal had asked. “If we won?”

Amelia had smiled at him, slowly.

“Oh, yes. They are bound to give me what I ask, so long as it does not physically harm them.” She had paused there. “And I know exactly what I shall demand.”

He had spent most of an hour at it. He had tried asking straight out, and guessing, and pretending not to care. All she would say was that if he wanted the answer, he would have to win.

* * *

Now, with Fern’s orders given, Bal was in his dorm with a pad of paper on his knee, making notes on what he might do about Raizen’s High Priest. Most of them he crossed out. Toma had no gift of his own, which ought to make him easier, and kept making him harder, because Bal still had no idea why Raizen had chosen him. The priests met in Londra once a month. Pashar was far from Londra. He wrote that down twice without meaning to, and crossed one out. The history book he had been using for ideas was open beside him on the cushion.

The girls were curled up on the plush couches with their rune homework, comfortable as cats. Now and then Lora leaned over to check one of Viridia’s lines, and Viridia let her. Hopper, shrunk to fit the room, lay across both their feet and twitched in a dream.

Amelia was on the rug by the hearth with her feet folded under her skirt. She hadn’t spoken since dinner. She was staring into the fire as if there were something in it she was reading, and Bal, who had been glancing up at her between notes, saw her lips part.


CHAPTER NINETEEN

The Rule

“It’s going to happen tomorrow.”

Bal’s pencil skidded off the end of a word. He had been half listening to the fire for so long that a voice coming out of it startled him.

Amelia hadn’t stirred from the hearth.

“What’s going to happen tomorrow?”

“That school rule you were talking about a while back.”

On the couch, two heads lifted from the rune homework at once. Viridia’s book slid an inch down her lap before she caught it.

“I heard Mr. Porter talking to Mr. Winters about it earlier,” Amelia said. “Quite excitedly. Apparently, when the originators called the teachers in this morning, Mr. Porter told them that neither Lora nor Viridia spends any time with her own sygl anymore.” She turned her head at last, enough to find Viridia. “Plus you have a bloodwolf familiar. That’s what did it, I think. Raizen wouldn’t want anyone who carries his rarest inheritance gift walking around with something so clearly connected to me.”

Viridia said nothing.

“The others left the decision to Raizen,” she added. “Kenmei didn’t care either way.”

Kenmei was Lora’s originator. Bal waited for it to hurt her.

Lora considered the news about Kenmei for perhaps a second, and found nothing in it worth keeping, and dropped it.

That surprised him more than anything Amelia had told him yet.

“So Raizen asked Mr. Porter if he had any suggestions,” Amelia said, “and he did. Ban students from socializing with other sygls, except in class, or when they’re off the grounds.” She paused. “He also said they should make you release Hopper.”

That last was to Viridia.

Hopper was asleep across both girls’ feet, shrunk small by her collar, and she went on sleeping.

“Can they do that?” Viridia’s voice climbed as she said it.

“As long as your originator doesn’t care, yes, they can,” Amelia said. “Raizen gave him permission. So that will be something they’ll talk to you about tomorrow.”

* * *

The rumors had reached Bal weeks ago. He had hoped they would stay rumors.

Neither girl spoke. Lora’s pencil had dropped to the page and dragged a long gray line straight through a rune she had spent ten minutes on. Viridia’s hand had found Hopper’s back and rested there, flat, in the thick red fur.

Tomorrow they could be told to choose: stop coming around Bal, or be expelled. And Viridia would have to send Hopper away besides.

Six weeks had done something to the four of them. Saturdays on the road, the bear, the snake, the stew at midnight on the square. Bal couldn’t have named the day it happened, but somewhere in there none of them could have stood to lose the others.

He reached over and closed the history book. It had given him a few ideas about Toma, and Toma could wait. He squared it with the table’s edge, though nobody had asked him to, and swung around on the cushion toward the girls.

He had known for weeks what he would say, if it came to this. How Viridia would react, he still couldn’t guess.

He was surer about Lora, and that was the problem. Every single member of Lora’s family bore the Waves sygl, and had since before she was born. Her father had served in Kenmei’s army. Viridia’s village was different. Sygls were rare enough there that one more strange one might not matter as much. Hopefully.

“There is a way to circumvent the rule,” Bal said. “But it is not something to take lightly.”

His voice was less sure than he wanted. He heard it himself. So did they; Lora’s face went still, and Viridia sat forward and lifted her palm from Hopper’s fur.

“What is it?” Lora asked.

Bal looked to Amelia.

She was going to let him handle this on his own. He thought he understood why. If they said no, it would hurt less if she had never been part of the asking.

“They can only enforce the rule,” he said, “because we all belong to different sygls. But you could become blood sygls.”

Lora and Viridia looked to each other, then back at him.

“What exactly do you mean?” Viridia asked. “How would something like that even be possible?”

“As High Priest of the Blood sygl, I can convert someone. Change their sygl to blood.” He made himself go slowly. “But only if they choose to. It can’t be done to anyone who doesn’t want it. This isn’t something many people know.”

“Would I lose my inheritance?” Viridia asked.

“No. You wouldn’t.” He hesitated, then gave her the rest of it. “Actually, it’s possible to gain another inheritance gift.”

They were both quiet, weighing it. It scared him a little how badly he wanted to hurry them. He said the hard parts instead.

“Look, you need to think carefully about this. There’s no way to reverse it. And you have to think about your families too. You both know how a lot of blood sygls get treated.” His mind went to the villages, to Thad’s narrow room and the bad news. He pulled himself back out of it. “I care about both of you, and I would absolutely hate to lose you. But I don’t want either of you to be unhappy.”

* * *

Viridia twisted the earth ring on her finger, around and back, thinking.

“Would anything change?” she asked.

“It’s a bit different for every mage, from what Amelia has told me. Our sygl mainly gives us access to other magyks, ones our normal magyk doesn’t reach. It’s easier for me to control blood, even to use it as a weapon. You might lose some of your storm magyk.” He remembered the lake glowing to the bottom, and the snake floating up belly first. “Honestly, I doubt it. Not with your inheritance. Lora will lose her waves magyk, though.”

Lora waved that away.

“I’ve hardly ever had a reason to call on that side of my magyk. So that isn’t important.” Then, more carefully: “The teachers can’t retaliate, can they? If we do this?”

“Nope. I mean, what else could they do? You won’t be in their sygl anymore. And if a Lead Mage tries anything, I’ve got an in with the Blood Lead Mage.”

Viridia let out a pained sigh. Lora laughed, a short helpless burst she tried to catch in her sleeve and couldn’t.

When it had gone, nobody filled the quiet. The fire settled. Hopper stretched in her sleep until her claws ticked against the floor.

It was Viridia who broke it.

“We heard what you told Amelia in the pass,” she said.

Bal’s ears went hot to the tips. Amelia’s shoulders did something small under her dress that might have been a laugh she was keeping to herself.

Nobody asked him to say anything about it. He did not try.

“I can’t say how my father will feel,” Lora said. Her chin was up, and her voice did not hedge anywhere. “But I know he loves me. My family will stay by me. I’ll do it.”

“Yeah, I’ll do it too. I can’t have them trying to take Hopper from me,” Viridia said.

Nobody said a thing. Even Amelia came around from the fire for that one.

“Hey. I thought it was obvious I didn’t want them taking you from me either.” Viridia elbowed Lora, lightly. “Especially Miss Book Smart here.”

Lora was smiling at the teasing. Bal was glad to see it. The girl he had met in Warfare class would have hidden in a book until the conversation was over.

“Okay. So, last chance,” Bal said. “Is this something both of you want to do?”

“Yes,” said Lora.

“Yes,” said Viridia, at the same moment.

Amelia was smiling a little.

“Okay, then,” Bal said. “Let’s do it.”

* * *

He told them to come and sit on the tabletop in front of him, and went to fetch his dagger.

The bedroom was dark and smelled of his own clean laundry. He found the dagger by feel, next to the lamp, and waited a minute in the dark with it.

He had never done this before. Amelia had taught him, weeks ago, with her fingernail drawing a line across his palm. He went over it again, step by step, from the cut to the end.

He went back out.

The girls sat shoulder to shoulder on the tabletop, close, their knees almost touching his. Hopper was awake now, her muzzle resting across Viridia’s thigh, watching the dagger.

“Hold out your left hands,” he said. “Palm up.”

They did. Lora’s Waves sygl, Viridia’s Tempus sygl, in the middle of each palm. He had seen those hands across a hundred tables and had not thought twice about what was on them.

He meant to change one thing from her instructions. He would do them both at once.

He cut his own left palm first, and deep. It stung, then mostly stopped. Blood welled up and pooled in the cup of his hand, dark against the sygl.

He offered the dagger hilt first. His hand was not quite steady, and he saw both girls notice. Nobody mentioned it.

Viridia took it, and drew it across her palm without a sound, and passed it on. Lora cut herself with a neat, quick stroke. Then she wiped the blade on her pant leg and laid it aside, out of everybody’s way.

Three left palms, open, bleeding.

Bal reached for his blood.

It rose from his palm when he called it, and he split it into two thin streams, red threads in the firelight, and sent them over to the girls’ waiting palms. Where his blood touched theirs, theirs gave way. Their blood retreated into the cuts. It went back so far that in a breath both palms were bare, no blood and no sygl. Their hands were clear and white from the heel to the fingers. The only red on them was his, sinking into the cuts.

He pushed it deeper.

The cut in his palm was nothing. The pushing was the work. It had to be steady and it had to be gentle, in two people at the same time, and those two people were Lora and Viridia. Sweat ran down behind his ear. He did not wipe it. He did not dare take his mind off either thread for long enough to lift a hand.

It went in. Through it, he knew how far there still was to go. He followed it down until something in each of them pushed back, warm and dense and theirs, the place where their magyk lived.

Bal grunted and leaned on it, and his blood rolled over theirs and took hold.

The cuts closed. They shut together, both of them, and pinched off his blood in midair. The two red threads fell back into his hand.

On the two white palms, something was coming up from underneath. It was faint at first, a shadow of a line, then another line beside it. It darkened while the three of them watched, without anybody saying a word. Viridia held her hand up to the firelight. Lora had forgotten to breathe. Her glasses had slid to the end of her nose and she let them stay.

Some minutes later the marks on their palms were the blood sygl, as dark as Bal’s own.

* * *

Amelia had not left the hearth, her knuckles white in her lap. Now she was leaning toward them. Her eyelids came down. Her mouth curved.

“I can feel you now.”

Bal was on his feet. He hauled both girls into his arms and kissed them.

“Thank you,” he said. His voice cracked halfway, and he had to start it again. “If I’d lost you, I wouldn’t have known what to do.”

Viridia muttered something into his shirt that he did not catch. Lora’s forehead was pressed hard against his collarbone.

The three of them squeezed onto the couch with Amelia. She had left the rug to claim the end of it, and for a while nobody needed to say anything. The fire popped. Lora kept her left hand open in her lap, palm up, and could not stop reading it.

Then Amelia did something Bal had not expected. She rose and stood before the girls.

“I’m making you priests,” she said. “Now. It will give you more authority over my followers. And it will be good for Bal to have two priests he can trust completely.”

Lora made a small, astonished sound. Viridia, for once in her life, had no comeback.

“Bal,” Amelia said. “I’ll call a meeting of the originators and formally declare war on Raizen. It will limit some of the things he might try. Making another High Priest, for one. And it has to be now, before word of this spreads in the morning.”

She gathered all three into a hug and held on hard. When she stepped back, she was gone.

Hopper padded over from the table and claimed the spot where Amelia had been with a groan of deep contentment.

* * *

The room was much quieter.

In the morning, Bal thought, Mr. Porter would carry his new rule into the hall at breakfast, bring it to their table, and learn it was too late. Bal discovered he was looking forward to breakfast.

“You know,” he said, “I’ve only got one bedroom.”

He did not mention that he could have had another before he reached the door. The dorm would have made it for him gladly.

The girls traded a glance. They grinned, both of them.

“Then let’s go to bed,” they said together.


CHAPTER TWENTY

The Blood Whip

The new sweaters were black, and each carried the blood sygl between the shoulder blades in dark red, where Kenmei’s wave and Raizen’s lightning had been the day before.

Lora had buttoned the collar of her blouse to the throat under hers and then undone the top button again. Viridia had looked at herself in the bedroom mirror from the front and then twisted around to see the back, twice, and said nothing about it either time. Bal had not said anything either. He had been too busy trying not to grin like an idiot at the pair of them.

They walked to breakfast with one of them on each side of him. The corridors were nearly empty by then. Everyone else was already eating.

He pushed both doors open and held them so the girls could go in first.

The noise of the hall faltered. Heads came around, a few at first and then whole benches of them. Then the heads stayed turned. They had seen Lora’s back, and Viridia’s.

The Tempus students ate at the table beside Bal’s own, the last in the hall, and the three of them had to pass every bench of it to reach their places. A girl near the end of it stopped with a spoon of oatmeal halfway up and stared at the sygl sliding past her. She dropped the spoon and drove her elbow into the ribs of the boy beside her.

He was older than the three of them, a fourth year, with fair hair cut short and neat and a prefect’s straight back. He was halfway into his eggs. He turned on the bench, annoyed, and found what she was pointing at.

“Viridia,” he called. “What’s the meaning of this?”

Viridia slid into her usual seat across from Bal and pulled a slip toward her. She began to write her breakfast order.

“What’s the meaning of what, Sean?” she asked, still writing.

So that was the jerk. Bal had wondered.

Up at the teachers’ table, Mr. Porter’s glasses flashed toward them.

“Is there a problem, Mr. Kiin?”

Sean did not answer him. He was up and into the aisle, and before Bal could get a word out he had Viridia’s arm in one fist and was hauling her out of her seat. Her pencil skidded across the order and tore it.

Bal was already moving. Nothing in him stopped to decide.

His right palm lifted from the table, open, and the blood leapt from it.

The middle of the palm split open on its own, a clean line he never had to cut. Blood magic had no need of the sygl hand. The blood poured from the split and did not fall. It ran out into the air in a long dark rope, as thick as two of his fingers and bright where the light from the high windows caught it. It cracked over the table between the plates. It struck Sean in the chest and knocked him off Viridia and back two staggering steps, and then it was gone, drawn back into Bal’s hand, and nothing on the table had been touched except Viridia’s torn breakfast order.

Viridia rubbed her arm once, hard.

“I’d go sit back down if I were you, Sean.” Bal kept his voice level. “She’s not your concern anymore.”

Every table heard it. There was not a sound anywhere else in the room to cover it.

“Not my concern?” Sean was blotchy, red across the cheekbones and white around the mouth. “Not my *concern*? Who are you to tell me she’s not my concern, you filthy blood mage? She’s a Tempus. She’s not going around wearing your sygl. I’m her Lead Mage, and I’m telling her to go back where she belongs.”

Mr. Porter left his chair as soon as Sean’s voice went up. He was on his way to them with his napkin still in his hand, and when he reached them he was smiling. It was a small, nasty smile, and Bal suspected he had been saving it since last night.

“He’s right, Mr. Norheart. The originators have instituted a new school rule.” He let it sit there. “No sygl may socialize with another sygl unless they are in class or off the school grounds. As neither of those applies to you at present, Ms. Vizer, I’m afraid you will have to sit with your sygl.” The glasses swung to Lora. “That includes you, Ms. Aegis.”

Neither girl moved.

Lora had her hands folded on the table. Viridia had found a fresh slip and was smoothing it out flat.

Sean lunged for her again.

He never reached her. The blood was out of Bal’s palm faster than Sean could close his hand, and this time it did not strike. It wrapped. It went twice around Sean’s wrist and cinched there, and Bal pulled.

Sean came over the table on his stomach. Plates went skating off both sides of it. A pitcher of milk tipped and emptied into Lora’s lap, and she did not even flinch. A bowl of oatmeal cartwheeled onto the bench and a fork clattered to the flagstones somewhere behind Bal, and Sean fetched up with his face a foot from Bal’s own, hauling against the blood with all his weight and getting nowhere.

“What part of ‘she’s no longer your concern’ didn’t you understand?” Bal asked him quietly.

Sean was trying to work his wrist free. The blood was wet and warm and slick under his fingers, and he could not get a grip on it.

“Now look here, Mr. Norheart.” Porter wasn’t enjoying himself anymore. “He is her Lead Mage. You have no right to interfere. Release him at once.”

Bal released him.

He did it by flinging his right arm back behind him. The blood went with it, and Sean went with the blood. He sailed across the aisle and landed on his own table halfway along, on his back, and kept going. He slid down the length of it through the Tempus breakfasts. Toast went. A plate of sausages went. Somebody’s teacup went flying off the table’s end well ahead of him, and when he came to rest there was a clear lane through the center of the wood where the food had been, and he was lying in a puddle of tea with jam on one sleeve.

The blood came home to Bal’s palm, and the split there closed over behind it until the skin was smooth and dry again.

He stood up. Lora and Viridia rose with him. He had not asked them to.

“What you don’t understand,” Bal told Mr. Porter, “is that I’m their Lead Mage now. Not that fool.”

The hall broke into talk everywhere, everybody asking everybody else, and the noise climbed to the rafters.

“That’s impossible,” said Mr. Porter.

Bal glanced back at the girls. “Girls?”

They lifted their left hands, one and then the other, and turned the palms out to the room. The blood sygl was on each of them, dark as his own. Viridia held hers up and turned in a slow half-circle so the tables behind her got a look too. Lora held hers still, pink to the ears, and her arm did not waver.

“I assure you, it’s very possible,” Bal said. “Now, are you going to punish Sean for interfering with a member of another sygl, Mr. Porter?”

Mr. Porter found no answer at all.

“Of course not,” Bal said. “Why would you punish one of your own sygl?”

* * *

He had not meant to go on. But the hall had gone quiet again to hear what came next, the Terra and Waves tables and the teachers with their forks halted, and he found he had more.

“I know you’ve heard a lot of bad things about blood mages,” he said. “So let me set the story straight, here and now. Blood mages aren’t any crazier than anybody else in this hall. The stories saying so are lies, and people like Mr. Porter here keep spreading them so you’ll keep hating us.”

Mr. Porter’s napkin had been crushed into a ball in his fist.

“Lady Amelia didn’t abandon her followers. She was betrayed by her own family. They shut her away for something they believed she’d done, and she hadn’t. That was all it took. Instead of getting to the bottom of it, the originators, all of them, sacrificed the lives of their High Priests to trap her.” He let them hear that. “That’s the kind of originators you have. You mean nothing to them. At least Lady Amelia is here. We can speak to her. Where’s Raizen? Where’s Naisura, even? You see them one day a year and beg them to notice you.” He swung around on Mr. Porter. “And yet you wonder why anyone would want to change sygls.”

“The originators won’t stand for this,” Mr. Porter spat.

“Maybe. Maybe not. We’ll see. What I do know is that as of this morning, Lady Amelia’s officially declared war on Raizen.”

All along the Tempus benches, faces went pale. One boy closed his fist over his own palm. Even Mr. Porter looked shaken.

Bal laughed. It got out of him on its own.

“Did you honestly think she’d let what they did to her go?” he asked. “You’ve got no idea the trouble that’s heading your way.”

He looked out at the rest of them then, the whole long hall of them, the tables full of first years who had been told what Bal was before he had ever said a word to them.

“If anyone else is tired of feeling like they don’t belong,” he said, “or doesn’t want to be a pawn in someone else’s game, then the Blood sygl will welcome you with open arms.”

* * *

Sean had peeled himself from the wreck of breakfast. He was standing beside it, dripping, with a hand pressed to the small of his back.

Bal walked over to him. He did not hurry. He stopped so near that Sean had to lift his chin a little to meet his eyes, and once he had them he did not let them go.

“You know the rule,” he said. “Only Lead Mages may duel on school grounds. If you bother either one of my girls again, I’ll call you out, and I will kill you.”

Bal heard himself say it, and it occurred to him that Amelia was rubbing off on him.

Oh well.

Sean’s throat moved. He gave one short jerk of his chin that might have meant yes and might only have meant please stop talking to me, and then he went and dropped onto his bench in the tea.

Bal started for the doors, and the girls fell in beside him. Nobody on any bench said a word to them as they left.

* * *

Once the doors closed behind them, the girls talked nonstop. Viridia did Sean sliding down the table with her flat hand, five times at least, with sound effects, while Lora tried to wring the milk from her skirt and kept having to stop to laugh.

Bal walked between them and only half heard.

He had never had much of a temper as a kid. But when Sean had grabbed Viridia, Bal had wanted to kill him. Not hurt him. Kill him. It would not have taken much, either. A dart of blood in the eye would have done it before Sean had her out of the chair.

The skin of his palm still prickled where it had split.

Maybe he’d ask Amelia about this new temper later.

* * *

Amelia was waiting for them in the dorm, humming to herself. She had been to the originators in the night, and she had enjoyed their faces enormously when she made her declaration.

“Raizen tried to act as though he hadn’t seen it coming.” She sat on the arm of the couch. “As if I couldn’t tell. He was hoping to find all of my priests first, before I could declare war on him.” That made her laugh out loud.

Then she turned to Lora and Viridia.

“I’ve got two more priests besides you. Come with us tonight and you’ll meet them.”

Bal was supposed to meet Xavier tonight anyway, to go over strategies for taking out Raizen’s priests. Amelia waved them all to the couch and asked whether he’d had any more ideas.

“Yeah. Some of it depends on what they’ve found out for me, though. Tomorrow the two-week break starts. Hopefully we can get some things done.”

“How did this morning go? I wasn’t expecting you back so soon.”

The girls told her. They told her all of it, together and over each other, how Bal had stopped Sean with a blood whip and how he had stood up before everyone and as good as invited anybody who wanted to leave their sygl to come over to theirs.
