There had been some interested faces at the tables. Whether anyone would actually take him up on it, nobody could say.

Amelia heard them out, and when they were done she gave Bal a long look.

“After only a few days of practice,” she said.

The whip was a common weapon among blood mages. It still took concentration and willpower to use well. Bal meant to start Lora and Viridia on it soon.

After that the talk went to the war. Amelia and Bal laid out for the girls some of what Raizen had been doing in his villages, and the girls listened without interrupting, which was not like either of them.

“It’s honestly a good thing hardly anyone in your village gets a sygl,” Bal said to Viridia. “He’ll probably leave your village alone.”

Viridia did not answer that.

“Any idea where Londra is?” he asked.

Viridia thought about it with her eyes shut. “It’s not ringing any bells. Why?”

He told her. It was where Raizen’s three priests met each month.

“Does anyone know when they meet next?” Lora asked.

“I don’t. That’s one of the things I’m hoping we’ll find out tonight. Now that the war’s officially started, nobody else can be made a priest, or High Priest either. Once every priest and High Priest of an originator is dead, the war’s over.”

The girls both looked to Amelia.

“You mean the war hinges on us staying alive?” Lora said. “Not the army. Us?”

Amelia let the silence answer her.

Lora was quiet for a second.

“Talk about no pressure.”

* * *

It was a lot to hand anyone before lunch. Amelia saw that. She slid down off the arm of the couch into the cushions beside Lora and put on her brightest voice.

“Hey. We don’t have the meeting until tonight. Why don’t we do something fun until then?”

Bal admired her for it. “What were you thinking of?”

Amelia’s bright face wavered.

“Oh,” she said. “Um. I thought you might have an idea or something.”

He laughed and slung an arm over her shoulders and pulled her into his side, and she let herself be pulled.

“Hmm,” he said.

Outside the window the sky was clear down to the hills, the first truly hot day in a while. Somewhere past those hills lay a long, narrow lake with no snake in it anymore.

“Why don’t we go for a swim?” he asked.

Nobody had a better idea. The girls were up and through the bedroom door after their towels and their swimming things while he was still getting up himself.

“Can you take us to that lake we found last week?” Bal asked.

Amelia was smiling again, the real one.

“I can do that.”


CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE

Strawberries

Hopper would not go in.

Bal tried her from the shallows first. He waded into the lake up to his knees and slapped the water and called her, and she considered him from the warm rock at the edge. Then she considered the lake, from the near rocks all down its length to the far reeds, and lay down where she was and laid her muzzle across her paws.

“It’s dead,” Viridia told her, wading past with her elbows held high above the cold water. “I killed it. You were there.”

Hopper’s ears did not move.

A week ago there had been a snake in this water as big as a barn. Today there was a dragonfly, and a breeze puckering the surface out in the middle. Bal could not blame her. He gave up and went under.

The cold drove the breath out of him and then let it back in. He came up shouting, which Viridia thought was hilarious, and for a while after that he forgot about Hopper and everything else. They raced to a rock out in the shallows and argued about who had touched it first. Lora floated on her back with her glasses left safe on her towel, and drifted into Viridia, who splashed her. He dived for a stone Amelia dropped and surfaced with the wrong one, and she told him so.

When his fingers had gone wrinkled he climbed out and joined Hopper on the rock, still at her post. The stone was hot through his back. Hopper put her nose against his wet shoulder and resumed her watch on the water.

He kept her company.

It had taken a while for the girls to treat Amelia like any other girl. They had been careful around her for weeks. That was gone now. Lora ducked her without warning. Viridia climbed onto her back to be thrown off and was thrown off, and burst up spitting and demanding a rematch. The three of them shoved and shrieked and giggled as though Amelia were as human as they were, and Amelia shrieked loudest.

Her hair was plastered flat to her head and her face was wide open, and her smile made his heart hurt.

Three thousand years, and this was what she looked like underneath them.

The sun was hot on the rock. The girls’ voices carried across the lake and back, and somewhere among them he stopped hearing the words.

* * *

He woke to snoring.

It was soft, a small catch at the top of every breath. He opened his eyes and Lora’s sleeping face was inches from his own. She had left the water while he slept and come to share his rock, and her lashes were still wet. She had a dent in her nose where the glasses usually sat.

He closed the inch and kissed her forehead, lightly. She smiled for a second in her sleep and then forgot to.

Bal got an elbow under him to see where the others had gone.

Amelia was curled up against his left side, neat and small, her back to the sun. Viridia was stretched out above his head the long way, flat on her stomach, one arm flung out. Her right hand rested palm-up on the rock exactly where his head had been, reaching for it.

He bit down on the noise that wanted out of him and got clear of them without waking anybody.

The sun had crossed most of the sky. Hours, he guessed. The last time they were here, the day of the snake, he had seen a patch of strawberries on the edge of the woods, red under the leaves, and nobody had had time to stop.

He pulled on his shirt and pants. He caught Hopper’s eye and pointed at the girls, and Hopper thumped her tail against the rock. He climbed the slope to the woods still buckling on his rapier.

The woods were not thick. The trunks stood well apart, and the light came down between them in long slanting bars, but in places the branches knitted overhead and the ground went dim and cool. Birds were singing somewhere above him and kept on singing while he walked under them. He went slowly. The brambles caught at the legs of his pants as he waded through the weeds toward a flash of red he thought he had seen.

It was the patch. The berries were small and dark and warm from the day, and there were far more of them than he remembered.

Bal knelt in the briars and began to pick. The first three went straight into his mouth, which was only fair.

* * *

A branch snapped behind him.

That sound saved his life.

He twisted on his knees and there was someone there, right there, a shape against the light with steel in its hand already coming down. Bal threw himself sideways. He rolled to his right through the thorns, and they tore at his arms and his face, and he was grabbing for the rapier at his belt as he went, and it would not come clear. Blood magic was out of the question. He couldn’t have held one thought steady enough to call it. He burst from the briars and threw himself left, and the hilt finally came free in his hand.

Whoever it was, he was barely older than Bal.

He wore woodsman’s clothing, greens and browns that would vanish among the trees. His hair was short and brown. His eyes were blue and very sharp. He twirled his sword once, right-handed, easily, as if he had all afternoon.

“Almost had you there,” he said, conversationally.

He came in fast. He slashed to the right and then up. Bal let the sword slide along his rapier’s blade and twisted his wrist to wrench it out of the other’s grip, as Lora had drilled him all those mornings. His attacker pulled back before the move was finished, and laughed.

“You’ll have to do better than that,” he said. “How you ever managed to kill Heath with sword work that shoddy, I can’t imagine.”

The name meant nothing to Bal.

They traded blows. The stranger was quick and loose and he never stopped talking. On the fourth or fifth exchange his blade went past Bal’s guard and opened a long slice down Bal’s left side, and the shirt there went dark and began to cling.

“Oh, that’s got to hurt.” He was grinning. “Why don’t I see if I can even it up for you?”

It did not hurt yet. It would come later. He planted his boots.

They fought in earnest now. The thorns were at Bal’s back and the light kept going in and out of his eyes. Steel rang and rang. Bal took cuts he only knew about from the sting, and gave none, and gave none, and then the woodsman overreached on a lunge and Bal was inside it. He opened the man’s forearm. On the way back out he opened his ribs.

The grin went away.

He backed off a pace and touched the blood on his sleeve. When he spoke again his voice had changed.

“I was told to just kill you. But I think I’ll enjoy myself afterward. Those pretty girls of yours.” He waved his sword toward the lake, toward the sound of nothing, the girls asleep in the sun. “They’re nearby, aren’t they?”

Something in Bal went very still.

Lora, with the dent on her nose. Viridia’s hand open on the rock.

“Told by who?” he asked. He held his voice low, where it could not shake.

“Why, my High Priest, of course.” He seemed surprised that he had to say so. “He sent Heath to kill you, but Heath was past his prime. Now it’s my turn.”

He darted in again. He feinted right and swept low, and the blade bit deep into Bal’s right thigh.

Bal felt it. He gave one step back, only one, and swung his left fist into the man’s face as hard as he had ever hit anything.

The man had not been expecting that. His head rocked back and Bal kicked the sword free and hit him again. The man went scrambling after his weapon on hands and knees through the weeds, and Bal kicked him in the side, hard, and sent him into the nearest tree. He hit it with a sound like a dropped sack and slid to its foot.

The world got very quiet.

Bal could think now.

He turned the right palm out, and it parted down its middle with no more fuss than a mouth opening. The blood left it already moving. It went low across the clearing and curled once around the fallen man’s neck and drew snug there. Bal brought it in slowly, hand over hand without using his hands, and the man came with it across the ground on his heels. Then Bal lifted.

The woodsman rose up the trunk of the tree until his boots were kicking a foot off the ground.

“Tell me,” Bal said. “What’s your name?”

The man clawed at his throat. His nails scraped and scraped on the blood and found nothing to get under. It had set as hard as steel around him.

“It’s Iro,” he said. “It’s Iro.”

“How many of you did he send? And how’d you track us here?”

Iro had stopped fighting it. All the chatter had drained from him, and his face had gone young and scared.

“Just me and Heath. I swear. The High Priest told us we were a long shot, so it didn’t matter if we found you or not. Then one of the priests came to us the next day and said we’d be sent to wherever you were. They sent Heath first, because he had more experience. After you killed him, they decided to wait. Catch you off guard.” It poured from him in a rush. “They only told me an hour ago that I was coming here. No idea how they knew. I swear that’s all I know.”

How they had known Heath was dead, at least, Bal could guess. A red-gold heart on Heath’s right hand, back on the Whitehaven road. Somebody on Raizen’s side had been wearing its pair, and had felt it stop.

Sweat was running down Iro’s face into his collar. He had nothing left to hide behind. Bal believed him.

“Okay then.”

He closed the loop with a thought.

Iro’s body dropped to the leaves without its head, and lay still.

“No one threatens my girls,” Bal told it.

The blood returned across the clearing. It sank into his palm, and the skin closed over it, and his hand was only his hand again.

* * *

His shirt was finished. Iro had seen to that. It hung off him in strips, and it was wet down one whole side.

Bal dragged it off. He tied the sleeves together and knotted the tail, and knelt down again in the patch and picked the rest of the strawberries into it. There was no reason to waste them. Then he picked up his rapier and walked back down through the trees with the bundle in his other arm.

The berries bled through the cloth before he was halfway down. By the edge of the woods his forearm was sticky to the elbow, and which of the red on it was strawberry was anybody’s guess.

The girls were awake and talking when he reached the slope above the lake. Lora’s glasses were back on. She turned at the sound of him, lifted her hand to wave, and stopped with it in the air.

She was up and running. Her long brown hair flew out behind her.

“What happened?” She was shouting it before she reached him. Her hands were already on him, on his side, on his chest, turning him by the arm. “What *happened*?”

“Would you believe I fell on my rapier?”

Viridia and Amelia came up behind her.

Viridia took the knotted shirt from him and set it on the ground, so they could all see him properly. There was the long cut down his left side that was still oozing. There was a scatter of smaller ones over his ribs and stomach he had not even felt land. Viridia walked around him and found the one across his back, from his right shoulder to his left hip, and muttered something under her breath that Lora did not scold her for. There was the thigh. His pant leg was black with it to the knee. That was the one that would hurt most, now that it had started. The blade had gone deep into the muscle.

Amelia got him down onto the grass.

Lora was in her pack. She bandaged him fast and tight, the side and the back and the worst of the small ones, and she kept the healing potion back for the thigh. When she poured it in, he hissed through his teeth, and Lora said “Good,” fiercely, to the potion.

Amelia was facing the woods, and nothing friendly was left in her.

“It’s no use,” Bal said. “He’s already dead.”

“Explain,” said Amelia.

He told them. He had wanted to surprise them with strawberries, and he had nearly lost his head over it. He told them about the snapped branch, and the man in the woodsman’s clothes, and Heath, and the High Priest. He told them about the whip, and what Iro had said at the end of it. The girls let him get to the end.

“I just don’t get how they knew we were here,” he finished. “Heck, we didn’t even know we were coming here.”

Amelia studied him for a full minute. Then she was gone.

Nobody said anything.

She had done this before, gone off like that, unannounced. Bal hoped that this time she was going to get to the bottom of it.

* * *

Lora tied off the last bandage. She had used all the healing salve in the pot, and she held the empty pot upside down to show him, accusingly.

He untied the shirt. The strawberries had held up better than he had. He split them into piles on the grass, and made one pile for Amelia, on a flat stone where the ants would have to work for it.

They ate by the lake and talked and waited for her. Hopper had her chin on his good leg. Viridia wanted the whole fight again from the beginning, and he told it to her again, more slowly. She ate three of his strawberries while he did, without seeming to notice, and when he mentioned it she called them her fee for listening. Lora had no wish to hear it and listened anyway, and twice she retied the knot on his thigh bandage, which had been fine both times.

The strawberries were warm and a little squashed and they were good.

The shadows had gone long on the lake when Amelia came back. About forty minutes, Bal thought. She sat down cross-legged next to her pile of strawberries and began to eat them, singly, looking enormously pleased.

Bal gave her a minute.

“Did you find out anything?”

“Yep.”

She popped a strawberry into her mouth.

“And?”

Amelia laughed. She laughed until she was bent over her knees with it, until bloody tears ran down both her cheeks and she had to wipe them off with the backs of her hands.

“I wish you could have seen it,” she said.

She had gone to Raizen’s domain first, she told them, because she had been certain he was behind it. Raizen was not home. So she went on to the usual meeting place.

“They had pulled the teachers in again today,” she said. “And there was your Mr. Porter in the middle of them, explaining all about you converting Lora and Viridia. Raizen was furious. Positively purple.” She ate another strawberry. “The moment I arrived, they all started on me at once. My High Priest stealing their followers was the reason they locked me away before, and here it was happening again.”

“And you said?” Lora asked.

“That I had no part in what happened back then. And that I have given Bal my full permission to do whatever he thinks is right.” Amelia licked a thumb. “Then I walked up to Raizen. He was fuming. I told him that if he wouldn’t follow the rules everyone has agreed on for our wars, I would gladly take him on, right then, in front of all of them. All he had to do was say the word.”

Viridia had stopped chewing.

“The others had no idea what I was talking about. Raizen did. It was all over his face. So Aren asked me to explain. I did. Aren turned around and asked Raizen if it was true. And Raizen didn’t deny it.”

She paused for the space of one strawberry.

“Aren told me I could have one concession from them. I said I wanted the new school rule lifted. Naisura and Raizen both opened their mouths to argue, and Aren put up one hand, and they shut them again.” Her cheeks were still streaked. “Then he asked whether you meant to convert any more followers.”

Bal waited.

“I told him if you did, it would only be people you cared about. Not some great converting campaign, like the last High Priest. Aren considered it, and agreed.” She brightened all over again. “Mr. Porter stepped forward to object. And Aren snapped his fingers, and sent him back to the school. Gone, in the middle of a word. Then he told Raizen to keep his teachers in check.”

“And then?” Viridia said.

“Then, as I turned to go, Aren wished me good luck.” Amelia sounded amazed by it. “I think that surprised me more than anything.”

* * *

“Would you really go after Raizen?” Viridia asked.

“Yes,” Amelia said. “And they all know I would. That’s what will keep him in line.”

“So Raizen was the one telling them where I was,” Bal said.

“Yep. And then teleporting them in close to you.” Amelia brushed the green tops of the strawberries off her lap. “Once war is declared, the rules say the originators can’t help their followers. Thankfully, we have a loophole. I’m also your familiar, so I can help with certain things without breaking any rules. I’m fairly sure the others haven’t realized that yet.”

When the last strawberry was gone, Amelia popped home to the dorm and returned with fresh clothes for him. Bal changed behind Hopper, who did not care, and stood up in a clean shirt over all of Lora’s bandages.

Amelia took them to her temple next.

It was abuzz before their feet had properly landed. Men were racing through the hall with armfuls of spears. Someone was calling out names from a list. Half the army was getting ready to march somewhere, and nobody had told Bal where.


CHAPTER TWENTY-TWO

The Plan

Boots, mostly. That was what filled the place once Bal’s ears caught up with where they were. Hundreds of pairs on stone, down in the courtyards and on every flight of the long stairs, everybody bound somewhere in a hurry. Over the boots there was shouting, and over the shouting there was a steady clatter of metal being packed into spaces too small for it.

A soldier came past with a bundle of spears on each shoulder and had to turn sideways to fit through the arch. Two more trotted after him with a cooking pot slung on a pole between them. It swung and clanged against the doorframe, and the one in front swore at the one behind for it.

Along the wall below the stairs, a row of women sat rolling bandages into a basket that never got any fuller, because a boy kept coming to empty it. A soldier who could have been in Bal’s year at school sat on the lowest stair, lacing a pair of boots that were plainly somebody else’s.

Out past the arch, the big gate into the canyon stood open, and wagons were backed up to it in a crooked line. Men were heaving sacks of meal into the nearest one while a sergeant stood in the bed of it and told them they were doing it wrong. The air smelled of horses and lamp oil and wet rope.

Thad met them at the head of the stairs. He had his robe hitched up in one fist so he could take the steps two at a time, and a sheaf of papers crushed in the other.

“High Priest. Good.” He stopped to breathe. “I was about to set off the stone.”

“What’s going on?”

“Commander Fern’s sending a battalion to the edge of Raizen’s territory. It’s a few days’ hard march, and they won’t stop much on the road.” Thad leaned over the balustrade and pointed to the lowest courtyard. “That’s Eric. Fern’s given it to him.”

The man reading names off a list was young for the job. Not much past twenty, if that. He read each name in the same carrying voice, eyes on the paper while the soldiers answered him.

“The idea is to pull Raizen’s men into a fight,” Thad said. “Keep them busy at the front. While they’re looking at us, we get some of our people out of the border towns.”

“Blood sygls, you mean?”

“As many as we can. Anyone who’s willing to join the army, or help out around the camp, gets offered a place here.”

Thad’s stone had glowed often enough these past weeks with news from those towns, none of it good. Standing over Fern’s map, Bal had said they had nowhere to put anyone. Somebody had found somewhere. Bal liked that.

Below them, Eric reached the end of his page, flipped it, and kept going without missing a name.

* * *

Xavier stepped out of the priests’ door to check on the noise on the landing, which was how both priests met Lora and Viridia together.

“These are Amelia’s newest priests,” Bal said. “This is Lora Aegis. Viridia Vizer.”

Both priests stared at Viridia’s hair. It was hard to miss, that pale blue. Nobody in Raizen’s lands would ever mistake what it meant.

Viridia showed her left palm before anybody could ask.

“Converted,” she said. “Last night. It’s a long story.”

“It is. And it’ll keep,” Bal said.

Lora asked who trained the soldiers here. Thad told her the weapons master was down in the lower yard, if she could stand the noise, and she was halfway down before he finished giving directions. Viridia wandered outside with Hopper padding after her, and Bal heard someone below shout in alarm at the size of the wolf and then go quiet.

Amelia had kept to the doorway of the High Priest’s bedroom.

“I’ll stay put,” she said. “Better if nobody can say I was helping.”

“You don’t mind?”

“I’ll find something to read. Go have your war.”

The door closed behind her.

* * *

Next door was as Bal remembered it. The same table by the wall, the same three chairs, and now a fourth one dragged in from somewhere that did not match. Xavier took that one.

“So,” Bal said, once they were settled. “Tell me what we’ve got.”

Up close, both of them looked worn through. Thad’s eyes had sunk back in his face since the last time the stone had glowed. Xavier was gray in the face. From the creases in his robe, he had been sleeping in his chair.

“We’ve got a war is what we’ve got,” Thad said.

He read from the top sheet in front of him.

“Raizen’s sent out scouting parties. Big ones. Two of them found us. We managed to wipe out both before anyone could get word back, but this week’s been hard on everybody. We’ve put trained blood mages up on the cliffs, a full cohort, in case they get any clever ideas. Casualties were high after both attacks. We can hold out here a while. But the plain truth is they’ve got more of everything than we do, and their men are better trained. Ours won’t embarrass themselves. They just don’t have the experience Raizen’s have. We’re doing what we can. We can’t do it forever.”

Bal drew the papers over and read them himself.

They were reports on the two fights, written in a cramped clerk’s hand with the totals underneath. He went through each report and did the sum twice. For each of Raizen’s men they had killed, they had lost two. Sometimes three.

Thad was right. The blood sygl army did not have the men to go on fighting Raizen’s head to head.

“So where do we go from here?” he asked.

Xavier answered him. He had been waiting to.

“We have to send in a force to eliminate the priests. Then the High Priest. That is our only chance. Our spies have learned that Aleric, one of Raizen’s regular priests, has already started for Londra. He and the others meet in a week’s time, at the Three Boars Inn.”

There was a map under the reports. Bal pulled it out and drew it to him, and found the canyon, and then the town.

“How long would a team need, to reach it?”

Xavier came around behind Bal’s chair and leaned over him to show him the spies’ route. It went well out of its way to keep clear of the towns.

“Riding hard, four days. Five at the outside.”

* * *

The spies were the obvious answer. Fern’s people already knew the roads, and nobody looked twice at a man with an empty palm. But if they tried and failed, the army would lose its only eyes inside Raizen’s camp, and they would have to fight the rest of the war blind. If they succeeded, though, it would hurt Raizen badly. Possibly enough to end it.

He went around the problem one way and then the other. He was looking for any answer besides the one that had been sitting at the back of his mind since Xavier said *Londra*. There wasn’t one.

In the end he gave in. It was the only practical choice. It was also, he was almost sure, the last thing Raizen’s High Priest would ever expect.

“If somebody went in there carrying the Blood sygl,” he said, “what are the chances nobody would notice?”

Thad scratched at the stubble along his jaw. He had not had time to shave in days, by the look of it.

“Well, they aren’t checking everybody’s palms. Not so far as I know.” He considered. “Most of the violence has been against people already known to have the Blood sygl. And you have to understand, even before all this, your odds of finding one of us in his territory were low.” He corrected himself. “Further in, I mean. The border towns are different. Border towns in any territory get more mixed sygls. So the closer you get to Londra, or Pashar for that matter, the less anybody’s looking for one.”

“So it could work.”

“The problem is that whoever you send would be strangers there. Strangers get looked at.”

Bal thought of Viridia’s pale blue hair out on the landing.

“With how people there feel about blood sygls these days,” he said, “traveling in the company of an actual Tempus would help with that. Wouldn’t it?”

“I don’t see why it wouldn’t,” Xavier said. “What’s your idea?”

Bal told them.

Viridia still had her inheritance. The conversion had not touched it. That hair was a visible mark of Raizen’s favor, and nobody in Raizen’s lands was going to question its owner very hard. Londra was new to her. That shouldn’t matter much, if they kept their heads down.

The word *they* did not get past either priest.

“No,” Thad said.

Xavier took longer about it. “You are our High Priest. Raizen’s territory is no place for you, and you know it. If you go in and don’t come out again, who is left to finish this?”

“And if I stay here,” Bal said, “who keeps Viridia alive? Iro found us this afternoon at a lake nobody had planned to visit. Raizen’s next man won’t need a plan either.” He was not going to sit four days’ ride away and wait to hear how it went. “She goes with me or she doesn’t go at all. That’s the end of it.”

Thad was going to argue anyway, and thought better of it.

“Then we’d better give you everything we’ve got,” Xavier said.

He was already pulling papers toward him. Thad went to a chest in the corner for more. Between them they sorted out everything the spies had sent back on Londra and Pashar, and Bal left them to it and went downstairs to find the girls.

* * *

His thigh complained on every step. Lora’s potion had closed the cut, and the muscle under it had decided not to forgive him yet.

He found Lora climbing from the lower yard with her hair falling down and the fight still in her face. Viridia was not far behind, with Hopper ranging ahead of her through the press of soldiers. Nobody tried to stop her now.

Lora started talking the moment she spotted him and kept it up for most of the climb.

“Their weapons master’s name is Marcus. He’s good, Bal. Really good. He asked if I wanted to go a round, and I thought he was only being polite. He wasn’t. He made me work for every touch. I won, but honestly, it could easily have gone the other way. I got lucky on a feint. He went for it, and he shouldn’t have, and he knew it the second he did.”

“He went for it because you sold it,” Bal said.

“Maybe a little.” She was trying not to look pleased, and the corners of her mouth kept giving her away. When he asked what the feint had been, she showed him on the stairs with her empty hand and nearly caught a soldier coming down in the ribs.

Viridia had spent her time walking around among the soldiers.

“They wouldn’t talk to me at first,” she said. “You can see why. One look and they think Raizen’s sent me. So I showed them my palm. And then Hopper came over and lay down by my feet, and after that they couldn’t shut up.”

Her voice changed when she got to the rest of it.

“All of them I talked to know they’re probably going to die,” she said. “They know it. And they’re okay with it. They kept saying how thankful they were to Lady Amelia. For showing them they weren’t on their own anymore.” For a stretch of stairs she said nothing. “Some of them had never been out of their village before. Just the two years of war training everybody has to do, and then home again. They’re scared of getting hurt. They’re excited anyway.”

Bal let them talk. He put in a word now and then, but mostly he listened and climbed, his mind on men who had never been past the edge of their own village, marching toward Raizen in the dark.

* * *

Thad and Xavier both got up when the girls came in and bowed to them, slightly, from the waist. Lora went pink. Xavier made room for them at the table.

He had a single sheet in front of him now, written front and back. He read from it.

“Londra is a medium-sized city. It sits almost directly south of Pashar. Our spies think that’s one reason the priests picked it. It’s about central to each of their stations. The Three Boars is in the northwest of the city. It mostly caters to the upper class. When the priests come into town, they take the whole back half of the inn to do their business.”

“The inn gets a delivery of ale every other Wednesday,” Xavier went on. “This coming one falls on the day the priests should arrive. That might be something you can use. The innkeeper might be open to a bribe. Our people couldn’t tell either way. The guard in Londra is about what you’d expect for a city that size. They step up the patrols while the priests are visiting. Aleric is the one you’ll know on sight. He has an old scar that runs down over the right eye.”

“Rooms will be waiting for you at the Winespring Inn,” he added. “Lady Amelia will write ahead to arrange them. It’s on North Street, just past the turning for the Three Boars.”

He put the paper down.

“Any questions?”

The girls had none, and neither did Bal.

He laid out the plan.

Viridia was to be a young noble lady, going home to Pashar because the war had made the borderlands too dangerous for her. Lora had been around nobility. She could coach Viridia on the road. And Bal would be the lowly manservant who saw to the horses and kept them all safe.

“Listen. This is dangerous,” he said. “If either of you doesn’t want to risk it, I understand. Nobody’s going to think less of you.”

* * *

Lora had pulled the map over in front of her while he talked. She was studying the marks for Raizen’s troops and the line of the road they would have to take into Londra, and she was not hurrying. She had grown up with a father who had been in an army. She knew better than Bal or Viridia what the middle of enemy country meant.

“It’s probably our best shot,” she said finally. “Even if we sent another team, they’d likely be found out as blood mages. Unless you sent Viridia along with them, like you’re planning to do with us.”

She slid her eyes sideways at him. He kept quiet.

“Yeah,” she said. “Didn’t figure you would. So that leaves us doing it ourselves. And Amelia can’t come. The others would see it as interfering, so she’ll have to stay behind.”

Lora pushed the map away an inch.

“And then there’s me. What am I, in all this? To Viridia, I mean.”

“Her companion,” Bal said. “A friend from home. Somebody she’d travel with.”

“Then I’m a lady too, and ladies get introduced. The first priest who sits down with us asks whose daughter I am and where my father’s land is, and I don’t have an answer that lasts past the soup. Make me her maid. Nobody asks a maid anything. A maid can go out alone to the shops and the market, and nobody bothers to remember her face.”

Bal had pictured Lora riding in at Viridia’s side as near enough her equal. It was plain now how fast that would come apart at a supper table.

“Maid it is,” he told her.

Viridia tipped her chair onto its back legs and stretched her long tan arms up over her head until her shoulders popped.

“You know Amelia’ll hate this,” she said. “If we get caught, there’d be nothing she could do. Well.” She brought her arms down. “Nothing she *should* do. Honestly, when it comes to Bal, I think she’d take on every originator in one go.”

Bal laughed.

“I don’t think she’d mind,” he said. “She’s only waiting for them to give her a reason. You’re right, though. She won’t be happy. But I think she’ll understand. This is the best chance we’ve got of ending the war quickly. If we miss them at the inn, we’ll have to spend time hunting them down separately, and we don’t have that kind of time.”

Bal pushed his chair back and rose.

“See if you can find some noble lady’s clothes that’ll fit Viridia,” he told the priests. “And something right for Lora and me. After that, tell Commander Fern what we’re doing, and make sure there are good horses ready for us by noon tomorrow. We’ll leave then.”

He shook Thad’s hand, and then Xavier’s. Xavier’s grip lingered.

Then he went next door to tell Amelia that he was about to put himself within Raizen’s reach.

* * *

The girls trailed after him, guessing. Viridia bet on shouting. Lora bet Amelia would simply forbid it. Nobody had won yet when he went in.

Bal laid it all out for Amelia, from the beginning: Aleric, the inn, the noble lady and her maid and her manservant, the horses at noon.

Amelia heard him to the end in silence.

Her face went perfectly blank. He had known her furious, and he had watched her laugh herself into bloody tears, but until now she had never turned a face with nothing in it on him.

“No.”

That was all. The one word, flat as a table.

Behind him, Lora and Viridia burst out giggling. Whatever Bal’s face was doing, they were enjoying it very much.

He tried to explain why it had to be him.

“If we don’t catch them at the inn, we lose weeks. Maybe months. And every week this goes on, more of those men downstairs…”

“I heard you,” Amelia said. “All of it.”

Amelia was up and pacing, from the window to the washstand and back, with her skirt snapping around her ankles at every turn.

“Then get someone else to do it,” she said. “The plan will work just as well whether you’re there or not.”

Bal sighed.

“Would you want me to send Lora and Viridia with somebody else? Because you know I won’t.”

Amelia’s glance flicked to the girls on the bed, who were following every word. She turned at the washstand and paced back.

“No,” she admitted. “I don’t expect you to do that. But you’re my High Priest. Kill you, and there goes the war. They’re hunting for you even now.”

He knew it. Iro’s blade had opened his thigh to the muscle.

“Which is another reason it has to be me. They won’t expect me anywhere near his territory. Much less heading for the heart of it.” He looked to Lora for help. “Tell her, Lora. Isn’t it a good plan?”

Lora took a second to choose her words.

“I know you’d hate to admit it, but he’s right,” she said. “Nobody does a thing like this. Nobody would think of it. And with Viridia along, we should be able to get into Londra without too much trouble. The hardest part will be getting out again once the priests are dead. Unless we get extremely lucky and manage it without a huge fight. But all in all, it’s our best shot.”

No answer came from Amelia. She was by the bedpost now, and she looked from Lora to Viridia and then at Bal, and back again, trying to keep all three of them in sight at once. She had stopped pacing. She was afraid, and not only for him.

She would not stop them. She knew it, and so did he.

He stepped in behind her and wrapped both arms around her waist. She stayed rigid through one breath. Then she gave.

“If they kill you, I’m going after Raizen,” she warned him. Her voice was low and very even. “Damn their war. I will kill him.”

Bal chuckled into her hair.

“That’s my Warrior Goddess.” He gave her a squeeze and let her go. “Come on. Let’s go home and enjoy the time we’ve got left before we have to leave.”

He offered an arm. Lora and Viridia scrambled over and threw themselves around Amelia, one on each side, and the temple and all its boots went out like a lamp.

The dorm was dark and exactly where they had left it. Viridia shrank Hopper with her collar, and Hopper took the hearth rug. They lit the fire. Later there were cards, and Amelia lost at them loudly. She sat closer to Bal than she needed to all evening, her shoulder against his, and nobody remarked on it.

Noon and the road were hours off yet.


CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE

Road to Londra

Amelia saw them off at noon. Fern’s grooms had the horses waiting at the canyon gate, saddled and loaded, and she hugged Lora first and then Viridia and said something to each of them too low for Bal to catch. Then it was his turn. She held on to him longer than she had held either of them, and when she let go her fingers stayed on his collar a moment more, and straightened it, though it had been straight.

Hopper stayed behind with her. A bloodwolf at Viridia’s heel would have given the whole disguise away at the first gate.

Amelia stayed out on the road while they rode off. When Bal twisted around in the saddle at the bend, she was still there.

“Everyone else is beneath you,” Lora said. “Start there. It’s how most nobles feel, so it’s how you have to feel. Everyone.”

They had been riding an hour, three abreast where the road allowed it. Viridia sat in the middle in the clothes the priests had found for her, a gray riding dress with a high collar and more buttons than any one person needed. She had unfastened two of them before the canyon had dropped behind them. Under all those buttons she had on the green shirt, with every one of his runes in it charged. Lora had hers on under her maid’s dress. He hadn’t had to ask either of them.

“Everyone,” Viridia said. “The guards.”

“The guards. The innkeeper. The man who sells you a ribbon. Us. Especially us. A lady doesn’t thank her servants and she doesn’t explain herself to them. She doesn’t look at them at all unless she wants something.”

“Not looking at Bal? I’m a natural.”

“You’re not. You lean in when people talk. Don’t. Let them lean in to you.” Lora’s voice had none of its usual hedging. “And don’t say please. You can say thank you to someone important, once, and then never again. Short sentences. If you ask a question, ask it like you already know the answer and you’re checking whether they do.”

Viridia raised her chin and peered down her nose at Bal from the height of a horse that was a hand shorter than his.

“Water.”

Bal handed her his water skin. She drank and handed it back in silence.

“Better,” Lora said.

Viridia ruined it by grinning at him.

“And do your buttons up,” Lora said.

Bal rode with slack reins and listened to them. Lora went on to forks, and which ones, and Viridia asked why anyone needed three. Now and then he squinted up at the sun. They would have to push the horses soon if they meant to make Londra in time. He gave them another mile. Lora was enjoying herself, and Viridia was learning more than she let on.

The goodbye at the gate was still with him. It had gone straight through him. Since the mausoleum he had been fond of her, and amazed by her, and on one or two occasions a little afraid of her. Somewhere in these past weeks it had grown into more, and he had missed it happening until she was standing in the road getting smaller. He was falling in love with the Vampire Goddess.

Then there were these two. Lora was demonstrating how to hold a cup, with her pinky out, on a cup that wasn’t there. Viridia was copying her badly on purpose. What he felt for them grew every day too. He was amazed at his luck, constantly. One beautiful woman who wanted to be with him would have been plenty for an orphan from Saint Andre’s. He had found three.

He only hoped he could keep them safe. The plan was dangerous, and he had no illusions about everything going off perfectly. He had not seen any other way. And he was not about to trust their safety to anyone else.

* * *

The plan was to stay clear of every town until Londra. Most of the fighting was in the towns along the border, so they would be living on what Commander Fern had ordered packed for them: hard biscuit, dried meat, a wheel of cheese wrapped in waxed cloth, and oats for the horses.

When he judged that Lora had had long enough, Bal told them to quicken the pace, and he kicked his own horse into a gallop.

They held it until the horses had had enough. Then they slowed to a walk to let them blow, and then they galloped again. The country rolled past in long yellow stretches of grass, with a farm far off now and then and a line of trees along a stream. It was hot and the cheese went soft.

The first night, Bal had an armful of deadwood together before Viridia saw what he was doing.

“Put that down,” she said. “Out here? Anybody could see that smoke from a day off. Back home we always knew when the tinker was coming, because he cooked his supper every night on the road in. Half the village would be waiting for him at the well.” She tore off a piece of biscuit. “He thought we were just friendly.”

Bal turned to look. The grass ran flat and yellow to the edge of the sky all around them, and a single farm showed from miles away. He let the wood fall where he stood.

After that they made no fire at all.

* * *

They met no one on the road until the second day.

Up ahead, two people on horses were coming toward them, with two more on foot. When they were close enough, Bal saw that the riders were children: a boy on one horse and a girl on the other, too small for the saddles, their feet nowhere near the stirrups. A man and a woman walked beside them, one at each bridle. Their parents, Bal guessed.

The parents went stiff at the sight of Viridia’s hair. Bal raised a hand and called out to them, and they stopped anyway. They stopped the way people do when it would be worse not to.

Flies were bad on that stretch. The girl was swatting at them, and then the boy, and each time a small hand came up Bal saw the mark on its palm. Blood sygls. Both of them.

Bal’s own left hand had been born with that mark on it. Somebody had taken the hand off for it.

He could guess the rest.

“Any news from up ahead?” Bal asked. “What are things like?”

The father spat on the ground.

It landed in the dust between his boots and the hooves of Bal’s horse. His eyes never left Viridia.

“Same as everywhere else,” he said. “We got run out of our town a few days back. Just trying to make it to Kysmer. That’s the closest safe place for blood mages, around here.”

He took his son’s reins and started the horse walking. The mother went after him with the girl, and none of them said another word. The boy swatted at another fly.

Bal sat his horse until they were well down the road behind him. Kysmer was days off for children that size. Viridia kept her face to the front. Lora kept quiet.

He could not help them. Bal clicked his tongue to his horse and rode on.

* * *

It went on like that for the next two days. They kept meeting people on the road, fleeing the fighting, looking for some kind of peace in the other territories. Families with carts. A man on his own with a pack on his back. Some had been hurt. He passed a woman with her arm bound up in a sling made of a shirt, and a boy not much younger than him with a bandage around his head that had bled through and dried brown.

Normally, when one originator fought another, ordinary people were left out of it. Soldiers fought soldiers, and the farms went on farming. Bal had known that Raizen was doing something different. He had read Thad’s reports. He had not understood until now how many people that something reached. Nobody had written down the woman in the sling.

They all saw Viridia’s hair first. Then they found something to study in the road.

* * *

They reached Londra on the fifth day.

It sat behind a high stone wall, with a stone gate set in the middle of it. A guard stood at either side of the gate, waving people through. There was a short line of carts and walkers, and the dust they raised hung in the heat.

Lora and Bal dropped back together and let Viridia take the lead. Bal rounded his shoulders and tried to take up less of the road. He hoped he looked enough like a servant. He had seen plenty of servants. He had never paid attention to how they stood.

Lora had. “Sit up,” she said, low, without turning her head. “You look like you stole that horse and you’re sorry about it. A servant from a good house rides straight and looks at nothing. He isn’t ashamed. He’s bored.”

Bal straightened in the saddle. He picked out a stone in the wall above the guards’ heads and gave it his whole attention, and did his best to be bored by it.

The guard on the left put out an arm to stop them. He was a big man, bigger around the middle than in the arms, but he held a heavy black truncheon in one hand, and from his grip on it he had used it before.

“What’s your business in Londra?” he said. It was a nasal voice, pitched high for a man that size.

Viridia gave him the kind of look Lora had been drilling into her for five days.

“I am on my way to my family in Pashar,” she said. “We shall rest here a few days before we go on. I am to meet my father in Pashar this coming week, and I should like to buy him and my mother a gift while I am here.”

The other guard had not been listening. He was younger, and he had been gazing at her hair since they rode up, with something close to awe. Now he stepped over to her horse to inspect her eyes.

Her hair began to crackle.

It lifted off her shoulders at the ends, a strand at a time. Small blue sparks ran along it and snapped out. It did that when she was nervous.

“I’ve never seen that gift,” the young guard said. “It’s really rare.”

Viridia threw her head back.

“Of course it is. Now, may we pass? I am tired and I would like to rest.” She flicked her fingers back at the road they had come by. “After passing filthy blood mages on the roads, I’ll be glad to be among my own people.”

Bal held his tongue, as a servant would.

“Of course,” the guard with the black truncheon said, and waved them on.

Viridia started forward, then pulled up short. Something else had occurred to her, or she wanted it to look that way.

“Could you give me directions to the Three Boars Inn? I usually stay at the Winespring when I’m here, but I hear their wine is exceptional. I should like to try it.”

“Certainly, my lady,” the big guard said, quick and eager now. “Head up to North Street, then take the third street on your right. You’ll see their sign hanging off the building.”

Viridia thanked him, the once Lora had allowed her, and slapped the reins on her horse’s neck.

* * *

Bal and Lora followed her through the gate in silence.

The streets inside were crowded. They threaded their way through people on foot and people with handcarts, past a woman selling onions off a blanket and a boy chasing a goose that had other plans. People nudged each other wherever Viridia passed, a good many of them. A man on a doorstep forgot the pipe in his hand until it went out. She rode through it with her chin up and her buttons done.

Bal brought his horse over beside Lora’s and spoke under the street noise.

“Exactly how rare is Viridia’s gift?”

Lora had noticed the stares too.

“Honestly? No idea. She doesn’t really talk about it.” She frowned. “From what I’ve read, her powers are a lesser version of Raizen’s. How much weaker, I couldn’t tell you. The only time I’ve seen her use it was at that lake. When she shot lightning out of her hand, straight down beside that big snake.” She paused. “That poor snake never stood a chance.”

Bal remembered. He could still smell it.

“And she’s still got her lightning,” Lora went on, “and now she’s got her blood magic on top of it. She’s one scary lady.”

Ahead of them, Viridia turned her horse off the main street. North Street, Bal assumed, until she went straight past the third turning on the right. Soon after that she drew up at a stable yard attached to a long two-story building with its shutters thrown open. The painted board over the door said WINESPRING INN.

Bal glanced at Lora. So that was why Viridia had asked the guard the way to the Three Boars: Xavier had put the Winespring just past it. Lora lifted her eyebrows back at him and offered nothing.

The inn’s stable boy had been leaning in the shade of the yard wall. He came over at a run, and stood ready at Viridia’s stirrup while she got herself down out of the saddle in all that gray skirt.

“M’Lady,” he said. “We’re happy to see you. I hope your travels have been pleasant.”

He reached for her reins.

“We were told to expect you.”


CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR

The Winespring Inn

The innkeeper hurried out from behind her counter at nearly a trot, drying her hands on her apron as she came.

She was a plump woman with a smile that started well before she reached them. Behind her the shutters stood open along the whole long wall, letting whatever breeze Londra had to offer wander between the tables. Six or seven people were drinking in the cool. One old man had his boots off under his table and was not ashamed of it.

The stable boy’s words had followed Bal in from the yard. That would be Amelia’s letter, as Xavier had said, and it still sat badly with him that an inn in Raizen’s own country had been told to expect them. But too many people had already seen Viridia: two guards, half a street, a man whose pipe had gone out. If they turned around now and rode back through the gate, somebody would ask why, and he had no answer ready.

He stood a step behind his lady with the bags and kept his face as empty as he could get it.

“Welcome to the Winespring Inn, my lady,” the innkeeper said. “We had your letter just this morning, and your rooms are all made up. And I was told to give you this when you arrived.”

She drew a plain white envelope from her apron pocket. Viridia took it as if envelopes were brought to her on every landing in the country, and passed it straight back to Lora.

“I’m Mrs. Locklan. Come up, come up. You’ll want out of those riding things. Your room has a servants’ room off it, so your people will be right there whenever you want them.”

She went ahead of them up the stairs, still talking. The stairs were broad and dark with polish, and they creaked on every other step. On the landing Viridia stopped, and Mrs. Locklan stopped too, a step above her.

“Mrs. Locklan. The letter you got this morning. How were you told you’d recognize me?”

“Why, by the sparking hair, of course.” Mrs. Locklan beamed down at her. “Normally I’d have taken a thing like that for somebody playing a bad joke on me. But it came with payment for the rooms and all your meals, and nobody plays a joke that expensive.” She pressed her palms together under her chin. “I still can’t wait to tell my husband. A high-born lady with Raizen’s favor, staying in my inn. He’ll say I’m making it up. Anyway.” She edged past them toward the noise below. “Whatever you need, my lady, don’t hesitate to ask.”

Her steps went creaking away down the stairs.

* * *

The servants’ room was a narrow slot with one bed, one window, and a door through to Viridia’s room that did not quite meet its frame. Bal stowed the bags under the bed.

Back in the other room, he found Viridia with the envelope open.

She read it with the worried crease still between her eyebrows. Halfway down, the crease let go, and she started laughing, so hard and so suddenly she had to sit down on the bed. She handed the page to Lora. Lora read it, clamped her mouth shut on whatever wanted out, and tossed it to Bal.

He knew the hand at once.

*My loves,*

*Do not think I was joking about going after Raizen if something should happen to you. Surely you would not want me to have the death of a fellow originator on my hands.*

*Be safe and come home to me.*

There was no name. Just a single *A* at the bottom, with a tail on it that ran on well past where a tail should stop.

Bal read it again. Then he tore it in half, tore the halves, and kept on tearing until the letter was a little drift of white flakes in his cupped hand, too fine for anybody to piece together. He tipped them into the cold grate.

“We ought to have guessed she’d pull a stunt,” he said. “That girl loves having the last word.”

Neither of them argued.

* * *

Viridia stood and turned her back to Lora, who started on the long row of buttons down it without being asked.

“Why did you ask the guards about the Three Boars?” Lora said, now that the door was shut on the rest of the inn.

“I wanted to see if anybody would say something about the priests.” Viridia worked her arms out of the sleeves. “They’ll be here in two days. I thought maybe the guards would try to warn people off staying there.”

Bal turned to the window before the dress came down, and stayed turned, and studied the stable yard below as if he would be tested on it later. Cloth rustled behind him. When she said he could look, she was in a clean shift and a plain skirt, and she was grinning at him for having found the yard so interesting. Then she combed her fingers back through her hair, from the scalp right out to the ends, and little threads of blue went skittering off the strands ahead of her fingers and popped out in the air.

It brought back what had been nagging at him since the gate.

“Now I’m wondering if I made the right call. Bringing you both.”

Viridia’s hands stopped in her hair. “Where’s this coming from?”

“I honestly didn’t realize what a big deal your gift was,” he said. “You use it so rarely, I hardly ever remember you’ve got it. Then we came through that gate.”

“Yeah. That’s probably something we should’ve thought of. I didn’t realize either. I knew it was rare. I guess I never knew how rare. After today, I’ll be noticed everywhere we go.”

Lora stopped folding the gray dress.

“You know, it might not be as bad as we think,” she said. “Yeah, they’ll notice you. But if they’re looking at you, they won’t be looking at us.”

Bal pictured the ride in. It had all been for the blue hair in front. Nobody on that street could have said what color the manservant’s coat was.

“Huh,” he said.

“Well, it’s too late to fret over it now,” Viridia said, already done with it. “We need a plan for the priests when they get here.”

* * *

A manservant out on his lady’s errands was nobody, so Bal went out to learn what he could.

Xavier’s people had said the Three Boars took a fresh cask of ale every second Wednesday, and a cask came from somewhere. He asked a woman selling apples where the brewers were, then a carter watering his mule, and the carter sent him east, to a big plain building with a warehouse joined to the back of it that ran right down onto the water. The water there was slow, the color of tea. Only two ships were tied up along the dock, both well down from the brewer’s end.

Bal knocked at the street door, waited the polite length of time, and let himself in.

A man came from an office at the back to meet him. He was somewhere in his forties and built like he still lifted his own barrels, with his sleeves rolled to the elbow. His brown hair had started going thin on top. His eyes were sharp. They went over Bal’s servant’s coat and dusty boots and found a customer.

“Hello. My name’s James. How can I help you?”

Through the doorway behind him was the office desk, and everything on it sat square to the edge. Even the pens lay in a row.

“My lady’s heard our revered priests will be in town soon,” Bal said. “We won’t be staying long ourselves, but she thought she’d buy them some spirits while we’re here. She hears the Three Boars is where they always stay, and that you supply it. She hoped you might know what they’d like.”

James kept quiet until Bal wondered what he had gotten wrong. Then he jerked his head at the office. He took the big desk; Bal got the chair in front of it, hard and straight-backed.

“I might know,” James said. “They usually stay two days. First day they get roaring drunk. Second day they do their business, whatever it is, and then they head home.”

“Would you have any idea of a suitable gift for them?”

“That depends.” James put his elbows on the desk. “On how much coin you’ve got. And how much your lady wants to impress them.”

Bal smiled back. “I’m sure my lady wants to impress them very much. Money’s no object.”

James laughed from the belly, one big round boom that filled the little office and bounced off its walls. “All right, then,” he said, getting up. “Follow me.”

* * *

He took Bal out the back and along a short covered passage to the warehouse. Business was good, James told him; a shipment had come in from Cartesia a few days back, and the place was nearly full. He hauled the door open on its runner.

It was cool inside, cool enough to lift the hairs on Bal’s neck. The big ale casks were stacked in rows from the floor to well above a tall man’s reach, and every lid had a date burned into the wood.

“What are the dates for?”

“When that one was brewed.” James slapped a lid as he passed it, fondly. “The longer an ale sits, the better it gets. Some of these I’m in no hurry to sell.”

The smaller casks, the brandy and the sherry, lived in the far right-hand corner, lined up neat against the back wall in a single row. They were pale maple, and small enough for Bal to wrap both arms around one and lift it.

“Your priests usually take the cheaper brandy,” James said. “They don’t get paid much. Not that it stops them spending plenty getting drunk on it.” He walked along the row and stopped by the final four. “Now these. These are older. I’ve been sitting on them for years. Bought them off a trader vessel that came through, out of the Ventish region. By far the most potent brandy I’ve got.”

Bal considered the row of them. “The most expensive, too, I’ll bet.”

James shrugged. “Of course. If you want the best, it isn’t cheap.”

A gift like that would surely get them close to the priests. What came after, once they were that close, was a question for later, and later was coming up fast.

“How much?” he said.

They haggled. James named a price that made Bal’s stomach drop, and they went at it for a while, and in the end Bal allowed himself to be talked up further than he had to. He arranged for the cask to go to the Winespring Inn, so his lady could present it herself, and counted the coins onto the lid of a sherry cask. It took most of the purse.

* * *

The girls had stayed in their room. They’d had lunch sent up instead, and there was a pot of stew on the table under a cloth and a loaf beside it still warm enough to steam when Viridia tore it.

The room had only one chair. Bal got it. Viridia handed him a bowl and half the loaf, and he ate.

The stew had real meat in it, a lot of it, and carrots and onions and some kind of bean, all in a broth thick enough to hold the spoon up. At Saint Andre’s, meat in a stew had been more of a rumor than an ingredient. Bal made every spoonful last.

“How did it go?” Lora asked.

“Pretty good. I bought us some brandy.”

“You could have bought brandy here.”

“Not Ventish brandy, I couldn’t.” He tore off more bread. “Good thing Amelia paid for the rooms. That cask put a big dent in the purse. It’ll be worth it, though. With that, we ought to get in front of them. I just have no idea what we do once we’re there.”

“Well, at least that part’s figured out,” Lora said. “We could just use our blood darts. Kill them right there.”

“No,” Bal said. “We want to walk out of there alive, remember? Kill them like that and we’re fighting our way out across half the city. I know you’re good with that rapier. Viridia and I aren’t.”

Nobody had anything after that. A cart went by under the window and someone in the yard shouted at it. Bal finished his stew, wiped the bowl out with the last of the bread, and took it down to Mrs. Locklan. He offered to wash it. She looked at him as though he had offered to marry her, and took it away from him.

* * *

When he got back upstairs Lora had her alchemy chest open on the floor, standing vials on the boards around her in a widening ring.

“Looking for something?”

“Seeing if I’ve got everything for the nightstalker poison.” She held a vial to the window, squinted at the label, added it to the ring. “It was in that alchemy book you bought me. I remember reading it and not paying much attention, because I couldn’t think when I’d ever want to poison anybody. But this is perfect.” Another vial came out. “It stays dormant for twenty-four hours. Nothing. Then you get lethargic and it gets hard to breathe, and your heart stops about six hours after that. They’ll all be on their way home by then, and we’ll be well on our way too.”

Bal reached down, brought her up from her knees, and kissed her.

“You’re amazing,” he said, when he let her go.

Lora’s glasses had gone crooked. She did not fix them.

“Have you got everything you need?” he asked.

She knelt among the vials again and read the labels a second time, frowning. “Everything except asphodel leaves and ginger root, it looks like. But I can get those here. They go into a lot of ordinary potions, so nobody’ll think twice if I ask. It’ll take about twenty hours to brew. I need to go get them now.”

She packed everything away, hugged Bal, hugged Viridia, and promised to hurry.

* * *

She was three hours.

The first hour went by without anyone mentioning it. In the second, Bal cleaned his boots, and then cleaned them again. Twenty hours of brewing, and the priests in two days, and Lora not even started.

“She’s fine,” Viridia said, from the bed. “She’s probably found a shop with a book in it.”

“Yeah.”

“She’s fine, Bal.”

It might have worked better if Viridia had not gotten up twice to check the window herself.

Close to the end of the third hour, the door swung inward and Lora came in backward, pushing it with her back because her arms were stacked to the chin with paper packages. Bal reached her before the door finished opening. He took the pile off her and laid it on the bed, carefully, in case any of it broke.

“Thanks.” Lora poured herself a cup of water from the pitcher and drank it straight down. “Sorry I’m late. Sorry. The alchemist wanted help brewing a night-sight potion, and I sort of got roped in. He took the shop over when his father passed, I think, and he’s had less training than I have. At least I’ve had Ms. Summers to help me. That guy’s got no one. He’s been getting by on his father’s notes, but there’s nothing underneath them. He didn’t even know what half the herbs in his own shop were for. Once we were done he gave me a bunch of herbs for free, so I restocked some of the ones I’d run out of. You never know what you’ll need. And I got the asphodel and the ginger root.”

Viridia might have guessed as much, and so might Bal. Sword work, books, alchemy, it made no difference what it was. Once Lora got into something, she went in deep and forgot to surface.

Lora was already unwrapping, sorting each herb into its own jar or twist of paper. Bal offered to help. So did Viridia. Lora told them to enjoy the city instead, since she couldn’t leave the cauldron once the brew was under way. They hung back and offered again.

“Does either of you know a single thing about alchemy?” Lora said. “No. You don’t. You’ll just be in my way.”

She herded them out with both hands, told them to have fun, and closed the door on them.

Out in the hall the two of them cracked up together and had to hold it in until they got to the stairs.

“Well, my lady,” Bal said on the landing, back in his manservant voice, “what now?”

Viridia held her haughty face past every table downstairs and into the yard.

“Let’s go see the Three Boars,” she said on the street.

* * *

Bal walked a pace behind her, where Lora had coached him to walk, and caught up when the crowd allowed.

“Once you’ve finished school, what then?”

“Assuming the war goes our way, you mean?” She went quiet over a stretch of cobbles. “If I’m honest, no idea. All I ever wanted was out of my village. Now I’m out, and I’ve got nothing after that.” She asked it without turning. “Is Amelia going to challenge the other originators, you think?”

Now it was Bal’s turn not to know.

“If she makes a big enough statement fighting Raizen, she probably won’t have to. If we win, she can demand pretty much anything she wants from him.” He thought of Amelia’s face when they had talked about it, and the smile she’d had at the end. “And from that smile, she’s got something good in mind.”

“About Lora’s poison,” he said. “Does it go in their drinks?”

“That’s the plan.”

A couple had stopped dead in the middle of the walk in front of a shop, and Viridia stepped around them. The shop was a jeweler’s. Rings and necklaces lay on a slope of dark cloth in the window, and Viridia’s eyes went across them, quick, and returned to the street.

“Lora says it’s got no taste,” she went on. “Her book says so. So that part’s fine.”

“Why not poison the whole cask?”

“Yeah. That would probably work better.” She considered it. “Less risk of getting caught slipping something into a cup.”

Her hair still drew notice, but less than it had at the gate. Word had gotten around, maybe. They were getting used to her.

* * *

The Three Boars was big. Three stories of gray stone, with a neat stable built on, and a long brick chimney climbing each end, one for the kitchen and one for the taproom, Bal guessed. The smell of roasting meat rolled out of the open kitchen windows and found him across the street. Every window had white shutters.

“Ten windows on the second story,” he said. “Ten on the third.”

“That’s a lot of rooms.” Viridia had stopped on the opposite side of the street, as though she were only waiting for a cart to pass. “The inn in my village had six. And that wasn’t counting the one the innkeeper and his wife slept in.”

Bal had half a mind to cross over and stroll down the side of the place, past the stable, to see the back half the priests would take.

“Don’t,” Viridia said, still facing the street. “A lady’s manservant doesn’t wander off to poke around somebody else’s stable while she stands in the road. Somebody would ask him his business. Then they’d ask me.” She tipped her chin at the traffic. “We’re waiting for a cart. Watch for the cart.”

Bal watched for the cart.

The front doors stood wide open. Laughter rolled out of them now and then, the loud afternoon kind. Two stable boys in rough clothes were lounging against the stable wall in the heat. One of them spotted Viridia. He drove his elbow into his friend’s ribs and pointed straight at her, with no shame at all.

Viridia gave them the smallest wave, a lady’s wave, two fingers raised and lowered. Then she turned for home.

“It’s funny,” she said, a street later. “All these people, you could almost believe they weren’t at war. They seem happier than I’d have thought.”

Bal grunted. “That’s because they feel safe behind their walls. I’d bet most of these people have never so much as passed anyone with our sygl, let alone met one. All they hear is their priests saying how crazy we are. That if our originator got so crazy she went and attacked them, it’s only a matter of time before all of us go crazy too.”

A pair of guards came by on their rounds, walking slow, heading up the street toward the Three Boars. Bal held his pace and let them go by.

“You have to figure the other territories are about the same,” he said.

They didn’t say much after that. The streets thickened toward the center of town, where vendors were calling out their wares. A stall at a corner was selling sweet rolls, glazed and still warm, and Bal bought two. They stood off to the side of the crowd and ate them without talking.

He hadn’t asked her about after school only to learn her plans. He’d hoped hers might give him some idea of his own. He was never leaving any of them. Past that, he had nothing. His whole life had come off its track the day he enrolled. Once this war was decided, he would either be dead, or he would need to decide what the rest of his life was for, and on a corner in Londra with sugar on his fingers, he couldn’t tell which looked likelier.

He had told Viridia the truth, though. He didn’t think Amelia would go after the other originators. She would make her statement whether she won or lost. If he and the girls died out here, Bal fully believed she would kill Raizen for it, and nobody alive could talk her out of it. The letter in the grate had said as much. It was a poor sort of comfort. But at least the others would leave her alone after that.

Viridia ducked behind him.

She was gone in the same breath, off through the crowd at a pace sure to get her noticed. Bal crammed the rest of his roll in and went after her. She swung into a side street. He caught up with her there and steered her into the mouth of an alley, then put his head out around the corner.

Carts. Shoppers. A dog. Nobody hurrying after them.

He got the roll down and faced her. Viridia was leaning on the alley wall with both hands spread on the bricks behind her.

“Sorry. We had to run. That was one of them. A priest. I recognized him from what Xavier told us. He’s got a long scar right through his right eye. He wasn’t wearing his azure robes, but it was him.”

Bal swore, quietly. “I thought they weren’t supposed to be here yet. Why’s he early?”

“No clue.” Viridia took hold of his sleeve and started towing him home through the back streets. “But we’d better stay hidden until the potion’s done. I’d rather not risk a fight in the middle of town. Especially without Lora.”

He let himself be towed, and they walked as fast as a lady and her manservant could walk without anyone wondering why.

* * *

Lora had the cauldron going over a little brazier by the window when they were let back in. The room smelled sharp and green, like a cut stalk. Viridia described the scar.

Lora swore too.

“We should have counted on Aleric coming early. From the little Thad and Xavier managed to get on him, he’s the most unpredictable of the three.” She took a pinch of something dark and dried from a twist of paper and began to crush it between two spoons. “Titus and Leon just follow orders. Aleric’s got ambitions, and he’s smart. With the war on, it makes sense he’d turn up ahead of the others.”

She tipped the crushed nightshade over the cauldron and let it fall in a thin dark trickle, then stirred, slow and gentle.

“Everything’s fine on my end,” she said, still stirring. “But I agree with Viridia. We should keep to our rooms until we’re ready to act.”

* * *

For the next fifteen hours, they did.

Bal read. Viridia read too, gave it up, and talked instead, low, so as not to bother Lora. Lora would not have noticed. She stirred and measured, and held vials up to the lamp, and later to the window when it turned gray. She ate whatever was handed to her without looking at it.

Some time in the night, Bal woke in the chair with his neck bent and saw her silhouetted against the brazier, the spoon going around and around. When he woke again it was morning and she was in the same place. He would have sworn she hadn’t moved.

The day went by beyond the shutters. Meals came up on trays from Mrs. Locklan and plates went back down. Tables scraped and voices rose and fell under the floorboards. Somewhere out in the city, a man with a scar through his eye was walking around a day early, and Bal stayed clear of the window.

At about five in the afternoon, Lora stretched. Her back went off in a long row of pops, like knuckles. She breathed out from somewhere around her heels.

“I think that’s the hardest thing I’ve done in a while,” she said. She was smiling, tired down to the bone. “But it looks like it turned out perfectly.”

She dipped her spoon into the cauldron and lifted it, and let what was in it run slowly back off the edge in a thin string.

It was clear as water. Twenty hours of dark powders and green stalks had gone into that pot, and what was left looked like you could wash your face in it.

“See how clear it is?” Lora said. “Not a hint of anything I put in.”

Viridia was half lying on the bed with her shoulders propped on the headboard. “And you’re sure they won’t be able to taste it?”

“As sure as I can be. My book hasn’t let me down yet. Even Ms. Summers said it was an exceptionally good book. That’s glowing praise, coming from her.” She waved at Bal. “Bring the cask over.”

The cask had arrived from the brewer’s that morning on a boy’s shoulder. Bal carried it to the cauldron and started on the stopper. It fought him. He got a better grip and twisted, and it came free with a pop so loud that everybody jumped.

Bal emptied a mugful of the brandy into the washbasin first. Lora transferred the poison with her wooden spoon. It took ages. The spoon was not large, and she would not spill a drop, and Bal held the cask steady while the clear stuff went in until she was scraping the bottom of the cauldron.

Getting the stopper back in was worse. It went in partway and stuck, though Bal threw his whole weight on it. In the end he pulled his boot off and used the heel as a hammer. Five hard blows drove the stopper home, and they must have carried down to every table below.

He set the cask on the table, which Lora had cleared of her bottles and jars.

The three of them stood around it.

It was a small cask. Pale maple, a date on the lid, the stopper driven back flush with the wood. They were pinning their lives on it.

Nothing else remained to be done.

* * *

The knock came the next morning and brought Bal bolt upright.

He was out of bed and through into the servants’ room before he was fully awake. He pulled the door to behind him, as far as it would go. Through the crack he watched Lora open the door.

It was Mrs. Locklan. A letter for her lady, she said. She hoped she wasn’t too early.

Lora thanked her as a maid would and closed the door softly.

Bal came back out. Lora was by the cold grate with an envelope in her hand. Its face was blank.

She turned it over. The flap was sealed with a round of wax, and pressed into the wax was Raizen’s Tempus sygl.


CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE

The Three Boars

Lora left it sealed. She crossed to the bed and sent it spinning onto the blankets over Viridia’s knees.

Viridia had not moved since the knock. She was propped against the headboard with her legs under the covers and her hair squashed flat where the pillow had been. Bal climbed back in beside her. His side of the sheet was still warm.

“What is it?”

Viridia turned the envelope over, and he felt her go rigid against his arm.

“What’s wrong?”

The seal cracked across Raizen’s mark and came away in two pieces.

“Only one kind of person is allowed to seal a letter with Raizen’s Tempus sygl,” she said. “A priest of Raizen.”

From his pillow he could make out a few lines in a narrow, slanting hand and a lot of empty paper under them. She went through it fast, with a frown that deepened as she went.

“They know I’m here. Not as one of Amelia’s. As someone with Raizen’s inheritance gift. They’d like to meet me.”

The cask sat on the table opposite the bed, untouched since last night. Viridia’s attention went to it and did not leave.

“Well. It’s just as well I bought them a gift.”

Lora had her arms wrapped around herself, though the room was already warm. “I don’t like it. But it was always a possibility. They were bound to hear about you, and if they heard, they’d want to meet you. Honestly, it’d be stranger if they hadn’t asked.” She unwrapped her arms. “It does get us in the door. That’s the one good thing. We don’t have to find an excuse.”

“Lunch,” Viridia said. “Today. The Three Boars.”

Bal climbed out of bed and went looking for his other boot.

* * *

Bal packed everything they owned. Lora’s chest was the heaviest, the cauldron scrubbed and wrapped in a shirt and wedged in on top of the jars, and Bal carried it down the creaking stairs first, then went back for the rest. If the day went as planned, they would ride out of Londra straight from the priests’ table. Nobody was coming back to the Winespring Inn.

He had the horses saddled before either girl came down. He held Viridia’s while she mounted, skirts and all, then Lora’s. He strapped the cask behind his own saddle, where it rode against his spine like a passenger, and swung up onto the black.

Viridia led. Bal fell in behind Lora, a servant’s length back, and watched the city go by.

It was another hot morning. A woman was beating a rug over a balcony rail, and the dust drifted down onto everyone passing under it, and nobody complained to her about it. The crowds parted for Viridia’s hair the same as before.

The doors of the Three Boars were shut.

That was new. Yesterday they had stood wide open, with laughter spilling into the street. Today the building offered only its plain front and white shutters, and not a sound.

The girls dismounted out front. Bal took their reins and led all three horses around to the stable built onto the side.

* * *

A stable boy came at a run, the same boy who had elbowed his friend and pointed. He had a cowlick standing straight up at the back of his head and a stalk of straw stuck to his shirt.

“I can take that, sir,” he said, already reaching.

He walked Viridia’s horse to a stall with enormous care, then hurried back for Lora’s and the black. Bal let the boy stall all three before digging in his purse.

“We’ll only be a short time,” he said, and put the coins in the boy’s palm.

The coins were silver. He gaped.

“Yes, sir!” He nearly shouted it, and Lora’s horse put its ears back.

Bal laughed. The boy’s face was too much. “Make sure they’re watered. And give them some oats before we go.”

“Yes, sir.” Quieter. The coins had already vanished somewhere inside the shirt.

Bal unbuckled the straps and lifted the cask from the black’s rump. It was heavier than it had been last night, or else he was tired. Then he headed around to the front.

* * *

Mrs. Locklan had come to meet them at a trot. The man behind the bar of the Three Boars never left it.
