Long-faced and gray at the temples, he was polishing a glass that did not need it. He took in Bal’s coat and then the cask in his arms, and, silent, he lifted the hand with the rag in it and pointed.

The taproom was enormous. Most of the tables stood empty, chairs pushed in, and the floor had been swept and was still damp in places. At the far corner three men in azure robes were gathered at a table with cups in front of them. Viridia was already there, in a chair that had plainly been dragged over specially. Close by, Lora had a table to herself and a small cup of water, and was taking sips.

As Bal came in, the nearest priest beckoned, two quick flicks of the fingers.

“Come now,” he said. “We’ve been waiting for you.”

He was stout, with a red neck rising out of his collar, and his fingers were drumming the table.

“Calm yourself, Titus. We have all day.”

That was Aleric. Bal knew him by the scar before he knew him by the voice: a long white seam that ran from his brow down across his right eye and onto his cheek. The eye under it was clouded. The other was dark and quick and missed nothing, from what Bal could tell.

The third had to be Leon. He was the youngest of them by ten years at least, with soft hands and a wispy attempt at a beard, and he leaned in to study the cask as Bal set it on the table.

“I’ve never had Ventish brandy,” he said. “I’d be most curious to try it. And you said the whole cask is for us?”

“Certainly.” Viridia smiled at him. “It’s a small token, to show how much we appreciate what the priesthood does for us. Even now, with this dreadful war going on.”

Aleric inclined his head to her, and the scar showed whiter than ever in the window light.

“We do appreciate it, my lady,” Aleric said. “Come. Share a drink with us. Let us celebrate Raizen’s glory.”

Viridia waved the offer off with the back of her hand. “Oh, I couldn’t. I had the cask brought just for you. Surely you’d enjoy it more than I would. I seldom take spirits.”

Aleric’s good eye narrowed.

“I insist.”

He drained what was in his cup. Then, to Bal at the table’s end:

“Get her a cup.”

* * *

Viridia’s face did not change. Her hands did. They had been resting on the table, and now they went still and pale on the wood, the knuckles standing out. The pulse was going in her throat, as plain as the scar on Aleric’s face.

A table away, Lora had heard every word. Her cup was halfway to her mouth. She had gone white to the lips.

There was no antidote. Lora had read them that part of the book too.

Viridia lifted her eyes to Bal. Whatever had been in them a moment ago was gone, and what was there now was something set and hard, and he understood it. She had already decided. If drinking with them was what it took to kill these priests, she would drink, and she would die of it a day from now along with them.

She let her lashes fall once, slowly, the signal a lady gives her servant, and turned her smile back on Aleric.

Bal went for the cup.

He kept it slow on purpose, one boot and then the other across the long damp floor, because every step bought him time to think. He could attack them. Turn around right now and go for them with everything he had. He could probably take one of them before they cut him down. One. And then Lora and Viridia would still die, here or in the street outside, and it would all have been for nothing.

No. Something. Something here.

He reached the bar. The long-faced man set down his glass.

“A small cup,” Bal said. “For my lady.”

The man went through a door at the back. Bal waited at the bar and looked along the shelves without taking in any of it. Rows of glass bottles, green and brown and clear. A clock somewhere ticking. His mind kept running in circles, out the door, back to the table, out again, and finding nothing at either end.

One of the bottles had a mark on it.

It was a tall clear bottle second from the top, nearly at the end of its shelf, and something had been cut into the glass on its side, fine white lines in a careful pattern. Bal leaned across the bar to see it better.

“It’s a cold rune.” The barman was back, with a cup in his hand. The cup clicked onto the counter between them, and now there was something like warmth in his voice. “I bought it off a fellow last week. Keeps anything you put in it cold. Any liquid at all.”

He went on saying something about the price. Bal had stopped listening.

Runes.

That was it.

He took the cup. It was turned wood, small and smooth, varnished dark. He lowered it below the edge of the bar, out of the barman’s sight, out of everybody’s sight, and held it upside down, left-handed.

His other palm, the right, split open by itself. A thread of blood slid from the split and drew itself together and hardened into a blade no longer than his little finger, thin and sharp and dark red.

He carved fast. The purification rune was one he had copied off Mr. Winters’ chalkboard that first week, stroke by stroke, and his hand had not forgotten the order of them. The blood blade cut the wood as easily as soap, and the shavings curled away and dropped to the floorboards at his feet.

There might not be a cure for nightstalker poison. But a purification rune turned any liquid into pure water. Whatever went into this cup would be water before it ever reached her mouth, if he had cut it right.

The blade sank back into his palm, and the cut sealed. He hurried back to the table.

* * *

The priests were talking among themselves about a road somewhere, and whose fault the state of it was. Viridia sat quietly in the middle of them, her hands resting in her lap, out of view.

Bal set the cup in front of her and hoped to catch her eye. She had her face turned to Aleric.

“That will be all,” Titus said, and waved him off.

So that was all. Bal stepped back to the wall, a few paces behind her chair, where a manservant could be called if he was wanted and ignored if he was not.

As the youngest, Leon got the job of opening the cask. He worked at the stopper two-fisted until it gave with a pop that went bouncing around the quiet room. Then he put his nose right down to the hole and breathed in so long and so deep that Titus snorted at him.

“Now that,” Leon said, coming up pink, “smells divine.”

He poured Viridia’s first. Then Aleric’s, then Titus’s, and his own last, filling each to the brim with great care and spilling a little anyway.

The brandy went into Viridia’s cup, dark gold, right up to the rim. It stayed dark gold. If the rune worked, it would work down there where nobody could see, in the moment before she drank. If it didn’t, then he had cut some stroke wrong, crouched behind a bar with a blade made of his own blood, and he would not find out until tomorrow, when finding out would do nobody any good.

Aleric lifted his cup. “To Raizen.”

“To Raizen,” they said, and Viridia said it too, and all four drank.

She tipped the cup back and drained it to the bottom, and her face stayed a lady’s face.

Aleric’s good eye had been on her all through it, above his own cup. Then, as she lowered hers, something in the priest’s face let go and smoothed out. He told Leon to fill the priests’ cups again.

“So,” he said. “You’re on your way to Pashar?”

“I am.” Viridia’s hands had gone back into her lap. “I’ve heard there are some wonderful shops in the capital. I mean to do some shopping.”

“There are many good shops there,” Aleric agreed. “Our esteemed High Priest will want to meet you, of course.” He smiled. “I assure you, word will reach him soon after you enter the city. As it reached us, when we arrived here. To find someone with so clear a sign of Raizen’s favor is rare indeed. I could send a letter on ahead of you, if you like. Let him know you’re coming.”

Viridia hesitated, barely, and Aleric waited on it. Bal knew what she must be weighing. A lady with nothing to hide would be flattered. A lady who said no would give the man one more thing to wonder about.

“That would be very kind of you,” she said.

* * *

They talked for about an hour.

Bal spent the whole of it by the wall, and his feet hurt, and the priests kept drinking.

Leon kept the cups full. Titus got louder with every one, and redder, until he was pounding the table when he laughed and the cups jumped. Leon started a story about a goat and could not finish it. Later he got up to find the privy and returned by way of every chair between. Even Aleric loosened, though less than the others. His courtly sentences began to wander and come home late.

Viridia’s cup stayed where she had set it, empty. No one offered to fill it again. She asked about Pashar and listened to the answers chin high, a lady being gracious to her inferiors, and only Bal, from behind, could see how straight she held her back.

Lora, off at her own table, wet her lips with her water. Her cup never got any emptier.

After their fourth cup, Viridia told them she ought to be going.

Aleric waved her on with a loose hand. “I’ll send the letter tomorrow.”

Viridia thanked him, gave them each the gracious farewell Lora had drilled into her on the road, and then beckoned the two of them after her.

None of the priests got up. Titus was refilling his own cup.

* * *

The door swung shut behind them. Viridia sagged back against it.

Her face crumpled. Tears ran straight down it, and her knees gave, and Bal caught her by the arms before she hit the step.

“I had to do it.” She was shaking so hard it went through his hands. “I had to. It was worth it.”

“Viridia.” The words came too fast, and he could not slow them. “You’re okay. You aren’t poisoned.”

She stared at him.

“What’re you talking about?” she said. “I drank the brandy.”

Bal got her moving toward the stable. Lora hurried along at their heels.

“I put a purification rune on the bottom of your cup,” he said. “I doubt that’s what it’s meant for. But it should have done the job.”

Viridia stopped walking. She was going back over it, he could tell: her runes class, the cup in front of her, the first mouthful, how watery it had tasted. Then he saw it reach her face.

“You mean all I drank was water?”

She was coming back.

“Sorry I couldn’t warn you. Getting the rune on the cup at all was hard enough, before I brought it over.”

* * *

The stable boy had the horses watered and fed and waiting in the yard. He might have waited with them the whole hour. Bal boosted Viridia into the saddle, then Lora, then mounted his own.

They rode out of the yard. Lora brought her horse up alongside Viridia’s and reached across and touched her arm, lightly, just above the wrist.

“I was afraid for you,” Lora said. “It was a brave thing you did.”

Viridia managed a wet little laugh by way of thanks. Then she gathered her reins.

“Now we just have to get clear of this place.”

She dug her heels in. Her horse jumped forward into the street, and people scattered out of her way, a man with a barrow, two women with baskets. Bal rode in silence behind her, keeping up, and watched her bounce in the saddle the length of North Street. Lora had taught her plenty about being a lady in five days. Riding like one had not been among them.

Near the gate they eased back to a walk. Bal rode straight and looked at nothing, bored. The same two guards were on duty, or two just like them. They let the three of them through without stopping them. Apparently they cared much more about who came into Londra than about who left it.

Then the gate was behind them, and the road ran out ahead into the long yellow country they had ridden in through.

They kept to a walk until the walls had dropped behind a rise.

Lora let her horse drift back beside the black.

“I spent that whole hour at my table going through the book in my head,” she said, low. “Every page. Hunting for an antidote I already knew wasn’t in it.” She was watching Viridia’s back up ahead. “The cup never crossed my mind.”

She left it at that. From Lora, that said plenty.

Anything he said back would have sounded like bragging. He rode beside her and kept it to himself, and it stayed warm in him for miles.

Then Viridia turned in the saddle, and Bal heeled the black, and the girls’ horses surged after his into a gallop. The horses stretched out. The road went pounding away under them, mile after mile of open grass with precious little cover between here and Amelia’s border, and every stride put more ground between the three of them and the soon-to-be-dead priests of the Three Boars.


CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX

Together

It was well after nightfall when they finally pulled up.

They had galloped until the horses could not, and walked them until they could again, and the road had faded from yellow to almost nothing under a thin slice of moon. They had passed one farmhouse with a light in the window, miles back, and beyond it, grass and more grass and the wind moving through it. Bal’s black was blowing hard. Twice it stumbled on ruts too dark to see. When a clump of trees rose out of the dark beside the road, Bal turned off toward it, and Lora and Viridia followed without asking why.

The ground in among the trunks was soft. The hooves sank into it with hardly a sound, and fallen leaves lay over everything so thick that they covered the tracks almost as fast as the horses made them. Bal led them in until the road was gone behind them. From what he remembered of the ride in, this was the last real cover they would have until Amelia’s territory. After this it was open grass clear to her border.

He swung down. His legs had quit belonging to him an hour or two back, and he stood with a hand on the saddle until they were his again.

“We can’t risk a fire,” he said.

“No,” Lora said. “No, you’re right.”

Viridia was down off her horse first.

The girls saw to the saddlebags. Bal walked back out to where the trunks thinned and stood a while, watching the road. It ran off pale in both directions until the night swallowed it. Nothing moved on it. No hoofbeats, no lantern, nobody shouting. The priests would be sleeping off the brandy by now, most likely, and Leon would never finish his goat story. He returned to the horses.

They tied the horses where there was something to crop and spread the bedrolls on the leaves. Lora dug the bread out of a saddlebag, and the dried jerky after it, and passed it around. Viridia took her share and kept quiet. On any other night she’d have made some crack about eating supper in the dark like a pack of bandits. The bread was hard. The jerky was tough and salty and Bal ate it anyway.

It was cold in the wood once they were off the horses. The leaves under his bedroll crunched whenever he moved. One of the horses stamped and blew, and somewhere farther in, something small went pattering off through the undergrowth and was gone. Lora ate in small bites, half turned toward Viridia throughout.

Nobody talked.

Viridia had a piece of bread in one hand that she wasn’t eating. Even by starlight the day showed on her. At noon she had taken a seat among Raizen’s priests expecting to be dead by tomorrow night. Then, out on the step, she had learned she wouldn’t be. Bal couldn’t guess what that did to a person, but it couldn’t be nothing.

He chewed his bread and waited. She would talk when she was ready.

Viridia lifted her head.

“Thank you, Bal.”

She said it straight at him and held his gaze while she said it. He didn’t make a joke. He let her say it, and he let it stand.

She took a deep breath. It left her slowly and unevenly.

“When Aleric told me to drink,” she said, “I was so scared.”

She said it to the bread in her lap, as if it were something she ought to apologize for.

Lora got up and crossed the leaves and settled on Viridia’s bedroll, right beside her, so their shoulders touched. She took Viridia’s free hand in hers and waited for the rest.

“All the fights we’ve been in,” Viridia went on. “I always knew somebody could get hurt. Really hurt. Maybe even killed. I knew that going in.” Her voice wobbled and steadied. “But I never expected I’d have to drink poison myself to kill an enemy.”

Her hair lay still.

Bal had not noticed until then. In all the weeks since the day they’d met, he could not remember it ever being quiet for long. It snapped when her nerves were up, it fizzed when she laughed, and on the long dry days on the road it drifted about her collar all by itself, like it had somewhere to be. He had stopped seeing it, the same as a person stops hearing a clock.

Tonight it lay down flat on her shoulders and did not stir. Nothing ran along it. Nothing popped. In the dark it could have been anybody’s hair.

“Still,” Viridia said. “Those priests couldn’t go free. I had to. My only consolation was that you and Lora would get away.”

Her fingers tightened on Lora’s, and her mouth tried for a smile and gave up halfway.

“I just wanted you to be safe.”

Lora gathered her in.

That was what did it. The day was done, and whatever had been holding Viridia up through the priests’ table and the city gate and all those miles of grass gave way. She cried. Big, ragged, gulping sobs that shook the two of them together. Over by the trunks one of the horses lifted its head at the sound, decided it was none of its business, and dropped its nose to the grass again. She was alive, and it had only now reached her.

Lora murmured close to her ear, a steady murmur Bal couldn’t catch a word of.

Bal kept to his bedroll and broke twigs.

The ground was covered in them. He snapped one into short pieces and dropped the pieces between his boots and found another without looking, and the little pile grew.

He would have killed each of those priests with his bare hands for making her feel this. He would have been happy to. His hands closed on nothing and opened and closed again, and he could nearly feel Aleric’s neck in them.

The only comfort he had was that by around six tomorrow night, those priests would all be dead.

After that, there was the High Priest. Toma hardly ever left Pashar, and getting to him there would be hard.

* * *

Viridia’s crying wore down to hiccups, and the hiccups to long shaky breaths, and then she was quiet. Lora slid free of her and made room on the bedroll. She drew the blanket up to Viridia’s chin.

Past Lora’s shoulder, Viridia found Bal. She gave him a tired little smile. It looked like all she had left. She was asleep before Lora had the blanket straight.

Bal and Lora stayed up talking, low, about the day and what they might have to do next. Lora had gone back across to her bedroll. She sat with her legs folded under her and her hands resting on her knees, and the starlight that got in past the branches caught her glasses. Her face was harder to read than usual. She had been holding Viridia up for an hour, and before that she had spent all of that lunch at her little table, sipping water she couldn’t taste, white and silent.

“You know going after Toma’s going to be worse than this,” she said. “One of ours almost died today, and we didn’t even get into a fight. Imagine what could happen next time.”

Bal pushed at the picture, wanting it gone. The two of them lying somewhere on open ground. It would not go.

“I do imagine it,” he said. “Enough that I’ve thought about having Amelia keep you both while I go after him alone.”

Lora laughed, one short breath of it, held down for Viridia’s sake.

“And you think Amelia would let you go off alone? If you do, you aren’t as smart as I thought.”

Bal grumbled something at the leaves. He didn’t mean much by it, and she seemed to know he didn’t.

“Even so,” he said. “Doesn’t mean I like it.”

Across the little clearing Viridia had started snoring, softly, one arm thrown clear of the blanket and a leaf caught in her hair. Lora’s attention went to her and lingered.

“We don’t like it either,” Lora said. “But we’re in this together. We just have to find some way for everybody to make it through alive.”

Bal didn’t try to hide what was on his face. Nobody was awake to see it but Lora.

“I’m out of ideas,” he whispered.

The words hung there in the dark. Even the horses had gone still. Viridia snored on, a small, steady sound in all that quiet.

Lora leaned across the gap between their bedrolls and laid her hand along his cheek. Her palm was cool from the night air and smelled faintly of reins.

“We’ll figure it out,” Lora told him. “Together.”

Bal closed his eyes, and while her hand stayed there he did not think about Toma at all. Opening them again, he smiled at her.

“We should try and sleep,” he said.

Lora pulled her blanket up. On his own bedroll, with the leaves crackling under him, Bal listened to the two of them breathing, one soft snore and one not. Through a gap overhead a handful of stars showed. Nobody seemed to have followed them down the road. Somewhere out toward it an owl started up, gave it three tries, and quit.

In the morning they could take their time.


CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN

Thunder from a Clear Sky

Viridia was laughing before the horses were even saddled.

Lora had pulled a leaf out of Viridia’s hair, a big brown one that had ridden in there all night, and waved it at her. The noise Viridia made sent something flapping out of the branches overhead and away, complaining. It was a good sound to wake up to. Bal stayed on his bedroll longer than he needed to and listened.

They led the horses out to where the trees ended and stood there a while, studying the road in both directions. It lay empty. No dust hung over it anywhere, no riders, no cart.

Bal was satisfied. “Nobody’s after us. No reason I can see to rush. We can take it easy today and walk them.”

The black blew through its nose as if it agreed.

They rode at a walk south through the yellow grass, and the morning came up warm and then warmer, and Viridia talked. She was telling Lora about her village, story after story, about people from home. Lora asked after each of them, and Viridia answered with her hands going as much as the reins allowed.

Last night she had cried herself out in Lora’s arms, and then she had slept. This morning she was herself again, or near enough that Bal saw no difference from where he rode.

He was thinking about the bookshop in New Spring. About standing in the stacks with an armful of books he had not been reading, while Viridia told Lora, past the shelf, that her whole life had been planned out the day she was born. Who she’d marry, even. He had never asked her about it. It had not been his to ask, when he’d only heard it by accident.

But she was in a talking mood.

“So what was it like?” he said. “Growing up there. I bet all the boys were chasing after you.”

Something went across Viridia’s face. It came and went like a cloud over a field, and her mouth went flat while it lasted. Bal wished he had the words back.

Then it cleared, and she answered him anyway.

“The day I was born, it was decided I’d marry the headman’s son. Bran. He was two at the time.” She gave the name a sour twist. “My whole childhood, every time I told him I didn’t like him, he’d smirk at me. Every single time. And he’d tell me it didn’t matter, because he would have me eventually.” She let the grass go by for a moment. “Getting my sygl was what saved me from marrying him.”

Lora said nothing on her other side.

Bal chewed on that for a stretch of road. He knew the rule already, that anyone with a sygl was allowed out. He had not thought much about how few must ever use it.

“Are sygls really that rare in your village?”

Viridia took a long drink from her water bottle before she answered, and the sun winked off the neck of it.

“Yeah. Not sure why, though. Since the village lets anyone with a sygl leave, it basically gave me a new life.”

She laughed, the same as this morning.

“That fool Bran tried to get his father to make me stay. Another six months and we’d have been married. And his father wouldn’t do it.” She told it to the sky, enjoying it. “Bran was not happy.”

* * *

Bal smiled at her. Her hair was popping again, tiny blue snaps along the ends of it every time the breeze lifted them, and he had missed the sound more than he’d realized. The Three Boars was behind her, then.

It was a hot day. By late morning the sweat was running into his eyes, and he mopped his forehead with a rag from his pocket.

He kept checking behind them, without ever quite deciding to. The road was always empty.

After the fourth or fifth time, Lora nudged her horse in beside the black.

“Did you hear something?”

“No. It’s only, for some reason I’ve got this funny feeling. Like somebody’s got a bow pointed at my back.” He laughed at himself. “It’s probably me being paranoid.”

To take their minds off it, and his, he started telling them jokes. They were the ones he’d learned at Saint Andre’s, mostly from the older boys, and they were mostly terrible. Lora groaned at the worst one so hard she nearly dropped her reins. Viridia made him tell another one twice because she had not believed it the first go-round, that anybody would end a joke there.

The feeling did not go anywhere.

If anything it grew. It sat between his shoulder blades through the noon heat and into the long afternoon, and the more he ignored it, the heavier it got. When the sun began to drop, he gave up on jokes. His head kept going around: behind them, the sky, the grass to the right, the grass to the left, behind them again.

Somewhere along the way the girls had stopped talking. Bal missed the moment. They did not seem to feel what he felt. They rode with their hands tighter on the reins anyway, because the feeling was his and they trusted him.

The shadows of the three horses stretched out long and thin on the road beside them. It would be close to six. Back in Londra, three priests who had drunk Ventish brandy at lunch yesterday would be dying about now, if Lora’s book was right. The plan had been his. He had found it over a map at Xavier’s table, while Amelia sat next door with a book so that no one could accuse her of helping. Lora had brewed the poison and Viridia had bet her life on a cup of brandy, and he had walked them both deep into Raizen’s country to do it.

The boom came from behind them.

It rolled down on them out of the north, out of Londra, and went over them and on across the grass, and all three saddles creaked at once as they twisted in their seats.

It had sounded like thunder. One single tremendous clap of it, the kind that came straight down on a house in a summer storm.

Overhead the sky was clear from one edge to the other, a hot, high, faded blue, without one cloud in it anywhere to have made that sound.

Viridia had gone pale under her tan.

“Can you feel it now?” Bal asked her.

“Yeah.” She was still facing north. “Yeah, I can.”

Lora said, “Feel what?”

“I can’t explain it.” Viridia’s voice had dropped. “It feels like we’re in danger.”

Bal’s mind was still back toward Londra, and he could not pull it loose.

“Are we in danger?” Lora asked him.

“Yes.”

“Can you tell from what?”

* * *

The bolt hit the road between their horses.

It came from nowhere. From that blank blue sky. White light and a crack that went right through Bal’s teeth, and then the black was screaming and going straight up, and Bal was in the air with no saddle under him.

He landed on his shoulder and rolled. Hooves went past his head. All three horses were bolting, reins flying, stirrups flapping. More bolts came down around them, one and then three and then too many to keep track of, and each one blew a hole in the earth and threw dirt in his face.

He got to his knees. He could not hear anything but a high thin ringing.

Viridia was up before him.

She was planted in the thick of it with her feet apart and her arms stretched straight up at the sky, and her hair had risen and spread out behind her like a blue flag in a wind. She was trying to pull them in. Every bolt that came down, she reached for it, trying to drag it off course and into herself, where it couldn’t hurt anybody. Some of them bent toward her. Most of them didn’t. There were too many.

Bal flung himself clear of one that came down where he had been kneeling.

He saw another strike ten feet to his left, the hardest yet. He saw the earth go up. He saw something dark and big as a loaf fly out of it, tumbling end on end, and he was still moving clear of the light when it caught him on the side of the head.

Then there wasn’t anything.

* * *

His head woke first. It was throbbing, a slow heavy beat, far off and steady, and his mouth was full of the taste of copper.

He worked his tongue around. It hurt. He had bitten it in the fall, bitten it hard, and the side of it was thick and raw.

Bal pushed himself upright.

He tried to call for Lora and Viridia. His voice was a croak, hardly a sound at all.

The road hadn’t moved. Neither had the grass. Between them, in a wide ragged ring around him, the ground was full of craters. They were blackened down in the middle and scorched at the rims, and a few of them were still smoking. Hours had gone by. The sun hung low and red in the west, and the air had cooled.

Nobody was there but him.

The wind shifted, and he smelled burnt horse.

He stood. It took two tries. He started walking, around the edges of the craters, calling their names. His voice returned slowly, cracked and then hoarse and then loud.

“Lora! *Viridia!*”

All he had left of either of them was Viridia with her arms up and her hair flying. He kept calling anyway. He circled the craters, and went on out past them, and back to the road, searching the ground for anything. A footprint. A dropped glove. Broken grass where somebody had been dragged, or had run.

There wasn’t anything. The grass stood up straight wherever he looked. The road was hard and dry and kept no marks. It was as if the girls had dropped off the edge of the world.

He went on calling until the sun was down and his voice was gone again, and the dark had risen out of the ground so he could not see his own feet.

He could not search in the dark. He knew that. Stopping was harder.

The horse that had been hit was Lora’s. Neither of the others was anywhere in sight. It lay on its side at the edge of the farthest crater, and he forced himself over to it. Her bedroll was still tied behind the saddle. He worked the straps loose, breathing through his mouth, and pulled the roll free, and carried it upwind until the air was clean enough to breathe. He spread it out and stretched out on top of it.

Sleep was slow coming. Every time his eyes shut, something was waiting behind them. Lora somewhere dark. Viridia with her hands tied. People who wanted them dead, with the two of them right there in reach. He turned over, and turned back, and the bedroll carried a faint smell of Lora’s alchemy chest, sharp and green.

Toward morning he slept, badly.

* * *

The first light of the sun came over the grass and into his face.

He shaded his eyes and sat, slowly.

Lora’s bedroll. Lora’s horse, dark against the grass. The black craters. Nobody else.

It hit him all over again, as hard as it had at dusk, low in the stomach. He stayed like that with his hand still raised to the sun and waited for it to let him breathe.

He rose and searched again. He ranged out farther than yesterday, in wide loops through the grass, east of the road and west of it, hoping for something. Anything. He found nothing.

They had been taken.

How, he couldn’t begin to guess. Who, or from where, or how anyone had gotten them away without leaving a single mark. But nothing else made sense. If the girls had run, or been hurt, or worse, he would have found something by now.

He stood on the road and looked north, toward Londra.

If it was Toma behind this, the girls would not be in Londra. They would be headed for Pashar. And Pashar was days and days from here. With no horse and no supplies, nothing but the clothes he stood in and Lora’s bedroll, he could not possibly reach them in time to do any good.

The army was evacuating blood sygls out of the Tempus border towns. He remembered Thad saying so, on the stairs at the temple. If he found the army, he’d find a horse.

Bal rolled up the bedroll, tied it with its own strap, and turned south, toward Amelia’s territory.

* * *

It took him two days on foot.

The road was white with dust both days. He drank from a ditch, and once from a horse trough at the edge of a field whose owner never appeared. He had nothing to eat, and gave no thought to finding any. At night he slept a few hours on Lora’s bedroll beside the road, and woke before light, and walked again. By the second afternoon he was not thinking much at all. His feet went on without him.

Some of the way he talked to the girls in his head. He told Lora he was coming. He told Viridia not to give anybody any lip until he got there, which was a waste of breath, because she would anyway, and he hoped she did.

Whenever he stopped, their absence found him again, and he stopped as little as he could.

The village he came to at the end of the second day was small, a crossroads with a well and maybe twenty houses, and there was a party of Blood soldiers in the square.

They were waiting. Behind them a little knot of people was hurrying in and out of a handful of the houses with bundles and baskets and one with a kettle, loading whatever they meant to keep onto a cart. Some of those people had black eyes. Some had split lips, scabbed over. One man had his arm strapped up across his body in a sling, and he was trying to carry a sack with the other.

The rest of the village stood back along the fronts of the houses and cursed the soldiers. They did it loudly, and they meant it, and nobody stepped off a doorstep. The soldiers had their hands near their weapons. They looked ready to fight, and the villagers knew it.

Bal walked straight in among them.

Let anybody who liked see his palm. He wasn’t hiding it. He knew, with a cold, steady calm, that if anybody in that village tried to stop him, he would kill them.

Nobody tried.

Bal knew one of the soldiers. He had seen him at the temple: a centurion, Jazen Grey.

Jazen heard boots behind him and spun. Then he was coming across the square, fast, and keeping his voice down.

“High Priest.” He looked Bal over, the dust and the dried blood in his hair and Lora’s bedroll clutched to his side. “What brings you out here? We all thought you were off in Londra.”

“I was.” He sounded tired, even to himself. That couldn’t be helped. “Things happened. I need to get to the temple, fast. Do you have a horse you can spare?”

Whatever Jazen saw in his face settled it.

“Of course. Take mine.” He said it without a breath between. “If you ride hard, you can be at the temple by noon tomorrow.”

Over by the blood sygls and their cart, another soldier was leading horses up.

“We’ll get there some time tomorrow night,” Jazen said. “When you see Commander Fern, would you let him know we’ve had no trouble? And that we’ll be with him shortly.”

“I’ll tell him.”

Jazen called to one of his men, who jogged off and returned leading a horse.

It was the biggest horse Bal had ever stood next to. A warhorse, deep through the chest and heavy in the legs, with a head on him the size of a small barrel. He was white all over, and in the late sun his coat shone.

Jazen ran a hand down the great neck and gave it a fond, heavy pat.

“Ol’ Barda’s never failed me yet,” he said. “He’ll get you there safe. And quick.”

“I owe you for this. Truly.”

He got a foot into the stirrup and hauled himself up. Barda was tall, and he took Bal’s weight without shifting an inch. Bal found the reins, swung the big white head around toward the south road, and raised a hand to Jazen.

Jazen returned it.

Bal gave Barda his heels, and the village fell away behind them.

* * *

He rode all night.

He ran Barda at a gallop until the horse’s breath went ragged, then let him drop to a trot until it evened out, then ran him again, all through the dark, under a sky full of stars. Barda never balked. Some time past midnight Bal woke with a jerk, his chin down and the reins slack in his fingers, and found the big horse still pounding steadily south as if he knew the road better than his rider did. He went where he was pointed and went on, and the heat of him rose through the saddle into Bal’s legs, and the beat of the big hooves made more sense than anything else in the night.

The sun came up on his left. He kept on. The hills began, low ones at first, then higher, and a few were familiar.

It was a little before noon when he heard the fighting.

* * *

He topped the hill and pulled Barda up hard.

Below him, in the open ground in front of the canyon where the temple hid, a small party of Tempus soldiers was fighting Amelia’s. It had to be another of the scouting parties Thad had told him about. It had been going on a while. Bodies lay scattered all over the field between the lines, men from both sides, all of them still.

Lightning cracked across the field.

At the rear of the Tempus party, two mages were hurling bolts into the blood soldiers without a pause between them. Far behind Amelia’s lines stood two figures in scarlet robes, and even from here he knew them. Thad, with Xavier at his shoulder. Each had a great whip of blood going, and they were working furiously, catching the bolts in midflight and slapping them aside into the dirt.

They could not catch all of them. Every time one got through, it slammed down among the soldiers and men and horses went flying.

Bal’s road had brought him in behind the Tempus party. From the hilltop the two mages were plain to see, their backs to him, bolt after bolt leaving their hands, steady and unhurried.

He slid down off Barda, blown and head-down, and left him to graze. Then he took to the trees on foot.

He went as quietly as he could, crouched, from trunk to trunk. He kept waiting for a sentry. There wasn’t one. With the temple in front of them, every man they had was down on the field.

The trees thinned. Bal left them still crouched, and found himself a few yards from a raised wooden platform, as high as a tall man’s shoulder, with the two storm mages standing on top of it.

From up there, the pair of them were killing his soldiers one bolt at a time.

He watched the field in front of them one more breath. Then he called the blood.

His left palm opened on its own, and the blood slid out and drew itself in, short and straight and wickedly pointed, and set hard in his fist. The right split in the same breath. From that one the blood poured long and loose and lay along the ground beside him, waiting.

He jumped onto the platform.

Both men spun around. The nearer of the two already had his hand out to throw. Bal drove the dagger at his face and put it into his eye, the right one.

The man screamed. He had jerked back at the very last instant, and the blade had gone in only a finger’s depth. He clutched at his face, and Bal planted a boot in his middle and kicked him clean off the platform. He landed somewhere below with a thud.

Bal released the dagger.

That left the other one.

The second storm mage was young. Not much past Bal’s age, with hair so light a blond it was nearly white. He did not run. He smiled at Bal, a grim little smile with no fun in it.

He held a whip. It was made of lightning. Pure crackling light, as long as Bal’s own and alive along every inch, spitting and humming to itself.

Nothing like it had ever come at Bal before. He kept his distance.

“You’re too late,” the mage said. “I’ve already sent word to my commander. Every man we’ve got will be here soon.”

He flicked the lash.

It went past Bal’s ear tearing the air, and the air behind it stank of ozone, sharp, the smell after a storm.

Bal told his own lash what he wanted, and it did as it was told. Its edge thinned until it would cut. All along its length, heavy spikes pushed up through the blood and hardened there, as red and as sharp as the dagger had been.

He swung it.

It met the lightning in the air between them with a crack and a hiss. They swung, and swung again. Blood on lightning, lightning on blood, meeting every time and sliding off and coming around for more. Neither of them gave an inch. They circled each other on the boards, feet sliding, looking for a way in.

The mage found one first.

His off hand came up, and a bolt leaped out of it at Bal’s face. Bal twisted aside and the bolt went by so close it lifted his hair. He was still off balance when the lightning whip came down across his chest.

It threw him. He sailed off the platform after the first mage, and hit the ground flat on his back.

* * *

For a while, everything was white and empty.

The smell came next. Burnt skin. His own. He could not tell yet whether it hurt. His body had gone somewhere else.

His blood came crawling back to him over the dirt, and slid home.

Up on the platform, the storm mage laughed. Then he jumped down, and Bal heard his boots land, and then walk. Unhurried. They came around and stopped by his head.

He had to move. He had to. His arms lay where they had fallen. His legs would not answer.

“I think I’ll get a promotion after killing you,” the mage said, in an easy, friendly sort of voice, as if they were waiting together for a coach. The lightning whip swung lazily to and fro above Bal’s face, humming. “It’s too bad I have to end it now. I can’t chance you getting away.”

He raised it high. Up there against the sky it changed, the white of it going to a deep blue, with an orange core burning through, like the inside of a flame.

His wrist flicked.

Bal’s eyes closed.

Lora was somewhere north of here, and Viridia. *I’m sorry,* he told them, without any voice. *Forgive me. I couldn’t protect you.*

He lay there and waited for it.

Nothing came.

After a moment, he opened one eye.

The storm mage was still standing over him. An arrow stood out of his neck, feathered and quivering, and he was scrabbling at it, trying to pull it free. The whip had gone. His face found Bal’s, and what was in it now was fear. He knew. He knew exactly.

A bolt from a crossbow went through the side of his head.

He dropped. He fell sideways into the grass and lay still.

Bal managed to roll his head toward the canyon. It was all of him that would move. Across the field, blood soldiers were coming, running toward him, a lot of them, shouting something he could not make out.

The dark took him.


CHAPTER TWENTY-EIGHT

The Chalice

“Water.”

He croaked it. His tongue was glued in place, and the word had to tear itself loose.

He was lying in the High Priest’s bed. He knew it first by the curtains, deep red with gold worked through them, tied back on their posts, and then by the soft weight of a quilt that was too good for anybody who had come in off a battlefield. Amelia sat cross-legged on the covers beside him, near enough for her knee to touch his arm. Her face was pinched with worry. It cleared the instant he spoke.

She was up and gone from the covers before he got the word out twice. A pitcher stood on the little bedside table, and she filled a cup from it, slopping some on the wood, and was back beside him on her knees with the cup at his mouth before the spill had finished spreading. Her other palm cradled his skull to keep it off the pillow. Bal drank. The water was warm from sitting and he drank it all anyway, and some ran down his chin into the pillow.

He let his head go back down and shut his eyes.

“How long was I out?”

“Two days,” Amelia said softly.

Two days.

Two more days of Lora and Viridia in the hands of people who wanted him dead. Two more days of them waking up somewhere strange to him, and going to sleep there. He lay and let that settle on him. Lying there made it no easier.

“How much do you know?” He forced his head around on the pillow to face her.

Amelia hesitated.

“Some,” she said. “You’ve been talking in your sleep.”

Whatever he’d said in his sleep, he decided to leave it there. He told her the rest, all of it, from the leaf in Viridia’s hair to the bolt out of a clear sky, the craters, the empty grass, Jazen and Barda, the mage with the whip made of lightning. His voice gave out twice and she waited both times. Partway through, his hand ended up between both of hers. When he finished, she sat studying their hands on top of the quilt.

“Should I find them,” she said, “and bring them home?”

It was not an idle question. Bal knew that much. If he said yes, she would go. She would go right now, this minute, and find them wherever they were, and bring them back, and she would break the rule against interfering to do it. Then the other originators would have every reason they needed. That was what this whole war was about to begin with: Amelia and the rest of them, and the rules, and who got to break them.

He refused to be the reason for more of it.

He would have to deal with this himself. Somehow.

“No,” he said. “I’ll figure something out.”

He saw what it cost her to hear that. Her thumb stopped moving on his knuckles. She could see what it cost him to say it, too. She did not argue. She let his answer stand.

The quiet went on, and he let it.

Amelia could have had them home before supper. She could have managed it in less time than it had taken him to drink the water. He had told her no, and if she asked again tomorrow he would tell her no again, and every part of him that was not busy with the war wanted the word back. He turned his face to the curtains so she would not have to watch him want it.

* * *

Someone knocked on the door, hard.

Bal jumped, and everything from his neck to his navel objected.

“Come in!”

The door opened slowly, a crack and then wider, and Xavier put his head around it. He looked worried. He kept hold of the door, as if he might yet back out.

“What is it, Xavier?”

Bal dreaded the answer before it came. Whatever could put that face on Xavier, it would not be anything good.

“Uh. Lady Amelia. High Priest.” Xavier coughed into his fist. “I’m afraid we’ve got news.”

Bal waved them in.

They came in together and halted by the bedpost. Neither man looked to have slept in the two days Bal had been out. Their scarlet robes were filthy, mud to the knee and something darker splashed higher up, and their white sashes had gone gray. Under Xavier’s clear blue eyes the skin was smudged nearly black. Thad’s ponytail had come half loose and he had not bothered to fix it. In the hush after Xavier’s announcement, Thad’s stomach growled, long and loud. He ignored it with great dignity. Xavier ignored it too, harder.

The two of them seemed to be deciding between them who was going to say it. Xavier left it to him. Thad sighed and laced his fingers together in front of his robe.

“High Priest Toma’s army is approaching from the north,” he said. “They’ll be here in about two hours. We’ve also had a messenger from Toma. Under a flag of parley.”

From inside his robe he drew a roll of parchment.

“The letter wasn’t sealed,” he said, and handed it over.

Bal accepted it. The parchment was worn fuzzy along its edges; somebody had rolled and unrolled it more than once on its way up to him. He was afraid he knew already what it said. He unrolled it slowly anyway.

The hand was neat and upright and very sure of itself. Then he read it.

The High Priest Toma had Lora and Viridia. His army would sit on their doorstep until every last soul in the temple was dead, unless Bal agreed to a Mages Duel. One on one. If he accepted, the girls would come to no harm. To accept, he was to hang a large blue cloth from a pole and wave it once the army had arrived. Then, at the very bottom, in the same tidy hand, came a postscript. He could even have his *pet originator* teleport him, if he liked, so that they could settle the matter quickly. He was to meet the High Priest in two days’ time.

Bal groaned. His course was plain. Whether he had the strength to do it was not.

“Hang the blue cloth,” he told Xavier. “I accept.”

Xavier went to see to it himself, and Thad went with him. They slipped out to the corridor and eased the door to, so gently that the latch hardly clicked.

Bal’s head dropped back onto the pillow. The letter slid off his fingers onto the quilt and lay there, half rolled up again.

Amelia smoothed the hair back off his forehead and stayed close, and neither of them spoke.

“So I have two days,” Bal said, without opening his eyes. “Two days to figure out how to beat Toma and get Lora and Viridia back.”

He got himself upright, and not quietly. The burn across his front pulled tight, and the breath left him in a hiss. He swung his legs out and got his feet on the floor and stood.

The room tipped. His knees went loose.

Amelia reached him mid-sway, got an arm under his, and steered him back to the bedding.

“You can’t fight him like this,” Amelia said.

“What else can I do?” Even saying it hurt.

He tried again, steadier, and this time the room stayed put.

“You can use me,” Amelia said, with a huff, as if it ought to have been obvious.

“Use you?” Surely he had misheard.

Amelia appealed to the ceiling for patience.

“You’ve been researching blood powers,” she said. “Even ones nobody else has ever known about. But there’s one not even you have figured out.” She considered that. “To be fair, nobody else ever did either. But still.”

Bal lowered himself onto the bed again, carefully, and faced her. For now, the pain in his chest could wait.

“What is it?”

“Drinking blood will heal you,” Amelia said simply.

Now that she had said it, it fit. Amelia was a vampire. Vampires healed themselves by drinking blood. She had made the Blood sygl, so in a way it made perfect sense that her own would heal whoever had it. And most people were never going to drink blood if they could help it, so it also made sense that nobody had found that out. He had done it once, of course, on a stone bench in her temple, and walked out with a hand. He had never thought of it as healing since.

There was a gleam in Amelia’s eye. He knew that gleam.

“And does it do anything besides heal me?”

Amelia smiled, pleased with him. That had been the right question.

“It also makes your blood magyk stronger,” she said. “It might give you other blood magyk abilities too.”

Bal mulled that.

“What happens if I drink the blood of somebody with a different sygl?”

“That’s too unpredictable to tell.” She held up a finger. “And this only works for my High Priest. That’s you. Not for regular blood sygls. A regular priest gets the healing, though. Nothing else.”

Bal smiled at her.

“Then would you please let me drink some of your blood?”

“Of course, Master.”

She slipped from the room, and he heard the latch fall.

* * *

Bal waited, his feet on the cold floor.

He could hear her moving somewhere in the next rooms. A drawer. A door. Then nothing for a while. Down in the canyon, very faint, somebody was shouting orders, and somebody else was shouting back that he’d heard, thanks. Two hours. The army would be closer than that by now. Out there men were getting ready to die because of a letter on a quilt, and the only person who could stop any of it was sitting on the end of a bed, waiting for a cup.

The letter lay on the quilt where it had fallen, curled up on itself. The postscript showed at the open end. *His pet originator.* He nudged it with his knuckles until it rolled behind the pillow and was gone.

In a little while Amelia returned.

She carried a chalice before her, wide and plain, with a silver foot and a cup of gold, and it was filled to the brim. He did not have to ask with what. It was dark red, nearly black at the center, and so full that the surface stood up a little above the rim and trembled as she walked. She did not spill one drop. She rested it on the bedside table, next to the pitcher.

Then she knelt on the floor in front of him.

She did it just as she had done in the temple behind Saint Andre’s, on the day of the binding. Bal remembered that afternoon in a rush, the braziers and the stone under her knees. It had been barely two months. It felt longer.

“Drinking this will bind us together even more strongly,” Amelia said. Her eyes held his. “Do not speak of what you see when you drink. At least, not now.”

What she meant was beyond him. He took her word for it without a question.

Amelia lifted the chalice again, two-handed, and offered it up to him.

He did as she had done and took it with both of his. It was heavier than it looked, and the gold was warm where her fingers had been. Over the rim, Amelia waited. He lifted it to his mouth and drank.

The first drop touched his tongue, and the room was gone.

Memories. They came at him in a flood, too fast, too many, each crowding the last, and it was some minutes before he understood that none of them were his. They were Amelia’s. He was not seeing them through her eyes, either. He was outside them somehow, like somebody watching a movie of somebody else’s life.

He watched.

They were private. That much was plain right away, and he understood then why she had warned him not to speak of them. It touched him deeply that she would let him see them at all.

Then, as they went on flashing past, he began to understand something more. He began to see why she was letting him.

He drank until the cup was empty.

When he lowered it, the bedroom was back. The curtains, the quilt, the pitcher, and Amelia on her knees in front of him, right there still.

“Did you get what you needed?” she asked.

Bal returned the chalice to the table. Then he reached down and drew her up into his arms and held her.

“Thank you,” he whispered against her ear.

He felt her sigh against him, held, it seemed, since she had left the room.

He released her and stood.

He was healed. He knew it before he checked. Nothing pulled. The floor held still. He breathed to the bottom of his lungs. Across his chest, where the whip of lightning had laid him open, the skin had closed over smooth. There was no scar. Not even a mark.

Bal grinned.

Time to go get Lora and Viridia back.

* * *

Over the two days that followed, he trained almost without stopping.

He never had to rest. Amelia’s blood kept him going, through the days and through both nights, and he did not miss sleep. They fought it out on her temple roof, high above the canyon, under the sun and later under the stars, on flat stone gone warm in the day and cold at night. Amelia held herself back as far as she could, so that he could practice against her.

He had ideas to try. New ones. Some of them came from things he had seen in her memories, and the best person in the world to try them on was Amelia herself, because nothing he did could hurt her. He tried them, and they failed, and he tried them again, and she told him exactly how badly each one had gone. She made none of it sound better than it was. He’d have hated it if she had. Sometime in the first night, one idea nearly worked. She called across the roof for him to do it again, and it fell apart on him, and they kept at it till it held.

Once, with the sun straight overhead, his whip got through her guard. The edge opened her forearm a hand’s length above the wrist, and for one breath his blood lay in the cut with hers.

It pulled.

His blood leaned into hers. It wanted in. Every inch of the whip wanted to close around what it touched and keep it, and the wanting ran back up the lash to his hand before he had decided a thing.

He tore the whip loose and called it home. His palm shut on it.

Amelia’s arm had closed already. She shook it out and told him she had seen that swing coming a mile off.

He didn’t ask her about the rest. He couldn’t have shaped the question yet. He went at her again, and for what was left of that day he kept the edge of the whip off her skin.

On the second evening the stone up there was scuffed in long pale streaks where his bare heels had skidded.

Then the morning of the duel came, and he was ready to face Toma.

He was not fooling himself. There was still a good chance he would die today. But there was no other road open to him, and at least now he had a fighting chance, which he’d lacked entirely in that bed.

The bandolier crossed his chest from shoulder to hip. In each of its little loops sat a small glass vial filled with his own blood, stoppered tight. He pressed the stoppers in turn with his thumb, end to end. All secure.

“Ready,” he told Amelia.

He had chosen not to wear a shirt. He had on a plain pair of brown pants and nothing on his feet. The stone of the roof was cool under his soles, and gritty, and the wind rising off the canyon went straight across his bare skin.

Amelia crossed the roof to him. She was barefoot too, and wearing her black dress, the one he loved so much, its long hem stirring in the wind.

Her hand slid into his.

Her fingers fit between his, cool and sure. The bond hummed in it, stronger than it had ever been, and her blood moved somewhere in his own. The canyon, the roof, the army’s noise below them: in a moment all of that would be gone. Somewhere far from here, Toma had been waiting two days for him too.

He waited for her to take him there.


CHAPTER TWENTY-NINE

Blood Elemental

The roof vanished from under his feet, and sand was there instead.

It was hot on top and cool a finger’s depth down, coarse as salt, and Bal’s toes sank into it. Light poured over everything. All around him, tier on tier, stone benches climbed from the sand in rows, hundreds of rows, up to a ring of high arches where the sky began. It was a coliseum, as grand as the old Roman one had ever been, and every seat in it was empty. A breeze spilled down the bowl from the rim and stirred the sand around his ankles.

Toma waited at the center of it.

Magnificent was the only word for him. His robes were blue, a deep, rich blue that fell clear to the sand, and lightning bolts were sewn all over them in silver thread, so that whenever he shifted they caught the sun and jumped. Fifty-six, Fern’s file had said. He wore his years proudly.

Behind him, a wide circle had been drawn on the arena floor and ringed with runes. Inside it stood Lora and Viridia.

Bal’s feet were moving on their own. Two steps. Three.

Toma held up a hand, and Bal halted.

“They are fine,” Toma said. “They have not been harmed.”

Bal searched the girls over, every inch of them in view. Viridia was hammering on nothing and shouting. The cords stood out in her neck with the shouting. Not a sound of it reached him. Lora was beside her, both palms on the air at the circle’s edge, and her lips were moving, slow and careful, shaping each word for him. Where the circle ended, the light bent a little, with a faint blue shimmer to it.

“They cannot hear you,” Toma said. “And their magyk will not affect anything outside the circle.”

Amelia was at Bal’s shoulder. “It’s a lesser version of what they did to me,” she whispered.

Bal’s teeth clamped together until his jaw ached. Thirty paces of sand, that was all. He could have walked it in the time it took to breathe twice, and they might as well have still been in Pashar.

Toma smiled at whatever he saw in Bal’s face.

“Yes,” he said. “Plainly you care for them.”

He let that stand. His voice, when it came again, had dropped, and some of the polish had worn off it.

“As I cared for my fellow priests.” He stopped. “But at least yours are still alive.”

Bal had braced for a speech and gotten something else. Three of Raizen’s priests had taken their seats at a lunch in Londra, and a day later none of them rose again, and this man had known them. Maybe for years. Bal felt no regret over those three. But the grief in Toma’s voice was real, and Bal didn’t pretend to himself otherwise.

Toma drew himself up, and the priest returned to cover the man.

“I assure you,” he said, “if I win, I will take great pleasure in taking their lives. Now. As mage duels go, among high priests, there can be no interference. No familiars may be called. This fight will decide the fate of the war.”

“If we’re having this fight to decide the war,” Bal said, “then killing Lora and Viridia after I’m dead would be an act of war. With no priests left to act as intermediary, Amelia would be free to do whatever she wanted.” He did not give Toma room to answer. “And even if you left one of them alive, the survivor could ask their originator to intercede on their behalf.”

Toma’s mouth soured.

“Agreed,” he said, short and quick.

Toma hadn’t expected Bal to know the rules. Bal had Amelia to thank for it. Deep in the second night on her roof, both of them sweating, she had made him sit and walked him through each rule till he had them by heart. But she had stood at his shoulder through that whole exchange with her mouth shut, because she had to. She could teach him the rules. She could not argue them for him. The argument itself he had made alone, and Toma had conceded it. So whatever happened now, both girls would live. For Bal, that was enough.

With the terms agreed, Toma crossed to one end of the arena, and Bal walked toward the other. Amelia came with him partway.

“Good luck,” she said, with a quick smile.

She bounded to the stands in a single leap, her long skirt flaring out around her, and landed on a bench six rows up, soundlessly. She tucked her bare feet beneath her skirt, ready for the show.

Bal walked on by himself.

The sand got hotter toward his end of the arena, where the sun had been lying on it longest, and each grain worked in between his toes. Under the stands at this end there was a low dark archway with an iron gate across it, rusted shut, and it breathed out a smell of old stone and something animal, long gone.

He stopped and faced around.

From here Toma was small, a blue mark at the other end with the sun glinting off his sleeves. The circle was off to Bal’s left, between him and Toma and a little off the line, and he could just make out Viridia’s hair, standing out from her head in a pale blue cloud.

Amelia stayed where she was, high in the stands.

* * *

This was Bal’s first mage duel.

Before Amelia’s blood, everything he knew about them came from books he had read since he started at school. Some were histories and some were manuals. They disagreed about almost everything. On one point every single book agreed.

Always make the first move.

“Kona Niente!” Bal yelled.

The sand at his feet heaved up and flew at Toma in a great boiling cloud of dirt. Under cover of it his fingers were already at the bandolier. He worked two vials loose and flung them wide, hard and low, so they would land to the left and right of where Toma stood.

From the middle of the cloud came Toma’s voice, perfectly calm.

“Czak.”

The cloud fell. All of it, at once, every grain, and it pattered down in a ring around Toma’s feet, where it settled. There was not a speck on his robes.

Toma raised an eyebrow.

“Is that it?” he asked. “That’s all you’ve got?” He brushed at one sleeve, though not a grain clung to it. “Well, no matter. It’s my turn.”

Two days ago, he’d have seen a man standing in the sun. Now, with Amelia’s blood in him, he saw it come. It came in from everywhere, from the air above the bowl and down out of the sky, thin bright threads of something he had no name for, and all of it drew in toward Toma and bunched around his hands until the light there was too thick to see through.

Toma threw back his head and cried out. It was a loud, raw, primal sound, and it seemed to tear itself out of his throat on its own.

The bolt struck the sand between them with Bal still flat-footed.

The blast picked him up and threw him. He hit the sand, and rolled, and hit again, and slid. There was sand in his mouth and sand in his ears and a ringing over everything.

He got his head up.

The storm elemental filled his whole sight.

It stood where the bolt had struck, taller than three men, a crackling mass of white and blue that was shaped something like a man and something like a thunderhead and would not hold either shape for long. Light ran over it and through it without stopping. The fine hairs along his forearms rose and prickled. He pushed himself to his feet, and once he was there he could not do anything but stare.

This was no mere elemental.

*No inheritance gift,* Fern had read out of the file. The file had been wrong.

Toma was enjoying this, and made no secret of it.

“I see you are beginning to understand the gravity of your situation,” he said. “The rules clearly state no familiars. But my inheritance gift is calling a life-bonded elemental, so it is not, technically, a familiar. Which is enough to satisfy the rules.” His smile went ugly at the edges. “Now. It’s time for Akora to attack.”

The elemental surged forward.

Bal scrambled backward and yelled a quick incantation, and a short wall of sand rose from the arena floor between them. He only needed time. A little distance.

Akora hit the wall and went straight through it.

Where the lightning touched the sand, the sand turned to glass, and the glass burst. Shards blew out in every direction, spinning and glittering. Bal twisted away and felt them go into him, a hot sting across his back and both arms, then a dozen more.

Somewhere behind the elemental, Toma was laughing.

Bal ran. Akora threw lightning at him, bolt on bolt, and he flung himself clear, dropped, rolled, came up running. The sand slid under his bare feet. Flickering whips of electric light lashed out at him from the thing’s body, cracking past on his right, and he dodged left to get clear of them.

His foot came down wrong. The sand gave.

He crashed to the ground.

He was still scrabbling for his knees when the hulking storm elemental loomed over him.

Bal lay under it, and he knew. He was about to die.

He found the circle. The girls were together at the edge of it, and they had the same fear on both their faces.

He mouthed it so they could see. *I’m sorry.*

* * *

Akora began to turn.

It spun slowly, then faster, light whirling around a hollow at its middle, and the pull of it took hold of Bal and dragged him over the sand and off it. He dug in his heels. He grabbed at the ground. The force of it was too strong to fight, and he had to let it take him.

There was blood on his back. The glass had seen to that. He called it, and it lifted off his skin and lashed out in whips of red, and he struck at the elemental with them, lash after lash. It was like whipping a river. The blood passed clean through the light, and the light did not care.

Then he was inside.

The maelstrom closed over him. He was in the heart of the elemental’s twisting body, borne around and around in a roar of light, and the glass was in there with him. All the shards from the wall had been caught up in the spin, thousands of them, and they whirled with him and through him and past him and back. Each one cut. They cut his arms and his legs and his back and his face, and they kept on cutting, shredding him. There was no taking this. Bal could only be in it.

And through it, with Amelia’s blood in his eyes, he saw the core.

It hung in the center of everything, a knot of light harder and brighter than anything around it, turning very slowly while all the storm went racing around it.

His strength was going, draining out of him in red. The blood was everywhere around him, flung off him by the spin, a fine red mist thinning in the light.

He called it back. All of it.

He pulled it home to him out of the storm, every drop that had leaked out of every cut, and he wrapped it around the core, and he pressed.

The core pulled at it.

He knew that pull. He had felt it on Amelia’s roof, in the cut on her arm, and he had torn his blood free of it without knowing what it was. He understood it no better here, and he was past saving anything. He let it pull, and he went with it.

He pushed it in. He forced it into the creature with all the strength he had, every drop he could spare. When he could spare no more, he called on the two vials he had thrown at the start, and their blood rose from the sand and crossed the arena to him, and he forced that in too.

It lasted past all counting.

A crack split the arena, a huge sound, bouncing off every tier of stone at once. The elemental shuddered all through.

It dropped him.

He fell. He struck the sand on his side and stayed down, and somewhere far away somebody was screaming, a high, terrible, endless scream. To Bal it seemed to come from another country.

Something landed on the sand before him, softly.

Slowly, he lifted his eyes. Where Akora had been, a shape stood over him, as tall as before, still turning, though no light was left in it. No lightning anywhere. It was blood. A great slow column of swirling blood, deep red, folding endlessly on itself without a drop spilling onto the sand, and it waited before him, as if summoned.

A blood elemental. Bal had no other name for it.

There was also no such thing. They did not exist. They never had.

Bal blinked up at it. He didn’t understand any of it.

The scream finally got through to him. It was Toma.

Bal rolled onto his side and tried to stand, and his arms folded under him. He tried again. After a few more tries he got as far as his knees, and then, swaying, he was up, with the arena tipping under him.

Toma was down on the sand, rocking, his head clutched in his hands.

Bal made his way over. It was slow going. The thing of blood came along close at his back, and he could hear it moving, a low wet rushing like a creek running under ice.

By then, Toma’s screams had worn down into crying, little whimpering sounds of pain. The old man lifted his face. Tears were running down it, and the tears were red.

“What have you done to me?” Toma moaned.

Bal was at a loss. What *had* he done? He checked behind himself. The blood creature hung there, turning. He faced Toma again, and Toma rocked.

* * *

Amelia flashed into being beside him, her bare feet on the sand at his elbow, and a heartbeat behind her came Raizen, with Aren after him.

Raizen was furious. He flung out an arm, the orange sleeve of his monk’s clothing swinging, and leveled a finger straight at Bal.

“Do you see?” he demanded of Aren. “This is exactly why we locked Amelia away! Her followers are too dangerous!”

Aren’s long white beard stirred in the breeze, and Toma’s huddled shape put a deep trouble in his old face.

Toma lowered his hands from his face.

Then Bal understood in a rush why the originators had come. On Toma’s palm was a sygl, and it was the Blood.

When Bal converted Akora, he had converted the High Priest as well. Elemental and priest were life-bonded. Whatever entered the one entered the other.

The idea knocked the air out of Bal. It reached Aren and Raizen too. It was there in their faces, the same thought in both. If he could convert a High Priest, then no one was safe if Bal chose to convert them.

Amelia wasn’t troubled in the least. On the contrary, she was enjoying herself enormously. And why not? Technically, the war was over. Raizen did not have a High Priest anymore.

“Raizen has a point,” Aren said at last. “Your last High Priest converting people was the source of our conflict. Now this one appears able to convert the unwilling. Can you not understand why we would find that troubling?”

“I understand why it would trouble you, yes. And yet you want me to play your games, and then you get upset when they don’t go your way. Bal never meant to convert Raizen’s High Priest. He acted on instinct, against Toma’s elemental. It was only because Toma and his elemental shared a life bond that he converted the High Priest along with it. You cannot penalize my High Priest for protecting himself.”

The gods could argue without him. He was having enough trouble staying upright. The breeze off the rim found every cut on him and stung it fresh, and blood ran down the backs of his legs in slow threads and dripped off his heels into the sand, where it sank and left little dark coins. Behind him the swirling thing kept up its low wet rushing, patient as a cart horse.

A fly had found Toma. It walked across the old man’s knuckles, and Toma, who had stopped rocking, made no move to brush it off.

Aren weighed her words. Then he put his question to Bal.

“Now that you are aware of your ability, what are your intentions?” Aren asked.

Bal found the circle again. Both girls were still inside it, pressed against the shimmering blue barrier, as close to him as it would let them come.

“I just want to live my life with my girls,” he said. “I’ll protect them with everything I have. If that means removing a piece from your armies, then I will. But it isn’t something I’m setting out to do.”

“So you do not plan to use your newfound ability?”

“Not if I don’t have to. If somebody comes asking me to convert them, I can compromise. I’ll have Amelia let you know before I do it. That’s as far as I’ll go.”

Amelia agreed at once, and Aren accepted it.

“That’s it?” Raizen burst out. “She just gets away with taking my High Priest?”

Aren sighed and faced him.

“Under the terms that were set, she has done nothing wrong,” he said. “She has not interfered, and the war was fought by humans alone. I declare Amelia the winner.”

Raizen shook. Bal had taken that for a figure of speech until now. The god shook all over with the force of his rage, from head to foot.

Aren’s fingers cracked together, one dry click.

The girls vanished, and the circle was empty sand. Toma was gone with them.

Bal lunged forward. Amelia stopped him.

“He only sent them home to the school,” she whispered. “They’re okay.”

He let her hold him back.

Aren frowned at him from under white brows. From his face, he was not used to mortals forgetting themselves in his presence.

“Now then.” Aren smoothed his beard and turned to Amelia. “As the victor, it is your right to demand anything of the defeated, barring his life. Have you made your decision?”

Amelia’s mouth curved, slowly.

“Oh, yes,” she said.

Raizen heard what was in her voice. So did Bal, the cruel excitement just under the words. The rage drained from Raizen’s face, and fear came in to take its place.

“You will confine yourself in the same temple I was locked away in,” Amelia said. “There you will remain, for three thousand years, with no contact with the outside world. You will serve as a warning to anyone else who desires to come after me or mine.”

She said the last part straight at Aren.

The two of them held each other’s eyes.

“Very well,” Aren said.

His fingers snapped again, and Raizen’s protest went with him before it was a word.

Aren lifted his face to the sky.

“You know the others will not like this,” Aren said, “but I assume that’s one reason you did it. I dare say it is not something they will forget. At least they will think twice about engaging you again. I must return.”

To Bal he sounded mild and old and very tired.

“Try not to start another war,” he said.

He was gone, and bare sand lay where his sandals had been.

* * *

Bal remained in the sun, the swirling thing turning at his back, and the tension left him by degrees. His shoulders first. Then his hands.

Amelia was shining, up on her bare toes in the sand, and seeing it, Bal knew. It was over. It was really over.

She rushed him. She caught him up in a hug that crushed his bones together, and each of the thousand cuts on him should have screamed at that, and none of them bothered him. He buried his face in her hair and breathed her in.

At last she stepped back.

“I’m so proud of you! I knew you could do it.”

“At least somebody did,” Bal said. “I’m still not sure how I won.”

He had not forgotten the thing behind him. He faced it.

“This is the part I don’t understand,” he said, and pointed at it.

Amelia circled the column of swirling blood, slowly, studying it. It turned for her without spilling.

“Honestly, I don’t either,” she said. “I’ve never heard of anything like this happening. Plus the fact that you broke the bond between it and the High Priest.”

She snapped her fingers at it, a bright, decided little crack, and the blood elemental was gone.

Bal didn’t have to ask.

“I sent it to my domain,” she said. “I’ll study it when I get the chance.”

Then she stepped forward and caught his hand, and the coliseum went away around them, all the empty seats and the hot sand and the scuffed place where Toma had knelt.

The warmth reached his bare skin first, and the smell of woodsmoke, ahead of any sight at all. Somebody at home already had the fire roaring.


CHAPTER THIRTY

The First Day

They landed on the rug in front of the big red couch, so close that Bal’s shins knocked the cushions.

The girls had been sitting there, waiting. They were up before he had his balance. Viridia vaulted the table instead of going around it. Lora went around like a sensible person and arrived at the same instant anyway, and the two of them hit him and Amelia together. The four of them went into one tangle of arms. An elbow found his ribs. Somebody’s knee knocked his. Lora’s glasses got mashed into his collarbone.

Bal hung on to all of them. Here were the people he cared about most in the world, as much of them as two arms would reach, and he wasn’t letting go for anything. His cuts stung under the squeeze, and he let them. Amelia’s arm was around his back and Lora’s was around his waist, and Viridia had one fist knotted in the strap of his bandolier, as though he might be teleported off again if she loosened her grip.

Amelia was laughing into Viridia’s hair. Viridia was saying something muffled against his neck, and he never caught a word of it, and didn’t need to.

He let go first, though it took some doing. He took Lora by one shoulder and Viridia by the other and held them off at arm’s length. He checked them from their hair down to their boots, hunting for anything wrong.

“We’re okay,” Viridia said, because his face had given him away. “Bal. We’re okay.”

He kept looking anyway.

They told him everything. At first they both talked at once and got tangled up, until Lora gave in and handed Viridia the beginning.

“It was that sky,” Viridia said. “Not one cloud in it. And then that crack, like somebody slapped the whole world.” A loose thread hung off her cuff, and she wound it around her finger. “Only a storm mage can make it thunder in fair weather.”

“The horses ran,” Lora said. “Except mine.”

“So I tried to pull the bolts down into me.” Viridia scowled at the rug. “If they were hitting me, they weren’t hitting you two. A few bent my way. Not enough.”

He remembered her like that, arms up, hair streaming. For days it had been the last of her he had.

“Then that rock caught you in the head,” Viridia said. “You went down and you didn’t get up. That was the worst part.”

“You were face down in the dirt,” Lora said. “I tried to see whether you were breathing. I was too far away to tell.”

“And then we were off our feet. Carried off, with you lying there.” Viridia snapped the thread off. “I couldn’t even get my head around far enough to keep you in sight.”

Lora took over for the days after.

“No one hurt us,” she said. “They just kept us.”

“Toma’s people,” Viridia said, and wrinkled her nose.

“We didn’t know if you were alive.” Lora kept her voice level, and it cost her. “We went over it and over it, and there was never any end to reach.”

“I reached one,” Viridia said. “You were alive. I said so. She wanted evidence.”

“I did want evidence,” Lora said. “I’ll accept you standing here.”

Then came the circle. Lora set it out as carefully as she’d write up a potion. Runes around the whole ring. Air at the edge that gave a little under your palm, then gave no more. Not a word went through it and not a spark of their magic, though she had tried, and she listed what she’d tried, and Bal followed about half.

“I talked slowly,” she said. “In case you could read lips.”

“I saw you,” he said. “I couldn’t make out any of it.”
