“I hit it,” Viridia said. “A lot.”

“Neither worked.”

“Mine felt better.”

“She’s making it sound tidy,” Viridia told him. “It wasn’t tidy. We had to stand there and watch the whole thing. The glass. That big spinning monster. You in the middle of it, and us shut in a bubble, no use to anybody.”

Lora let her, and carried on, quieter.

Bal heard every word.

He wanted to ask whether Viridia had given anybody lip. He’d ask later. He had a fair idea.

“We knew you’d come,” Lora said, once there was no more to tell. She said it simply, as a fact she had checked.

Down in the dirt below the platform, with the storm mage’s whip humming over his face, he had told them he was sorry. He had asked them to forgive him. And all that while, somewhere Bal couldn’t even picture, these two had been sure of him, surer than he’d been of himself on any single mile of that road.

There was no answer to that in him. He stood there in his torn pants and couldn’t get over them.

Amelia had stepped aside to let them have him. Now she rejoined them, and the talk turned to the fight. Viridia demanded it blow by blow, and then told them what she’d been shouting at the time. Some of it Amelia asked her to repeat, slower. Lora asked about the elemental. Bal admitted the elemental was a mystery to him, and Amelia confessed it was one to her too, which nobody had expected to hear from her.

Then Amelia described what she had done to Raizen. Three thousand years, shut up in the very temple where he had helped to lock her away.

Viridia had carried his sygl once. She took that in with her jaw hanging. Then she laughed so hard her legs gave out and dropped her onto the cushions.

“And Toma?” Lora asked, once Viridia could breathe again.

Aren had taken the old man to the Heir’s School. The healers there would look after him until he was well, according to Amelia, and after that he could decide for himself what a man his age did with the Blood’s mark on his hand. Bal found he was glad of it.

* * *

After that they ran out of talk.

They wound up in a row on that couch, which it was never built for. Amelia had the end. Lora came next, then Bal, then Viridia on his right. The other couch stood empty. Nobody suggested it.

Runes homework lay spread over the tabletop, half finished, a pencil rolled into the crease. One page had been kicked to the floor in the hug, likely by Viridia on her way over. Lora’s alchemy book lay face down and open on the far cushions, its back bent wide. Bal’s mind went to the homework and the due dates waiting behind it, and found he liked thinking about due dates. They were the best thing he had thought about in days.

The blaze had sunk to a good steady bed of coals with flames running over them. Bal stared into it. A log gave way at its center, and the two halves slid into the red and sent a small rush of sparks flying. His feet were still bare and gritty with arena sand, and that was fine.

Had the dorm thought to put out any supper? Was he even hungry? He decided he could hold out till breakfast. The plates would vanish afterward anyway, and who washed them remained an open question.

It was quiet a long while.

“What do we do now?” Viridia asked eventually.

She swung around toward Bal, on her left.

“The war’s over. Raizen’s gone. It all feels kind of unreal.” She faced the flames again. “Will they really leave us alone now?”

Bal caught up her hand in his.

“Things are going to be different,” he said. “I won’t pretend they aren’t. And there’s no telling how far what I did will reach, for us, later on. All we can do is live our life the best we can, and stand together.”

Viridia’s head came down on his shoulder, warm and heavy.

“Besides,” Lora said, from his other side, “we’ve still got school. And the Dawn faction.” She pushed up her glasses. “It’s not like we won’t have stuff to do.”

Bal laughed. He touched Lora’s shoulder lightly and smiled at her.

“Fair point. It’s sure been one hell of a break.”

* * *

Bal brought Lora up with him, and then Viridia, who came up grumbling, and then Amelia, who needed no help whatsoever and took his hand anyway. He waved them after him and led them off to the bedroom.

The lamp was low. Somebody had turned the covers back already. Viridia fought a knotted bootlace and lost, and swore at it, and Lora had it undone before Viridia was finished swearing. Amelia took down her hair. Viridia staked out the wall side and dared anyone to argue, and nobody did.

Bal lingered at the doorframe and gazed at those three beautiful women. The fire ticked behind him out by the couch. Lora’s eyes were already closing. Nobody was hunting anybody, and they could all sleep.

For the first time in his life, Bal was exactly where he belonged.
